Transition asymptotics for the real solutions of the sinh-Gordon Painlevé III equation
Kenta Miyahara, Maxim L. Yattselev

Abstract
. We consider solutions of the sinh-Gordon Painlevé III equation u x ​ x + 1 x ​ u x = sinh ⁡ u u_{xx}+\frac{1}{x}u_{x}=\sinh u that are real on ( 0 , ∞ ) (0,\infty) . They are parametrized by the monodromy parameter p ∈ ℂ ¯ p\in\overline{\mathbb{C}} , | p | > 1 |p|>1 , and an additional real parameter s ℝ s^{\mathbb{R}} when p = ∞ p=\infty . Our previous joint work with A. Its described the asymptotic behavior of these solutions as x → ∞ x\to\infty . Here, we describe the transition as x , p → ∞ x,p\to\infty , 2 ​ Im ⁡ ( p ) = − s ℝ 2\operatorname{Im}(p)=-s^{\mathbb{R}} , between singular solutions ( | p | < ∞ |p|<\infty ) and smooth solutions ( p = ∞ p=\infty ). In short, if we parametrize | p | 2 = 1 + e 2 ​ ϰ ​ x |p|^{2}=1+e^{2\varkappa x} , then the smooth exponential asymptotics of the solutions extends to the region ϰ > 1 \varkappa>1 , with a change of the leading order term at ϰ = 2 \varkappa=2 ; at ϰ = 1 \varkappa=1 the exponential behavior transitions into an elliptic asymptotics, which holds for all 0 < ϰ < 1 0<\varkappa<1 ; as ϰ \varkappa decays to zero, elliptic asymptotics degenerates into trigonometric one, which holds for all p p fixed. Keywords : Painlevé III equation; sinh-Gordon equation; isomonodromic deformation; Riemann-Hilbert problem; steepest-descent method



1 Introduction and Main Results

This paper is a continuation of our previous work
[
30
]
, joint with Alexander Its, where we studied the asymptotic behavior of
real
solutions of the sinh-Gordon Painlevé III equation

(1.0.1)

\displaystyle u_{xx}+\frac{1}{x}u_{x}=\sinh u.

Our interest stems from the fact that (
1.0.1
) is the simplest tt*-Toda equation of Cecotti and Vafa
[
13
,
12
,
14
]
whose analysis has been of much recent interest
[
27
,
28
,
24
,
23
,
25
,
26
,
33
]
.

We understand reality in the following sense. It is known that solutions of (
1.0.1
) can have movable logarithmic singularities and that going around any logarithmic singularity (via the complex plane) of a solution of (
1.0.1
) results in the addition of
2\pi\mathrm{i}
to the solution. So, when we speak of real solutions, we ignore all the acquired additions of
2\pi\mathrm{i}
(since (
1.0.1
) does not change when
u(x)
is replaced by
u(x)+2\pi\mathrm{i}n
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, thus obtained functions are still local solutions of (
1.0.1
)).

In
[
30
]
we were interested in the asymptotic behavior of the real solutions of (
1.0.1
) on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}:=(0,\infty)
as
x\to 0^{+}
and
x\to+\infty
. There, we have explained that these solutions can be parametrized by a parameter
p
from the extended complex plane with
|p|>1
and an additional real parameter
s^{\mathbb{R}}
in the case
p=\infty
. In terms of the asymptotic behavior at infinity, we have obtained the following result
[
30
, Theorem 2.1]
1
1

1

We refer the reader to our previous work
[
30
]
where the relation of Theorem
IMY
with the results of the earlier works of Novokshenov
[
35
]
and Kitaev
[
32
]
obtained by the isomonodromic deformation method is discussed in detail.
.

Theorem (Its-Miyahara-Yattselev)
.

To each finite
p
,
|p|>1
, there corresponds one real solution of the sinh-Gordon Painlevé III equation (
1.0.1
) on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and

(1.0.2)

\displaystyle u(x;p)=\ln\left(\frac{1-\sin\left(x-\kappa\ln x+\phi\right)+\mathcal{O}(x^{-1})}{1+\sin\left(x-\kappa\ln x+\phi\right)+\mathcal{O}(x^{-1})}\right),

as
x\to+\infty
, where the error term is uniform in
x
,

(1.0.3)

\kappa:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln(|p|^{2}-1)\quad\text{and}\quad\phi:=-\kappa\ln 4+\arg\left(p\,\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+\mathrm{i}\kappa\right)\right).

To
p=\infty
there corresponds a one-parameter family of real solutions of the sinh-Gordon Painlevé III equation (
1.0.1
) on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

(1.0.4)

\displaystyle u(x;\infty,s^{\mathbb{R}})=\frac{2s^{\mathbb{R}}}{\pi}K_{0}(x)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{e^{-2x}}{\sqrt{x}}\right),

as
x\to+\infty
, where
s^{\mathbb{R}}\in\mathbb{R}
is the parametrizing (Stokes) parameter and
K_{0}(x)
is the modified Bessel function of the second kind.

Formula (
1.0.2
) implies that solutions
u(x;p)
for finite
p
possess logarithmic singularities while solutions
u(x;\infty,s^{\mathbb{R}})
are singularities free for all
x
large enough (entirely smooth on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
when
|s^{\mathbb{R}}|\leq 2
, see
[
23
,
26
]
). In this work, we are interested in the transition asymptotics describing the evolution of singular solutions towards smooth solutions as
x\to\infty
and
p\to\infty
. More precisely, given real parameters
s^{\mathbb{R}}
and
\varkappa>0
, we are interested in
p=p(\varkappa,x)
such that

(1.0.5)

\operatorname{Im}(p(\varkappa,x))=-\tfrac{1}{2}s^{\mathbb{R}}\quad\text{and}\quad|p(\varkappa,x)|^{2}=1+e^{2\varkappa x}

(notice that
\pi\kappa=\varkappa x
in the notation of (
1.0.3
); also, given
|s^{\mathbb{R}}|
, it must holds that
e^{\varkappa x}\geq\frac{1}{2}|s^{\mathbb{R}}|
assuming that
|\operatorname{Re}(p)|\geq 1
). It is not obvious from the statement of the above theorem that the imaginary part of
p
must be kept constant while
|p|
tends to infinity. This choice will be made clear further below in Section
2.1
, see (
2.1.6
), where we explain that another natural way to parametrize real solutions is with the monodromy data (
2.1.3
) of the system (Lax pair) of matrix differential equations (
2.1.1
). That parametrization shows that
\operatorname{Im}(p)
and
|p|
can be viewed as independent parameters. Hence, we keep one of them fixed and send the other to infinity.

Investigations of this type are not new. The study of the gap probabilities in the bulk of the eigenvalue spectrum of Hermitian random matrices drawn from the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble and related questions naturally lead to the Fredholm determinant

D_{\sin}(s,\gamma)=\det\left(\mathcal{I}-\gamma\,\mathcal{K}_{\sin}\right),\quad K_{\sin}(\lambda,\mu)=\frac{\sin(\lambda-\mu)}{\pi(\lambda-\mu)},

where
\mathcal{K}_{\sin}
is an integral operator on
L^{2}(-s,s)
with the kernel
K_{\sin}(\lambda,\mu)
and
\gamma\in[0,1]
is a parameter. By the 1990s, the large
s
asymptotics of
D_{\sin}(s,\gamma)
were fully and rigorously found for each fixed
\gamma
. It was shown that
D_{\sin}(s,\gamma)
decays exponentially for each
\gamma\in[0,1)
, whereas
D_{\sin}(s,1)
admits a super-exponential decay. Dyson conjectured that the transition between these two modes happens when one considers the double scaling regime

\gamma=1-e^{-2\varkappa_{\sin}s},\quad\varkappa_{\sin}>0,

and that the transition asymptotic formulae involve Jacobi theta functions. This was only recently verified by Bothner-Deift-Its-Krasovsky using Riemann-Hilbert analysis of integrable operators in their ongoing seminal work
[
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
]
.

In a similar vein, Bothner
[
10
]
has studied the Fredholm determinant
D_{\mathrm{Ai}}(x,\gamma)
,
\gamma>0
, corresponding to an integral operator on
L^{2}(x,\infty)
with the Airy kernel, specifically, its asymptotics as
x\to-\infty
. This determinant admits an explicit expression

(1.0.6)

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{Ai}}(x,\gamma)=\exp\left[-\int_{x}^{\infty}(y-x)u^{2}(y;\gamma)dy\right],

where
u(x;\gamma)
is the solution of the Painlevé II equation

(1.0.7)

\displaystyle u_{xx}=xu+2u^{3}

such that
\gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}u(x;\gamma)
possesses Airy asymptotics as
x\to+\infty
. As with the large
s
behavior of
D_{\sin}(s,\gamma)
, asymptotics of
u(x;\gamma)
as
x\to-\infty
for each
\gamma
fixed was already known
[
1
,
2
,
29
,
31
]
. The Ablowitz-Segur solutions (
\gamma<1
) and the Hastings-McLeod solution (
\gamma=1
) are smooth near
-\infty
, while Kapaev solutions (
\gamma>1
) are singular oscillatory. Bothner has studied the transition from the Ablowitz-Segur and the Kapaev solutions to the Hastings-McLeod one in the asymptotic regime

\gamma=1\pm e^{-\varkappa_{\mathrm{Ai}}(-x)^{3/2}}\,\quad\varkappa_{\mathrm{Ai}}>0.

Moreover, in
[
9
]
, Bothner has studied the above double scaling limits of
D_{\mathrm{Ai}}(x,\gamma)
as well as of
D_{\mathrm{Bes}}(s,\gamma)
(a Fredholm determinant corresponding to an integral operator with the Bessel kernel) directly, i.e., without going through the Painlevé functions.

Figure 1
.
The type of behavior of
u(x;p(\varkappa,x))
for each choice of
(\varkappa,x)
.

As was the case in all of the above-mentioned works, the precise statements of our result are rather technical, but can be summarized as follows.
We show that the asymptotic formula (
1.0.4
) extends to the region
\varkappa>2
of the
(x,\varkappa x)
-plane (the first part of the exponential region on Figure
1
). At
\varkappa=2
, the exponential nature of the decay remains the same, but the leading order term changes from
K_{0}(x)
to
I_{0}(x)
, the modified Bessel function of the first kind. This behavior persists for all
1<\varkappa<2
(the second part of the exponential region on Figure
1
). These results are described in Theorem
1.1
. At
\varkappa=1
, exponential decay changes into the behavior similar to (
1.0.2
), but with the sine function replaced by a certain Jacobi elliptic function. This transition happens via “logarithmic cascades”, see Theorem
1.2
-
1.3
and the “logarithmic cascade” region of Figure
1
. Elliptic asymptotics holds for each
0<\varkappa<1
, see Theorem
1.4
, which corresponds to the “elliptic” region on Figure
1
. Finally, when
\varkappa
decays to zero, elliptic asymptotics degenerates into (
1.0.2
), see Theorem
1.5
. This is the “trigonometric” region of Figure
1
.

Figure
1
can also be understood as follows. Solution
u(x,p)
for a fixed parameter
p
corresponds to a horizontal line on Figure
1
. Hence, for a very large fixed
p
, we see that such a solution first resembles an exponentially decaying one with no singularities. However, as
x
increases, singularities start to appear, but seemingly not regularly enough to be described by an elliptic or trigonometric function (this corresponds to the horizontal line passing through the logarithmic cascades region). Next, for even larger
x
, the behavior of the solution is described with the help of elliptic functions, and eventually it becomes trigonometric as in (
1.0.2
).

Throughout the paper, we always assume that (
1.0.5
) takes place. For a non-negative quantity
q
, we let
q_{+}:=\max\{1,q\}=\exp\{\ln^{+}q\}
. We further set
\varepsilon:=\sign(\operatorname{Re}(p))
.

Our first theorem describes asymptotics of the real solutions of the sinh-Gordon Painlevé III equation (
1.0.1
) in the exponential region
\varkappa>1
.

Theorem 1.1
.

There exists a large enough constant
D_{1}>0
such that for all

(1.0.8)

\displaystyle\varkappa_{1}x\geq D_{1}+\ln|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+},\quad\varkappa_{1}:=\min\{1,\varkappa-1\},

and all
x\geq 1
, it holds that

(1.0.9)

u(x,p(\varkappa,x))=\frac{2}{\pi}s^{\mathbb{R}}K_{0}(x)-2\varepsilon e^{-\varkappa x}I_{0}(x)+\mathcal{O}\left(|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}x^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-2\varkappa_{1}x}\right),

where
I_{0}(x)
and
K_{0}(x)
are the modified Bessel functions of the first and second kind, respectively, and the constant in the error term
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is absolute. If
s^{\mathbb{R}}=0
,
\varkappa_{1}
can be replaced by
\varkappa-1
in (
1.0.8
) and (
1.0.9
).

Of course, condition (
1.0.8
) simply says that
x\geq D_{1}
when
\varkappa\geq 2
, while it becomes
\varkappa x\geq x+D_{1}
when
\varkappa\in(1,2)
, which is a simultaneous condition on
\varkappa
and
x
.

Remark
.

Recall that
K_{0}(x)
and
I_{0}(x)
possess the following asymptotic expansions:

\frac{1}{\pi}K_{0}(x)\sim\frac{e^{-x}}{\sqrt{2\pi x}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}\frac{a_{n}}{x^{n}},\quad I_{0}(x)\sim\frac{e^{x}}{\sqrt{2\pi x}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{a_{n}}{x^{n}},\quad a_{n}=\frac{((1/2)_{n})^{2}}{2^{n}n!},

where
(\cdot)_{n}
is the Pochhammer symbol (rising factorial), see
[
18
, (10.40.1-2)]
. Hence, for each fixed
\varkappa
and all
x
sufficiently large, it holds that

u(x,p(\varkappa,x))\sim e^{-\varkappa_{1}x}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi x}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{a_{n}}{x^{n}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}(-1)^{n}s^{\mathbb{R}},&\varkappa>2,\\
((-1)^{n}s^{\mathbb{R}}-\varepsilon),&\varkappa=2,\\
(-\varepsilon),&1<\varkappa<2,\end{array}\right.

assuming
s^{\mathbb{R}}\neq 0
, where we have omitted the error terms as they are smaller than the terms in the above expansions.

Theorem
1.1
allows us to take
\varkappa
as close to
1
as we wish at a price of increasing
x
since we always must have
(\varkappa-1)x\geq D_{1}
. Naturally, this condition does not allow us to take
\varkappa=1
. The latter case is addressed by the following theorem.

Theorem 1.2
.

For any non-negative integer
m\geq 0
, there exists a constant
D_{2}(m)>0
such that for all

(1.0.10)

\displaystyle x\geq D_{2}(m)+8\ln|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}\quad\text{and}\quad\varkappa x=x-(m+\nu)\ln x,\quad|\nu|\leq\tfrac{1}{2},

it holds that

(1.0.11)

u(x,p(\varkappa,x))=2\varepsilon\ln\left(\frac{1+\chi_{m}x^{-\nu-\frac{1}{2}}+\chi_{m+1}^{-1}x^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}+\mathcal{O}_{m}(x^{-1})}{1-\chi_{m}x^{-\nu-\frac{1}{2}}-\chi_{m+1}^{-1}x^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}+\mathcal{O}_{m}(x^{-1})}\right),

where
\chi_{m}=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{(-4)^{m}}{(m-1)!}
(it is zero when
m=0
).

In the integrable systems literature, asymptotic formulae of type (
1.0.11
) are known as
logarithmic cascades
since the coefficient next to the leading power of
x
changes each time a curve
x-(m+\frac{1}{2})\ln x
is crossed.

Remark
.

Any half-integer constant in front of
\ln x
can represent as
m+\tfrac{1}{2}=(m+1)-\tfrac{1}{2}
. If we plug these representations into (
1.0.11
), we obtain

2\varepsilon\ln\left(\frac{1+\chi_{m+1}^{-1}+\mathcal{O}_{m}(x^{-1})}{1-\chi_{m+1}^{-1}+\mathcal{O}_{m}(x^{-1})}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad 2\varepsilon\ln\left(\frac{1+\chi_{m+1}+\mathcal{O}_{m}(x^{-1})}{1-\chi_{m+1}+\mathcal{O}_{m}(x^{-1})}\right).

Clearly, both formulae lead to the same asymptotic statement in
x
. Of course, it is important to observe here that the error term does depend on
m
.

Remark
.

Formula (
1.0.11
) points to the appearance of the first (after the period of exponential behavior) singularity of
u(x,p(\varkappa,x))
. The matters are complicated by the dependence on
D_{2}(m)
that restricts the range of applicability of (
1.0.11
) and implicitly appears in the error term. However, ignoring these complications, (
1.0.11
) says that there must be a singularity around

|\chi_{m+1}|^{\frac{1}{2}-\nu}

for all
m
large and
\nu
sufficiently close to
\frac{1}{2}
(it is a zero of either numerator or denominator in (
1.0.11
) depending on the parity of
m
).

Formula (
1.0.11
) contains only two leading terms of the asymptotic expansion of the solution
u(x,p(\varkappa,x))
while (
1.0.9
) gives us a full expansion. In theory, our technique can be extended to obtain more terms, but in practice it is computationally unfeasible except in the case
m=0
, which is addressed in the following theorem.

Theorem 1.3
.

For each natural number
n
, there exists a constant
D_{3}(n)>0
such that for all

(1.0.12)

\displaystyle x\geq D_{3}(n)+8\ln|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}\quad\text{and}\quad\varkappa x=x-\nu\ln x,\quad|\nu|\leq\tfrac{1}{2},

it holds that

(1.0.13)

u(x,p(\varkappa,x))=2\varepsilon\ln\left(\frac{P_{n}(-x^{\nu})+\mathcal{O}_{n}\big(x^{\nu-n-\frac{1}{2}}\big)}{P_{n}(x^{\nu})+\mathcal{O}_{n}\big(x^{\nu-n-\frac{1}{2}}\big)}\right),

where
P_{n}(t)
is a polynomial of degree
n
defined by

P_{n}(t):=\det(I-t\,C_{n}L_{n}),

where
C_{n}=[c_{n,k+m-1}]_{k,m=1}^{2n-1}
(
k
is the row index and
m
is the column one),

(1.0.14)

c_{n,k}:=\frac{\mathrm{i}^{k}}{\sqrt{2\pi x}}\sum_{m=\left\lceil\frac{k-1}{2}\right\rceil}^{n-1}\left(-\frac{2}{x}\right)^{m}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)_{m}\binom{m+\frac{1}{2}}{2m+1-k}

for each
k=1,\ldots,2n-1
and
c_{n,k}:=0
if
k\geq 2n
, and

(1.0.15)

L_{n}:=\left[\frac{(-1)^{m}}{(2\mathrm{i})^{m+k-1}}\binom{-m}{k-1}\right]_{k,m=1}^{2n-1}.

Moreover, if we write

P_{n}(t)=1+p_{n,1}t+p_{n,2}t^{2}+\cdots+p_{n,2n-1}t^{2n-1},

then
p_{n,1}=\mathcal{O}\big(x^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)
,
p_{n,2k}=\mathcal{O}(x^{-k(k+1)})
and
p_{n,2k+1}=\mathcal{O}(x^{-\frac{1}{2}-k(k+2)})
,
k\geq 1
.

Clearly,
P_{n}(t)
is simply the reciprocal of the characteristic polynomial of
C_{n}L_{n}
.

Remark
.

Regions (
1.0.8
) and (
1.0.12
) overlap for
x+D_{1}\leq\varkappa x\leq x+\tfrac{1}{2}\ln x
.
Hence, we can readily see by comparing (
1.0.9
) and (
1.0.13
) that

p_{n,1}=-\mathrm{tr}(C_{n}L_{n})=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2\pi x}}\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\frac{a_{m}}{x^{m}},

which also can be verified by a direct computation of the trace.

Remark
.

It follows from the previous remark that

u(x)=2\varepsilon\ln\left(\frac{1-\tfrac{x^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}}{2\sqrt{2\pi}}+\cdots+\mathcal{O}_{n}\big(x^{\nu-n-\frac{1}{2}}\big)}{1+\tfrac{x^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}}{2\sqrt{2\pi}}+\cdots+\mathcal{O}_{n}\big(x^{\nu-n-\frac{1}{2}}\big)}\right),

which matches exactly with (
1.0.11
) (recall that
\chi_{0}=0
), where
\cdots
represent an increasing with
n
number of terms of the expansion. That is, Theorem
1.3
, in theory, gives a full asymptotic expansion of
u(x)
as compared to Theorem
1.2
, but only up to the first Stokes line
\varkappa x=x-\tfrac{1}{2}\ln x
.

Next, we turn our attention to the case
\varkappa\in(0,1)
. We show below in Lemma
5.1
that to each
\varkappa\in(0,1)
there corresponds a unique
\alpha\in(0,\tfrac{\pi}{2})
such that

(1.0.16)

\displaystyle\varkappa=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\alpha}\sqrt{2(\cos 2\theta-\cos 2\alpha)}\,d\theta.

Moreover,
\alpha(\varkappa)
is continuous and increasing with range
(0,\tfrac{\pi}{2})
. In fact, as in Lemma
5.1
, definition (
1.0.16
) can be rewritten as

(1.0.17)

\displaystyle\varkappa=E(\sin\alpha)-\cos^{2}\alpha\,K(\sin\alpha),

where
K(\cdot)
and
E(\cdot)
are the complete elliptic integrals of the first and second kind:

(1.0.18)

K(k)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{(1-y^{2})(1-k^{2}y^{2})}}\,dy\quad\text{and}\quad E(k)=\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\frac{1-k^{2}y^{2}}{1-y^{2}}}\,dy.

Figure 2
.
Graphs of
y=\sd(x,k)
for
k=0,0.5,0.8,1
;
y=\sd(x,0)=\sin(x)
(blue solid curve);
y=\sd(x,0.5)
(purple dashed curve);
y=\sd(x,0.8)
(pink dash-dotted curve);
y=\sd(x,1)=\sinh(x)
(red long-dash-dotted curve).

Recall also that
\sd(\cdot,\mathsf{k})
is one of the Jacobian elliptic functions corresponding to the elliptic modulus
\mathsf{k}
, where, to shorten our notation, we set

(1.0.19)

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}:=\sin\alpha,\quad\mathsf{k}^{\prime}:=\cos\alpha,\quad\mathsf{K}:=K(\mathsf{k}),\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{K}^{\prime}:=K(\mathsf{k}^{\prime}),

which are, of course, functions of
\varkappa
. The function
\sd(\cdot,\mathsf{k})
is odd and periodic on the real line with period
4\mathsf{K}
, see
[
18
, Table 22.4.1]
. It is increasing from
-1/\mathsf{k}^{\prime}
to
1/\mathsf{k}^{\prime}
on
(-\mathsf{K},\mathsf{K})
and then symmetrically decreasing on
(\mathsf{K},3\mathsf{K})
, see Figure
2
.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
\varkappa\in(0,1)
and, as before,
\pi\kappa=\varkappa x=\tfrac{1}{2}\ln(|p(\varkappa,x)|^{2}-1)
. Given
\delta\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
, define

(1.0.20)

\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}:=\left\{(\varkappa,x)~\big|~\left|\frac{x-2\kappa\mathsf{K}^{\prime}}{2\mathsf{K}}-n-\frac{1}{2}\right|<\delta,~n\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

Then, there exists a large enough constant
D_{4}>0
such that for all
(\varkappa,x)\notin\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
satisfying

(1.0.21)

\displaystyle(D_{\delta}^{2}x)^{\frac{1}{3}}+\ln|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}\leq\varkappa x\leq x-(x^{2}D_{\delta}\ln x)^{\frac{1}{3}}

and
x\geq e
, where
D_{\delta}:=\delta^{-2}D_{4}
, it holds that

(1.0.22)

u(x,p(\varkappa,x))=\varepsilon\ln\left(\frac{1-\mathsf{k}^{\prime}\sd(x-2\kappa\mathsf{K}^{\prime},\mathsf{k})+E_{\varkappa}(x)}{1+\mathsf{k}^{\prime}\sd(x-2\kappa\mathsf{K}^{\prime},\mathsf{k})+E_{\varkappa}(x)}\right),

where
x\delta^{2}\mathsf{K}^{2}\mathsf{k}^{3}(\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{6}|E_{\varkappa}(x)|\leq C
for some absolute constant
C
.

Remark
.

When
x-2\kappa\mathsf{K}^{\prime}=(2n+1)\mathsf{K}
, it holds that
\mathsf{k}^{\prime}\sd(x-2\kappa\mathsf{K}^{\prime},\mathsf{k})=(-1)^{n}
. Thus, the fraction in (
1.0.22
) is close to either zero or infinity at such points. This makes it more transparent why one might need to introduce the set
\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
and why the error term could be expected to depend on
\delta
(the distance to singularities). Of course, the same formation of singularities can be seen in (
1.0.2
) and (
1.0.25
) further below around the points where the sine function is equal to
\pm 1
. However, in those formulae the error terms hold uniformly for all
x
large and no analogue of the set
\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
is needed. The difference stems from employed techniques: one uses global parametrix that utilizes theta functions and the other uses dressing method with polynomial prefactor.

Remark
.

Assuming (
1.0.21
) and
x\geq e
, we show further below in Lemma
C.2
that
x\delta^{2}\mathsf{K}^{2}\mathsf{k}^{3}(\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{6}\geq c_{0}D_{4}
for some absolute constant
c_{0}
. Thus, it holds that

|E_{\varkappa}(x)|\leq C/(c_{0}D_{4}),

which can be made small by choosing
D_{4}
large. However, if
\epsilon\leq\varkappa\leq 1-\epsilon
for some
\epsilon>0
, then
\mathsf{K}^{2}\mathsf{k}^{3}(\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{6}\geq c_{\epsilon}
for some constant
c_{\epsilon}
, in which case

E_{\varkappa}(x)=\mathcal{O}_{\delta,\epsilon}\big(x^{-1}\big).

Remark
.

Trivially, every given point in the region
0<\varkappa<1
,
e\leq x
lies on some curve
\varkappa x=x-(m+\nu)\ln x
for an appropriate (and mostly very large) choice of
m+\nu
. This makes (
1.0.11
) meaningful only for very large
x
as the error term in (
1.0.11
) depends on
m
. It seems then that
x
should be so large that there is no overlap of this part of the curve
\varkappa x=x-(m+\nu)\ln x
with the region in (
1.0.21
). This makes direct comparison between (
1.0.11
) and (
1.0.22
) impossible, which is certainly a significant drawback of the logarithmic cascades asymptotics.

Remark
.

Recall (
1.0.3
), where now
\kappa=\kappa(\varkappa,x)
and
\phi=\phi(\varkappa,x)
. When

\pi\kappa=\varkappa x=Dx^{\frac{1}{3}},

we show in Theorem
C.4
, further below in Appendix
C
, that

(1.0.23)

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}^{\prime}\sd(x-2\kappa\mathsf{K}^{\prime},\mathsf{k})=\varepsilon\sin\left(x-\kappa\ln x+\phi\right)+\mathcal{O}\big(x^{-\frac{1}{3}}D^{2}\ln x\big)

as
x\to\infty
. This expression clearly shows the leading order matching of formula (
1.0.22
) with the one in (
1.0.2
), see also (
1.0.25
).

Finally, the following theorem holds.

Theorem 1.5
.

There exists a large enough constant
D_{5}>0
such that for all

(1.0.24)

\displaystyle(\varkappa x)_{+}\leq\left(D_{5}^{-1}x\right)^{\frac{1}{3}},\quad(\varkappa x)_{+}=\max\{\varkappa x,1\},

it holds that

(1.0.25)

\displaystyle u(x;p(\varkappa,x))=\ln\left(\frac{1-\sin\left(x-\kappa\ln x+\phi\right)+\mathcal{O}((\varkappa x)_{+}^{3}x^{-1})}{1+\sin\left(x-\kappa\ln x+\phi\right)+\mathcal{O}((\varkappa x)_{+}^{3}x^{-1})}\right),

where
\kappa,\phi
are as in (
1.0.3
) and the constants in the error terms
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
are absolute.

Formula (
1.0.25
) indeed does not have
\varepsilon
in front of the logarithm. This is already anticipated by the presence of
\varepsilon
in both (
1.0.22
) and (
1.0.23
).

Remark
.

Even though formulae (
1.0.22
) and (
1.0.25
) match to the leading order (as explained in the remark preceding Theorem
1.5
), unfortunately the region in (
1.0.21
) and (
1.0.24
) do not overlap as
D_{\delta}^{2}>D_{5}^{-1}
. On the other hand, Theorem
1.5
does overlap with Theorem
IMY
as the former does cover every
|p|^{2}\geq 2
fixed (i.e.,
\varkappa x\geq 0
fixed).

Acknowledgments: The authors thank Thomas Bothner, Percy Deift, Alexander Its, and Igor Krasovsky for sharing the preprint
[
7
]
, and also thank them, along with Peter Miller, for helpful discussions and remarks. The first author was partially supported by a scholarship from the Japan Student Services Organization and the Hokushin Scholarship Foundation.

2 Proof of Theorem
1.1

Throughout the paper,
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}
are the Pauli matrices

\sigma_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\sigma_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

The symbols
\mathbb{C}
and
\overline{\mathbb{C}}
stand for the complex plane and the extended complex plane, respectively. Moreover, we set
\mathbb{C}^{*}:=\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. We shall denote by
S^{\rho}
the circle centered at the origin of radius
\rho
. Some of our results required
x
to be sufficiently large. Even if no restriction is mentioned, we always assume that
x\geq 1
.

2.1  Direct and Inverse Monodromy Problem

As standard in the theory of Painlevé transcendents, we view (
1.0.1
) as a compatibility condition of an overdetermined system of linear differential equations, a Lax pair. A Lax pair for the sinh-Gordon equation was first found by Flaschka-Newell
[
19
]
, see also
[
20
, Chapter 15]
. Let
\Psi(\lambda,x)
be a
2\times 2
matrix function on
\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}
such that

(2.1.1)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{d\Psi}{d\lambda}=\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}x^{2}}{16}\sigma_{3}+\frac{xu_{x}}{4\lambda}\sigma_{1}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\lambda^{2}}Q\right)\Psi,\\
\frac{d\Psi}{dx}=\left(\frac{u_{x}}{2}\sigma_{1}+\frac{\mathrm{i}x\lambda}{8}\sigma_{3}\right)\Psi,\end{cases}

where

(2.1.2)

Q=\begin{pmatrix}\cosh u&-\sinh u\\
\sinh u&-\cosh u\end{pmatrix}.

One can readily verify that the compatibility condition
\Psi_{\lambda x}=\Psi_{x\lambda}
is equivalent to the sinh-Gordon Painlevé III equation (
1.0.1
).

The first equation in (
2.1.1
) has two singular points
\lambda=0,\infty
. Studying its Stokes sectors and formal solutions around these singularities allows one to identify the monodromy data of the equation, see for example
[
30
, Section 3.1]
. As it turns out, the corresponding monodromy surface is given by

(2.1.3)

\displaystyle\left\{m=(A,s^{\mathbb{R}},B^{\mathbb{R}})\in\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\,|~~|A|^{2}=1+(B^{\mathbb{R}})^{2},~~A-\overline{A}=\mathrm{i}s^{\mathbb{R}}B^{\mathbb{R}}\right\}.

The monodromy data
(A,s^{\mathbb{R}},B^{\mathbb{R}})
can be re-parametrized by a pair
(p,\iota)
, where
p\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}
,
|p|>1
and
\iota\in\{\pm 1\}
, via

(2.1.4)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}\displaystyle s^{\mathbb{R}}=-2\operatorname{Im}(p),\quad B^{\mathbb{R}}=\frac{\iota}{\sqrt{|p|^{2}-1}},\quad A=\overline{p}B^{\mathbb{R}},&p\neq\infty,\\
s^{\mathbb{R}}\text{ is any},\quad B^{\mathbb{R}}=0,\quad A=\iota,&p=\infty\end{array}\right.

(the inverse map is given by
p=\overline{A}/B^{\mathbb{R}}
,
\iota=\sign(B^{\mathbb{R}})
when
B^{\mathbb{R}}
is non-zero and
p=\infty
,
\iota=A
otherwise).

As we explain in the last remark of
[
30
, Section 3.2]
, if
u
is a solution of (
1.0.1
) corresponding to the monodromy data
(p,\iota)
, then the solution corresponding to
(p,-\iota)
is
u+2\pi\mathrm{i}
. Hence, real solutions of (
1.0.1
) do not depend on
\iota
. Moreover, it is also pointed out in
[
30
, Appendix A]
that the solution corresponding to the monodromy data
(-p,-\iota)
is given by
-u
. Hence, if
\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
, then

u(x,(p,\iota))=\epsilon u(x,(\epsilon p,1)).

It readily follows from (
2.1.4
) that transformations
(p,\iota)\mapsto(p,-\iota)
and
(p,\iota)\mapsto(-p,-\iota)
correspond to
(A,s^{\mathbb{R}},B^{\mathbb{R}})\mapsto(-A,s^{\mathbb{R}},-B^{\mathbb{R}})
and
(A,s^{\mathbb{R}},B^{\mathbb{R}})\mapsto(A,-s^{\mathbb{R}},-B^{\mathbb{R}})
, respectively. Thus, if
\varepsilon=\sign(\operatorname{Re}(p))
and
\iota=\sign(B^{\mathbb{R}})
, then

(2.1.5)

u\big(x,(A,s^{\mathbb{R}},B^{\mathbb{R}})\big)=\varepsilon u\big(x,(\iota\varepsilon A,\varepsilon s^{\mathbb{R}},\iota B^{\mathbb{R}})\big).

Observe that
\iota B^{\mathbb{R}}=|B^{\mathbb{R}}|
and that
\operatorname{Re}(\iota\varepsilon A)>0
. Therefore, in what follows, we study only solutions corresponding to
B^{\mathbb{R}}>0
and
\operatorname{Re}(A)>0
and recover the general case from (
2.1.5
). Since the representation of the monodromy data
m(\varkappa,x)
via triples
(A,s^{\mathbb{R}},B^{\mathbb{R}})
is more convenient for us, we restate (
1.0.5
) as follows. Given real parameters
s^{\mathbb{R}}
and
\varkappa>0
, we are interested in the asymptotic behavior of the solution
u(x;m(\varkappa,x))
corresponding to the monodromy data
m(\varkappa,x)=(A,s^{\mathbb{R}},B^{\mathbb{R}})
such that

(2.1.6)

B^{\mathbb{R}}=e^{-\varkappa x},\quad|A|^{2}=1+e^{-2\varkappa x},\quad 2\operatorname{Im}(A)=s^{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\varkappa x},\quad\operatorname{Re}(A)>0.

It is well understood in the theory of integrable systems that the solution
\Psi(\lambda,x)
of the first equation in (
2.1.1
) corresponding to the monodromy data
m
and therefore the solution
u(x;m)
of (
1.0.1
) can be recovered via a certain Riemann-Hilbert problem, see for example
[
30
, Section 3.2]
.

Figure 3
.
The contour
\Gamma_{\rho}
and the jump matrices
G_{\hat{\Psi}}
for RHP
1
.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 1
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
\hat{\Psi}(\lambda)
such that

(1)

\hat{\Psi}(\lambda)
is analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\rho}
, where
\Gamma_{\rho}=\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}\cup S^{\rho}
is oriented as on Figure
3
;

(2)

one-sided traces
\hat{\Psi}_{\pm}(\lambda)
defined by

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{\pm}(\lambda):=\lim_{\{\pm\text{ side of }\Gamma_{\rho}\}\ni\lambda^{\prime}\to\lambda\in\Gamma_{\rho}}\hat{\Psi}(\lambda^{\prime}),

where a subscript
+
(resp.
-
) refers to a limit to the oriented contour from its left (resp. right) side, exist a.e. on
\Gamma_{\rho}
, belong to
L^{2}(\Gamma_{\rho})
, and satisfy

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{+}(\lambda)=\hat{\Psi}_{-}(\lambda)G_{\hat{\Psi}},\quad\lambda\in\Gamma_{\rho},

where the jump matrices
G_{\hat{\Psi}}(\lambda)
on
\Gamma_{\rho}
are as on Figure
3
;

(3)

it holds that

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}(\lambda)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}(I+\mathcal{O}(1/\lambda))e^{\frac{\mathrm{i}x^{2}\lambda}{16}\sigma_{3}}&\text{as}\quad\lambda\to\infty,\\
P_{0}(I+\mathcal{O}(\lambda))e^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\lambda}\sigma_{3}}&\text{as}\quad\lambda\to 0,\end{array}\right.

where
P_{0}:=\begin{pmatrix}\cosh\frac{u}{2}&\sinh\frac{u}{2}\\
\sinh\frac{u}{2}&\cosh\frac{u}{2}\end{pmatrix}
.

That is, the monodromy data
m
determines the jump matrices
G_{\hat{\Psi}}
and the unique solution
\hat{\Psi}(\lambda)
of this Riemann-Hilbert problem determines the corresponding solution
u(x;m)
via its asymptotics at the origin.

2.2  Renormalized Riemann-Hilbert Problem

Take
\rho=4/x
in RHP
1
and apply the following gauge transformation:

(2.2.1)

\displaystyle Y(\lambda)=\hat{\Psi}\left(\frac{4\lambda}{x}\right)e^{-x\theta(\lambda)\sigma_{3}},\quad\theta(\lambda):=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\left(\lambda-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right).

Then
Y(\lambda)
is the solution of the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 2
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
Y(\lambda)
such that

(1)

Y(\lambda)
is analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma
, where
\Gamma=\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}\cup S^{1}
is oriented as on Figure
4
;

(2)

one-sided traces
Y_{\pm}(\lambda)
exist a.e. on
\Gamma
, belong to
L^{2}(\Gamma)
, and satisfy

\displaystyle Y_{+}(\lambda)=Y_{-}(\lambda)G_{Y}(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\Gamma,

where the jump matrices
G_{Y}(\lambda)
on
\Gamma
are as on Figure
4
;

(3)

it holds that

\displaystyle Y(\lambda)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}I+\mathcal{O}(1/\lambda)&\text{as}\quad\lambda\to\infty,\\
P_{0}(I+\mathcal{O}(\lambda))&\text{as}\quad\lambda\to 0.\end{array}\right.

Figure 4
.
The jump matrices
G_{Y}(\lambda)
for RHP
2
.

It holds on the imaginary axis that

(2.2.2)

4\theta(\mathrm{i}y)=-(y+1/y),\quad y\in\mathbb{R},

while for
\lambda\in S^{1}
we have that
2\theta(\lambda)=-\operatorname{Im}(\lambda)
and therefore

(2.2.3)

B^{\mathbb{R}}e^{\pm 2x\theta(\lambda)}=e^{-x(\varkappa\pm\operatorname{Im}(\lambda))}\leq e^{-(\varkappa-1)x}.

Furthermore, recall that
\operatorname{Re}(A)>0
. It readily follows from (
2.1.6
) that

\operatorname{Re}(A)^{2}=1+\left(1-\tfrac{1}{4}(s^{\mathbb{R}})^{2}\right)e^{-2\varkappa x}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\left(\tfrac{1}{4}(s^{\mathbb{R}})^{2}-1\right)e^{-2\varkappa x}\leq 1.

Trivially, we have that

|A-1|\leq|\operatorname{Re}(A)-1|+|\operatorname{Im}(A)|=\left|1-\tfrac{1}{4}(s^{\mathbb{R}})^{2}\right|\frac{e^{-2\varkappa x}}{1+\operatorname{Re}(A)}+\frac{1}{2}|s^{\mathbb{R}}|e^{-\varkappa x}.

Hence, if
|s^{\mathbb{R}}|\leq 2
, then
|A-1|<2e^{-\varkappa x}
. On the other hand, if
|s^{\mathbb{R}}|\geq 2
, then

|A-1|\leq\left(\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{4}(s^{\mathbb{R}})^{2}-1}+\frac{1}{2}|s^{\mathbb{R}}|\right)e^{-\varkappa x}\leq|s^{\mathbb{R}}|e^{-\varkappa x}.

Therefore, we can conclude that

(2.2.4)

\displaystyle|A-1|\leq 2|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}e^{-\varkappa x}.

Thus, the jump
G_{Y}(\lambda)
is close
I
when (
1.0.8
) holds. We make this statement more precise in the next subsection.

2.3  Asymptotic Analysis

Recall that
\varkappa_{1}=\min\{1,\varkappa-1\}
. Throughout the paper,

\|A\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}:=\int_{\Gamma}\|A(\lambda)\|^{2}|d\lambda|\quad\text{and}\quad\|A\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}:=\mathrm{ess}\sup_{\Gamma}\|A(\lambda)\|,

where
\|A\|
is the Frobenius norm of a matrix
A
.

We get from (
2.2.2
), (
2.2.3
), and (
2.2.4
) that

(2.3.1)

\|G_{Y}-I\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}\leq 2|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}e^{-\varkappa_{1}x}.

If
s^{\mathbb{R}}=0
, there is no jump on the imaginary axis and the above estimate holds with
\varkappa_{1}
replaced by
\varkappa-1
. Observe that

(2.3.2)

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{S^{1}}e^{\pm 4x\theta(\lambda)}|d\lambda|=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{\mp 2x\cos y}dy=I_{0}(2x)\leq C_{1}\frac{e^{2x}}{\sqrt{x}},

for some constant
C_{1}
, see
[
18
, (10.32.1)]
and the remark right after Theorem
1.1
, where to get the first equality, one needs to make a substitution
\lambda\mapsto\mathrm{i}\lambda
first. Moreover, we get from (
2.2.2
) that

(2.3.3)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\infty}e^{\pm 4x\theta(\lambda)}|d\lambda|

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{0}^{1}+\int_{1}^{\infty}\right)e^{-x(y+\frac{1}{y})}dy=2\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{te^{-2xt}}{\sqrt{t^{2}-1}}dt

(2.3.4)

\displaystyle=2e^{-2x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{(y+1)e^{-2xy}}{\sqrt{y(y+2)}}dy\leq 2e^{-2x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\frac{y+2}{y}}e^{-2xy}dy

(2.3.5)

\displaystyle=2\frac{e^{-2x}}{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\frac{t+2x}{t}}e^{-2t}dt\leq 2\frac{e^{-2x}}{\sqrt{x}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\frac{t+2}{t}}e^{-2t}dt,

where to deduce the second equality we made substitutions
y=t\pm\sqrt{t^{2}-1}
depending on the range of
y
and used
x\geq 1
on the last step. It readily follows from (
2.2.4
), (
2.3.2
), and (
2.3.5
) that

(2.3.6)

\|G_{Y}-I\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq C_{2}|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}x^{-\frac{1}{4}}e^{-\varkappa_{1}x}

for some absolute constant
C_{2}
. Again, If
s^{\mathbb{R}}=0
, (
2.3.5
) does not apply and (
2.3.2
) yields that (
2.3.6
) holds with
\varkappa_{1}
simply replaced by
\varkappa-1
.

Next, by the small norm theorem, see for example
[
20
, Theorem 8.1]
, (
2.3.1
) implies that there exists a unique solution of RHP
2
for all
\varkappa_{1}x
large and

(2.3.7)

\displaystyle Y(\lambda)=I+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\rho(\lambda^{\prime})(G_{Y}(\lambda^{\prime})-I)}{\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda}d\lambda^{\prime},\quad\lambda\notin\Gamma,

where
\displaystyle\rho(\lambda^{\prime})=Y_{-}(\lambda^{\prime})
for
\lambda^{\prime}\in\Gamma
. More precisely, if we denote by
\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}
an operator from
L^{2}(\Gamma)
into itself that acts according to the rule

(2.3.8)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}F)(\mu)&:=\left(\mathcal{C}_{-}\left[F(G_{Y}-I)\right]\right)(\mu)\\
&=\lim_{\lambda\to\mu\in\Gamma^{-}}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma}\frac{F(\lambda^{\prime})(G_{Y}(\lambda^{\prime})-I)}{\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda}d\lambda^{\prime},\end{aligned}

where
\mathcal{C}_{-}
is the Cauchy operator that sends a function into the trace on
\Gamma^{-}
of its Cauchy integral, then, by taking the limit
\lambda\to\mu\in\Gamma^{-}
, we can rewrite (
2.3.7
) as

\rho=I+\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}\rho.

In fact, the solvability of RHP
2
is equivalent to the solvability of this integral equation. Clearly,
\|\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}\|\leq\|\mathcal{C}_{-}\|\|G_{Y}-I\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}
(it is known that
\mathcal{C}_{-}
is a bounded operator on
L^{2}(\Gamma)
, see Appendix
A
). Hence, according to (
2.3.1
), we can choose
D_{1}
in (
1.0.8
) large enough so that
\|\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}\|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
(the specific value
\tfrac{1}{2}
is not important, any value in
(0,1)
will do). Then, using the von Neumann series, we get that

(2.3.9)

\rho=(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma})^{-1}I=I+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}^{m}I,

where
\mathcal{I}
is the identity operator. In particular,

(2.3.10)

\|\rho-I\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq 2\|\mathcal{C}_{-}(G_{Y}-I)\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq C_{3}|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}x^{-\frac{1}{4}}e^{-\varkappa_{1}x}

by (
2.3.6
) for some absolute constant
C_{3}
.

Since
G_{Y}(\lambda^{\prime})-I
vanishes exponentially at the origin,
Y_{+}(0)=Y_{-}(0)=:Y(0)
and one has that

(2.3.11)

\displaystyle Y(0)=I+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma}\frac{G_{Y}(\lambda^{\prime})-I}{\lambda^{\prime}}d\lambda^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma}\frac{(\rho(\lambda^{\prime})-I)(G_{Y}(\lambda^{\prime})-I)}{\lambda^{\prime}}d\lambda.

Notice that the supremum norm of
(G_{Y}(\lambda^{\prime})-I)/\lambda^{\prime}
on
\mathrm{i}[-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}]
has an upper bound as in (
2.3.6
) because

y^{-2}e^{4x\theta(\mathrm{i}y)}\leq y^{-2}e^{-\frac{x}{y}}\leq 4e^{-2x}\leq 4x^{-\frac{1}{4}}e^{-x},\quad y\in\big[0,\tfrac{1}{2}\big].

Hence, (
2.3.6
) also holds with
G_{Y}(\lambda^{\prime})-I
replaced by
(G_{Y}(\lambda^{\prime})-I)/\lambda^{\prime}
. Thus,

(2.3.12)

\displaystyle Y(0)=I+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma}\frac{G_{Y}(\lambda^{\prime})-I}{\lambda^{\prime}}d\lambda^{\prime}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{e^{-2\varkappa_{1}x}}{\sqrt{x}}\right)

by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and (
2.3.10
), where, again, the constant in
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is an absolute multiple of
|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}
. On the imaginary axis, we have that

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma\cap\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}}\frac{G_{Y}(\lambda^{\prime})-I}{\lambda^{\prime}}d\lambda^{\prime}=\left(\frac{s^{\mathbb{R}}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\infty}e^{2x\theta(\lambda^{\prime})}\frac{d\lambda^{\prime}}{\lambda^{\prime}}\right)\sigma_{1},

where we made a substitution
\lambda^{\prime}\mapsto-\lambda^{\prime}
in the
(2,1)
-entry. Using (
2.2.2
) and
[
18
, (10.32.9)]
we can rewrite the above integral as

(2.3.13)

\frac{s^{\mathbb{R}}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{x}{2}(y+\frac{1}{y})}\frac{dy}{y}=\frac{s^{\mathbb{R}}}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-x\cosh s}ds=\frac{s^{\mathbb{R}}}{\pi}K_{0}(x).

On the other hand, on the unit circle, we get that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{S^{1}}\frac{G_{Y}(\lambda^{\prime})-I}{\lambda^{\prime}}d\lambda^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\big(\operatorname{Re}(A)-1\big)I-\left(\frac{B^{\mathbb{R}}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{S^{1}}e^{2x\theta(\lambda^{\prime})}\frac{d\lambda^{\prime}}{\lambda^{\prime}}\right)\sigma_{1}

\displaystyle=\big(\operatorname{Re}(A)-1\big)I-B^{\mathbb{R}}I_{0}(x)\sigma_{1},

where we again made a substitution
\lambda^{\prime}\mapsto-\lambda^{\prime}
in the
(2,1)
-entry and the second equality follows from a computation identical to (
2.3.2
). Altogether, we have shown that

(2.3.14)

Y(0)=\operatorname{Re}(A)I+\left(\frac{s^{\mathbb{R}}}{\pi}K_{0}(x)-B^{\mathbb{R}}I_{0}(x)\right)\sigma_{1}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{e^{-2\varkappa_{1}x}}{\sqrt{x}}\right),

where the constant in
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is an absolute multiple of
|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}
.

It immediately follows from RHP
2
(3) and the definition of
P_{0}
in RHP
1
(3) that

(2.3.15)

Y(0)=P_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}\cosh\frac{u}{2}&\sinh\frac{u}{2}\\
\sinh\frac{u}{2}&\cosh\frac{u}{2}\end{pmatrix}.

By comparing (
2.3.14
) with (
2.3.15
), we get from (
2.2.4
) that

u(x)=2\ln\left[1+\frac{s^{\mathbb{R}}}{\pi}K_{0}(x)-B^{\mathbb{R}}I_{0}(x)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{e^{-2\varkappa_{1}x}}{\sqrt{x}}\right)\right]

for all
\varkappa_{1}x\geq D_{1}+\ln|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}
. Since it holds that

\left|\frac{s^{\mathbb{R}}}{\pi}K_{0}(x)-\varepsilon B^{\mathbb{R}}I_{0}(x)\right|\leq|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}\frac{C_{4}}{2}\frac{e^{-x}+e^{-\varkappa_{1}x}}{\sqrt{x}}\leq|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}C_{4}\frac{e^{-\varkappa_{1}x}}{\sqrt{x}}

for some absolute constant
C_{4}
, we have that

u(x)=\frac{2s^{\mathbb{R}}}{\pi}K_{0}(x)-2e^{-\varkappa x}I_{0}(x)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{e^{-2\varkappa_{1}x}}{\sqrt{x}}\right)

in the considered range. If we no longer assume that
B^{\mathbb{R}}>0
and
\operatorname{Re}(A)>0
in (
2.1.6
), then (
2.1.5
) together with the above formula gives

u\big(x,(A,s^{\mathbb{R}},B^{\mathbb{R}})\big)=\varepsilon\left(\frac{2\varepsilon s^{\mathbb{R}}}{\pi}K_{0}(x)-2e^{-\varkappa x}I_{0}(x)\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{e^{-2\varkappa_{1}x}}{\sqrt{x}}\right),

where the constant in
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is an absolute multiple of
|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}
, which is exactly (
1.0.9
) in the considered range. Finally, notice that the remarks made right after (
2.3.1
) and (
2.3.6
) imply that when
s^{\mathbb{R}}=0
, the above formula holds with
\varkappa_{1}
replaced by
\varkappa-1
and is valid for all
(\varkappa-1)x\geq D_{1}
. This finishes the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

3 Proof of Theorem
1.2

In this section, we continue to work with the matrix function
Y(\lambda)
from (
2.2.1
). We keep using all the notation introduced during the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

3.1  Modified Riemann-Hilbert Problem

It is clear from the analysis of the previous section that the jump matrices in RHP
2
(2) are close to the identity except in the vicinity of
\pm\mathrm{i}
when
\varkappa=1
. To overcome this difficulty, we first isolate the parts of the jumps that cause the difficulty. To this end, let
U_{\mathrm{i}}
and
U_{-\mathrm{i}}
be disks of fixed radius
\varrho\in(0,1)
centered at
\mathrm{i}
and
-\mathrm{i}
, respectively. We orient
\partial U_{\mathrm{i}}
and
\partial U_{-\mathrm{i}}

clockwise
. Define
\tilde{Y}(\lambda)
to be equal to
Y(\lambda)
everywhere except within
U_{\mathrm{i}}\cap\{\operatorname{Re}(\lambda)<0\}
and
U_{-\mathrm{i}}\cap\{\operatorname{Re}(\lambda)>0\}
, where we set

(3.1.1)

\tilde{Y}(\lambda):=Y(\lambda)\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}s^{\mathbb{R}}e^{2x\theta(\lambda)}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in U_{\mathrm{i}}\cap\{\operatorname{Re}(\lambda)<0\},\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}s^{\mathbb{R}}e^{-2x\theta(\lambda)}&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in U_{-\mathrm{i}}\cap\{\operatorname{Re}(\lambda)>0\}.\end{array}\right.

Clearly, this modification of
Y(\lambda)
moves its jump away from the imaginary axis within
U_{\pm\mathrm{i}}
to
\partial U_{\pm\mathrm{i}}
. Moreover, using
A-\overline{A}=\mathrm{i}s^{\mathbb{R}}B^{\mathbb{R}}
, one can readily verify that

\tilde{Y}_{+}(\lambda)=\tilde{Y}_{-}(\lambda)e^{x\theta(\lambda)\sigma_{3}}\begin{Bmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\overline{A}&-B^{\mathbb{R}}\\
-B^{\mathbb{R}}&A\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in U_{\mathrm{i}}\cap S^{1}\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\\
\begin{pmatrix}A&-B^{\mathbb{R}}\\
-B^{\mathbb{R}}&\overline{A}\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in U_{-\mathrm{i}}\cap S^{1}\end{Bmatrix}e^{-x\theta(\lambda)\sigma_{3}}.

That is, the middle matrices above no longer depends on the considered half-plane. Next, using
|A|^{2}=1+(B^{\mathbb{R}})^{2}
, we can obtain that

(3.1.2)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\overline{A}&-B^{\mathbb{R}}\\
-B^{\mathbb{R}}&A\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1/A&-B^{\mathbb{R}}\\
0&A\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-B^{\mathbb{R}}/A&1\end{pmatrix}

and

(3.1.3)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}A&-B^{\mathbb{R}}\\
-B^{\mathbb{R}}&\overline{A}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
-B^{\mathbb{R}}&1/A\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&-B^{\mathbb{R}}/A\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

Therefore, we set

(3.1.4)

\hat{Y}(\lambda):=\tilde{Y}(\lambda)\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}\begin{pmatrix}1/A&-B^{\mathbb{R}}e^{2x\theta(\lambda)}\\
0&A\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in U_{\mathrm{i}}\cap\{|\lambda|>1\},\\
\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
-B^{\mathbb{R}}e^{-2x\theta(\lambda)}&1/A\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in U_{-\mathrm{i}}\cap\{|\lambda|>1\},\end{array}\right.

and let
\hat{Y}(\lambda)
be equal to
\tilde{Y}(\lambda)
everywhere else. Clearly,
\hat{Y}(\lambda)
still satisfies RHP
2
except for RHP
2
(2), which now only holds on
\Gamma\setminus(\overline{U}_{\mathrm{i}}\cup\overline{U}_{-\mathrm{i}})
, while

(3.1.5)

\hat{Y}_{+}(\lambda)=\hat{Y}_{-}(\lambda)\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-(B^{\mathbb{R}}/A)e^{-2x\theta(\lambda)}&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in U_{\mathrm{i}}\cap S^{1}\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&-(B^{\mathbb{R}}/A)e^{2x\theta(\lambda)}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in U_{-\mathrm{i}}\cap S^{1},\end{array}\right.

and the jump relations on
\partial U_{\mathrm{i}}\cup\partial U_{-\mathrm{i}}
easily follow from (
3.1.1
) and (
3.1.4
), see Figure
5
.

Figure 5
.
The contour
\hat{\Gamma}
and the jump matrices
G_{\hat{Y}}(\lambda)
.

Thus,
\hat{Y}(\lambda)
solves the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 3
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
\hat{Y}(\lambda)
such that

(1)

\hat{Y}(\lambda)
is analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\hat{\Gamma}
, where contour
\hat{\Gamma}
is depicted on Figure
5
;

(2)

one-sided traces
\hat{Y}_{\pm}(\lambda)
exist a.e. on
\hat{\Gamma}
, belong to
L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})
, and satisfy

\displaystyle\hat{Y}_{+}(\lambda)=\hat{Y}_{-}(\lambda)G_{\hat{Y}}(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\hat{\Gamma},

where the jump matrices
G_{\hat{Y}}(\lambda)
on
\hat{\Gamma}
are as on Figure
5
;

(3)

it holds that

\displaystyle\hat{Y}(\lambda)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}I+\mathcal{O}(1/\lambda)&\text{as}\quad\lambda\to\infty,\\
P_{0}(I+\mathcal{O}(\lambda))&\text{as}\quad\lambda\to 0.\end{array}\right.

3.2  Local Parametrices

In this subsection we construct matrices
P^{(\pm\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)
that have the same jump on
S^{1}\cap U_{\pm\mathrm{i}}
as
\hat{Y}(\lambda)
and have a certain prescribed behavior on
\partial U_{\pm\mathrm{i}}
.

3.2.1  Model Local Parametrix

Let
\mathrm{erfc}(\cdot)
be the complimentary error function, see
[
18
, (7.2.2)]
. Set

(3.2.1)

\displaystyle He_{0}(z):=\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathcal{H}_{0}(z)\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R},

where
\mathcal{H}_{0}(z)
is a sectionally holomorphic function in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
given by

(3.2.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{0}(z):=\pm\tfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{erfc}(\mp\mathrm{i}z)e^{-z^{2}},\quad\pm\operatorname{Im}(z)>0.

It readily follows from
[
18
, (7.4.2)]
that

(3.2.3)

\mathcal{H}_{0+}(s)-\mathcal{H}_{0-}(s)=e^{-s^{2}},\quad s\in\mathbb{R}.

Moreover, it follows from
[
18
, (7.12.1)]
that
\mathcal{H}_{0}(z)
has the following asymptotic expansion

(3.2.4)

\mathcal{H}_{0}(z)\sim\frac{-1}{2\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\pi}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(1/2)_{k}}{z^{2k+1}},

which holds uniformly for all
|z|
separated away from
0
.

Furthermore, set
\gamma_{k}=2^{k}/k!
for
k\geq 0
. Given a natural number
m>0
, define

He_{m}(z)=\begin{pmatrix}p_{m}(z)&\mathcal{H}_{m}(z)\\
-2\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\pi}\gamma_{m-1}p_{m-1}(z)&-2\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\pi}\gamma_{m-1}\mathcal{H}_{m-1}(z)\end{pmatrix},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R},

where
p_{m}(z)
is the monic Hermite orthogonal polynomial of degree
m
:

\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{m}(s)s^{k}e^{-s^{2}}ds=0,\quad k\in\{0,\ldots,m-1\},

(
p_{m}(z)=2^{-m}H_{m}(z)
in the notation of
[
18
, Table 18.3.1]
) and

(3.2.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{m}(z):=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{p_{m}(s)e^{-s^{2}}}{s-z}ds.

It readily follows from Sokhotski-Plemelj formulae that

(3.2.6)

\mathcal{H}_{m+}(s)-\mathcal{H}_{m-}(s)=p_{m}(s)e^{-s^{2}},\quad s\in\mathbb{R}.

Since
p_{0}(z)\equiv 1
, (
3.2.3
) and (
3.2.6
) with
m=0
are the same. As both expressions (
3.2.2
) and (
3.2.5
) yield functions vanishing at infinity, one can deduce that (
3.2.5
) when
m=0
specializes to (
3.2.2
). It is known, see
[
18
, Equation 18.5.13]
, that

(3.2.7)

\displaystyle p_{m}(z)=\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\left\lfloor m/2\right\rfloor}\frac{\gamma_{m-2l}}{\gamma_{m}}\frac{(-1)^{l}}{l!}z^{-2l}\right)z^{m}.

Furthermore, it follows from orthogonality that

\mathcal{H}_{m}(z)=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{l=m}^{N-1}\frac{1}{z^{l+1}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{m}(s)\,s^{l}e^{-s^{2}}ds\right)+\frac{1}{z^{N}}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{s^{N}p_{m}(s)e^{-s^{2}}}{s-z}ds

for any
N>m
. Notice that if
l
and
m
have different parity, the integrals in the sum above are zero. Otherwise, we can compute using
[
3
, Equations (12) and (13)]
that

(3.2.8)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{m}(s)\,s^{2k+m}e^{-s^{2}}ds=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{\gamma_{2k+m}k!}

for
k\geq 0
. Therefore, it holds that

(3.2.9)

\mathcal{H}_{m}(z)\sim\frac{-1}{2\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\pi}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\gamma_{2k+m}k!}\frac{1}{z^{2k+1}}\right)z^{-m},

uniformly for all
|z|
large (one can readily check that expansions (
3.2.4
) and (
3.2.9
) coincide when
m=0
). Altogether, we see that the matrix function
He_{m}(z)
solves the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 4
.

Given a non-negative integer
m
, find a
2\times 2
matrix function
He_{m}(z)
such that

(1)

He_{m}(z)
is analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
;

(2)

there exist continuous traces
He_{m\pm}(s)
on
\mathbb{R}
that satisfy

(3.2.10)

\displaystyle He_{m+}(s)=He_{m-}(s)\begin{pmatrix}1&e^{-s^{2}}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad s\in\mathbb{R};

(3)

it holds as
z\to\infty
that

He_{m}(z)=\left(I+\frac{1}{z}\begin{pmatrix}0&-1/(2\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\pi}\gamma_{m})\\
-2\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\pi}\gamma_{m-1}&0\end{pmatrix}+\mathcal{O}_{m}\big(z^{-2}\big)\right)z^{m\sigma_{3}},

where
\gamma_{-1}=0
and we point out that the coefficient matrices in parenthesis above are off diagonal next to odd powers of
z
and diagonal next to even powers of
z
by (
3.2.7
) and (
3.2.9
).

3.2.2  Local Parametrix around
\mathrm{i}

To use model parametrices
He_{m}(z)
from the previous subsection, we need local conformal maps around
\pm\mathrm{i}
. To this end, observe that

(3.2.11)

1\pm 2\theta(\lambda)=\pm\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\lambda}(\lambda\mp\mathrm{i})^{2}=:\phi_{\pm\mathrm{i}}^{2}(\lambda),

where
\phi_{\pm\mathrm{i}}(\lambda)
is conformal around
\pm\mathrm{i}
, in particular, in the disk
U_{\pm\mathrm{i}}
, and

(3.2.12)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}\pm\phi_{\mathrm{i}}(\lambda)>0,&\lambda\in U_{\mathrm{i}}\cap S^{1},\quad\mp\operatorname{Re}(\lambda)>0,\\
\pm\phi_{-\mathrm{i}}(\lambda)>0,&\lambda\in U_{-\mathrm{i}}\cap S^{1},~~\pm\operatorname{Re}(\lambda)>0,\end{array}\right.

which fixes the branches of the square roots (
\phi^{\prime}_{\mathrm{i}}(\mathrm{i})=-1/\sqrt{2}
and
\phi^{\prime}_{-\mathrm{i}}(-\mathrm{i})=1/\sqrt{2}
).

Recall (
1.0.10
) and (
2.1.6
). That is,

(3.2.13)

\displaystyle B^{\mathbb{R}}=e^{-\varkappa x}=e^{-x}x^{m+\nu}.

We define local parametrix around
\mathrm{i}
by

(3.2.14)

\displaystyle P^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda):=E^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)He_{m}\big(\sqrt{x}\phi_{\mathrm{i}}(\lambda)\big)(-x^{m+\nu}/A)^{-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{3}}\sigma_{1},\quad\lambda\in U_{\mathrm{i}},

where
E^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)
is an analytic matrix function in
U_{\mathrm{i}}
given by

(3.2.15)

\displaystyle E^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda):=\sigma_{1}\left(-\frac{x^{\nu}}{A}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{3}}\left(\phi_{\mathrm{i}}(\lambda)\frac{\lambda+\mathrm{i}}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\right)^{-m\sigma_{3}},\quad\lambda\in U_{\mathrm{i}},

where the square roots of
-1/A
in (
3.2.14
) and (
3.2.15
) must coincide. Then, one can readily show that
P^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)
satisfies the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 5
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
P^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)
such that

(1)

P^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)
is analytic in
U_{\mathrm{i}}\setminus S^{1}
;

(2)

one-sided traces
P^{(\mathrm{i})}_{\pm}(\lambda)
are continuous on
U_{\mathrm{i}}\cap S^{1}
and satisfy

(3.2.16)

\displaystyle P^{(\mathrm{i})}_{+}(\lambda)=P^{(\mathrm{i})}_{-}(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-(B^{\mathbb{R}}/A)e^{-2x\theta(\lambda)}&1\end{pmatrix};

(3)

it holds for
\lambda\in\partial U_{\mathrm{i}}
that

(3.2.17)

\displaystyle P^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)=T_{i}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)\big(I+\mathcal{O}_{m}\big(x^{-1}\big)\big)\left(\frac{\lambda+\mathrm{i}}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\right)^{m\sigma_{3}}

for
i\in\{1,2\}
, where

(3.2.18)

\displaystyle T_{1}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}1&\displaystyle\frac{t^{(\mathrm{i})}_{12}(\lambda;x)}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
\displaystyle\frac{t^{(\mathrm{i})}_{21}(\lambda;x)}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}&\displaystyle 1+\frac{t_{21}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda;x)t_{12}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda;x)}{(\lambda-\mathrm{i})^{2}}\end{pmatrix}

and

(3.2.19)

\displaystyle T_{2}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle 1+\frac{t_{21}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda;x)t_{12}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda;x)}{(\lambda-\mathrm{i})^{2}}&\displaystyle\frac{t^{(\mathrm{i})}_{12}(\lambda;x)}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
\displaystyle\frac{t^{(\mathrm{i})}_{21}(\lambda;x)}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}&1\end{pmatrix},

while
t^{(\mathrm{i})}_{12}(\lambda;x)
and
t^{(\mathrm{i})}_{21}(\lambda;x)
are holomorphic functions on
U_{\mathrm{i}}
given by

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}t^{(\mathrm{i})}_{12}(\lambda;x)&\displaystyle=2\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\pi}\gamma_{m-1}A\left(\frac{\phi_{\mathrm{i}}(\lambda)}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\right)^{2m-1}\frac{(\lambda+\mathrm{i})^{2m}}{x^{\nu+\frac{1}{2}}},\\
t^{(\mathrm{i})}_{21}(\lambda;x)&\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\gamma_{m}}\frac{1}{A}\left(\frac{\phi_{\mathrm{i}}(\lambda)}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\right)^{-2m-1}\frac{x^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}}{(\lambda+\mathrm{i})^{2m}}.\end{array}\right.

We only need to explain RHP
5
(3). Assuming
\lambda\in\partial U_{\mathrm{i}}
, we get from RHP
4
(3), (
3.2.14
), and (
3.2.15
) that

(3.2.22)

P^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)=\Bigg(I+\frac{1}{\sqrt{x}\phi_{\mathrm{i}}(\lambda)}E^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}0&-1/(2\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\pi}\gamma_{m})\\
-2\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\pi}\gamma_{m-1}&0\end{pmatrix}E^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)^{-1}\\
+\mathcal{O}_{m}\big(x^{-1}\big)\Bigg)\left(\frac{\lambda+\mathrm{i}}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\right)^{m\sigma_{3}},

where we also used the fact that
E^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}(x^{\frac{1}{2}|\nu|})
and that the next term in the expansion is diagonal. Thus, it holds that

(3.2.23)

\displaystyle P^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)=\left(I+\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\begin{pmatrix}0&t^{(\mathrm{i})}_{12}(\lambda;x)\\
t^{(\mathrm{i})}_{21}(\lambda;x)&0\end{pmatrix}+\mathcal{O}_{m}\big(x^{-1}\big)\right)\left(\frac{\lambda+\mathrm{i}}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\right)^{m\sigma_{3}}.

Observe that it holds uniformly in
\partial U_{1}
that

(3.2.24)

\displaystyle t^{(\mathrm{i})}_{12}(\lambda;x)t^{(\mathrm{i})}_{21}(\lambda;x)=\frac{m}{2x}\left(\frac{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}{\phi_{\mathrm{i}}(\lambda)}\right)^{2}.

Thus, it only remains to notice that each
T_{i}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)
has unit determinant and

T_{i}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)^{-1}\left(I+\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\begin{pmatrix}0&t^{(\mathrm{i})}_{12}(\lambda;x)\\
t^{(\mathrm{i})}_{21}(\lambda;x)&0\end{pmatrix}\right)=I+\mathcal{O}_{m}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right).

3.2.3  Local Parametrix around
-\mathrm{i}

The construction around
-\mathrm{i}
is essentially identical. In fact, we simplify the presentation even further by setting

(3.2.25)

\displaystyle P^{(-\mathrm{i})}(\lambda):=\sigma_{1}P^{(\mathrm{i})}(-\lambda)\sigma_{1},\quad\lambda\in U_{-\mathrm{i}}.

Then,
P^{(-\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)
satisfies the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 6
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
P^{(-\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)
such that

(1)

P^{(-\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)
is analytic in
U_{-\mathrm{i}}\setminus S^{1}
;

(2)

one-sided traces
P^{(-\mathrm{i})}_{\pm}(\lambda)
are continuous on
U_{-\mathrm{i}}\cap S^{1}
and satisfy

(3.2.26)

\displaystyle P^{(-\mathrm{i})}_{+}(\lambda)=P^{(-\mathrm{i})}_{-}(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}1&-(B^{\mathbb{R}}/A)e^{2x\theta(\lambda)}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix};

(3)

it holds for
\lambda\in\partial U_{-\mathrm{i}}
that

(3.2.27)

\displaystyle P^{(-\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)=T_{i}^{(-\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)\big(I+\mathcal{O}_{m}\big(x^{-1}\big)\big)\left(\frac{\lambda+\mathrm{i}}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\right)^{m\sigma_{3}},

for
i\in\{1,2\}
, where
T_{i}^{(-\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)=\sigma_{1}T_{i}^{(\mathrm{i})}(-\lambda)\sigma_{1}
.

3.3  Small Norm Problem

Recall that matrices
P^{(\pm\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)
are holomorphic in
U_{\pm\mathrm{i}}
, but
T_{i}^{(\pm\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)
have poles at the centers of the respective disks. Put

(3.3.1)

\displaystyle\tau_{12}=t^{(\mathrm{i})}_{12}(\mathrm{i};x)\quad\text{and}\quad\tau_{21}=t^{(\mathrm{i})}_{21}(\mathrm{i};x).

Observe that
\tau_{12}\tau_{21}=mx^{-1}
by (
3.2.24
) and since
|\phi_{\mathrm{i}}^{\prime}(\mathrm{i})|=1/\sqrt{2}
. Hence, one of these constants is less than one in absolute value when
x>m
. We look for the solution of RHP
3
in the following form:

\displaystyle\hat{Y}(\lambda)=:\hat{Z}(\lambda)\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}T_{i}^{(\pm\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)^{-1}P^{(\pm\mathrm{i})}(\lambda),&\lambda\in U_{\pm\mathrm{i}},\\
\displaystyle\left(\frac{\lambda+\mathrm{i}}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\right)^{m\sigma_{3}},&\text{otherwise},\end{array}\right.

where we take
i=1
if
|\tau_{12}|<1
and
i=2
otherwise. Then,
\hat{Z}(\lambda)
must solve the following meromorphic Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 7
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
\hat{Z}(\lambda)
such that

(1)

\hat{Z}(\lambda)
is analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus(\Gamma_{Z}\cup\{\pm\mathrm{i}\})
, where
\Gamma_{Z}
is depicted on Figure
6
;

(2)

one-sided traces
\hat{Z}_{\pm}(\lambda)
exist a.e. on
\Gamma_{Z}
, belong to
L^{2}(\Gamma_{Z})
, and satisfy

\hat{Z}_{+}(\lambda)=\hat{Z}_{-}(\lambda)G_{Z}(\lambda),

where the jump matrices
G_{Z}(\lambda)
are as on Figure
6
;

(3)

it holds that
\hat{Z}(\lambda)=I+\mathcal{O}(1/\lambda)
as
\lambda\to\infty
;

(4)

\hat{Z}(\lambda)
has double poles at
\mathrm{i}
and
-\mathrm{i}
with principal parts such that the products
\hat{Z}(\lambda)T_{i}^{(\pm\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)^{-1}
are analytic in the respective disks
U_{\mathrm{i}}
and
U_{-\mathrm{i}}
.

Figure 6
.
The contour
\Gamma_{Z}
and the jump matrices
G_{Z}(\lambda)
. Note that
G_{\hat{Y}}(\lambda)
is stated on Figure
5
.

We look for the solution of RHP
7
in the form

(3.3.4)

\displaystyle\hat{Z}(\lambda)=\left(I+\frac{\lambda B_{1}}{\lambda^{2}+1}+\frac{C_{1}}{\lambda^{2}+1}\right)\left(I+\frac{\lambda B_{2}}{\lambda^{2}+1}+\frac{C_{2}}{\lambda^{2}+1}\right)Z(\lambda),

where the matrices
B_{k},C_{k}
,
k=1,2
, are independent of
\lambda
and will be specified shortly, while
Z(\lambda)
solves the following small norm Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 8
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
Z(\lambda)
such that

(1)

Z(\lambda)
is analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{Z}
;

(2)

one-sided traces
Z_{\pm}(\lambda)
exist a.e. on
\Gamma_{Z}
, belong to
L^{2}(\Gamma_{Z})
, and satisfy

Z_{+}(\lambda)=Z_{-}(\lambda)G_{Z}(\lambda);

(3)

it holds that
Z(\lambda)=I+\mathcal{O}(1/\lambda)
as
\lambda\to\infty
.

Let us now show that RHP
8
is indeed a small norm problem and therefore has a solution for all sufficiently large
x
by the small norm theorem, see (
2.3.7
). Since asymptotic formulae in RHP
5
(3) and RHP
6
(3) hold for each
i\in\{1,2\}
, our choice of
i
does not affect the forthcoming computations (however, this choice will be important for the construction of the rational prefactor in (
3.3.4
)).

We start by specifying precisely the disks
U_{\pm\mathrm{i}}
. We take their radius
\varrho
to be
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
. Then these disks intersect
S^{1}
at points with imaginary parts
\pm\tfrac{3}{4}
. That is,

(3.3.5)

\displaystyle|\operatorname{Im}(\lambda)|\leq\tfrac{3}{4},\quad\lambda\in S^{1}\setminus(U_{\mathrm{i}}\cup U_{-\mathrm{i}})

(this particular choice of
\varrho
is motivated by the simplicity of the above bound and nothing else). It further follows from the maximum modulus principle and the triangle inequality that

(3.3.6)

\max_{\lambda\in\Gamma_{Z}}\left|\frac{\lambda+\mathrm{i}}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\right|^{\pm 1}\leq\max_{\lambda\in\partial U_{\mathrm{i}}}\left|\frac{\lambda+\mathrm{i}}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\right|\leq 1+\frac{2}{\varrho}<e^{\frac{3}{2}}.

Now we are ready to estimate the deviation of the jump matrix
G_{Z}(\lambda)
from the identity matrix
I
. Recall (
2.2.2
). The form of
G_{\hat{Y}}(\lambda)
on the imaginary axis, see Figure
5
, and (
3.3.6
) yield that

(3.3.7)

\|G_{Z}-I\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{Z}\cap\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R})}\leq|s^{\mathbb{R}}|e^{3m-x}.

Similarly, using (
2.3.5
), we obtain that

(3.3.8)

\|G_{Z}-I\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{Z}\cap\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R})}\leq C_{1}|s^{\mathbb{R}}|x^{-\frac{1}{4}}e^{3m-x}

for some absolute constant
C_{1}
(the constants
C_{i}
appearing here are not related to identically labeled constants from the proof of Theorem
1.1
). Meanwhile, on the unit circle, it holds that

\displaystyle G_{Z}(\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}1+\mathcal{O}(|A-1|)&-x^{m+\nu}e^{-x(1+\operatorname{Im}(\lambda))}\big(\frac{\lambda+\mathrm{i}}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\big)^{2m}\\
-x^{m+\nu}e^{-x(1-\operatorname{Im}(\lambda))}\big(\frac{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}{\lambda+\mathrm{i}}\big)^{2m}&1+\mathcal{O}(|A-1|)\end{pmatrix},

where we used (
3.2.13
) and one needs to recall that
2\theta(\lambda)=-\operatorname{Im}(\lambda)
for
\lambda\in S^{1}
. Thus, we get from (
2.2.4
), (
3.3.5
), and (
3.3.6
) that

(3.3.9)

\|G_{Z}-I\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{Z}\cap S^{1})}\leq C_{2}|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}x^{m+\nu}e^{3m-\frac{1}{4}x}

for some absolute constant
C_{2}
. Next, it readily follows from RHP
5
(3) that

G_{Z}(\lambda)=\left(I+\mathcal{O}_{m}\big(x^{-1}\big)\right)\left(\frac{\lambda+\mathrm{i}}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\right)^{m\sigma_{3}}G_{\hat{Y}}(\lambda)\left(\frac{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}{\lambda+\mathrm{i}}\right)^{m\sigma_{3}},\quad\lambda\in\partial U_{\mathrm{i}}.

This means that

G_{Z}(\lambda)=\left(I+\mathcal{O}_{m}\big(x^{-1}\big)\right)\begin{pmatrix}A&(\mathrm{i}s^{\mathbb{R}}A+B^{\mathbb{R}})e^{2x\theta(\lambda)}\big(\frac{\lambda+\mathrm{i}}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\big)^{2m}\\
0&1/A\end{pmatrix}

when
\lambda-\mathrm{i}
belongs to the second quadrant (we need to replace
\mathrm{i}s^{\mathbb{R}}A
by 0 when
\lambda-\mathrm{i}
is in the first quadrant;
A
by 1 and
B^{\mathbb{R}}
by 0 when
\lambda-\mathrm{i}
is in the third quadrant, and simply set the last matrix to be
I
when
\lambda-\mathrm{i}
belongs to the fourth quadrant). We have that

2\operatorname{Re}(\theta(\lambda))=-\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{Im}(\lambda)\big(1+|\lambda|^{-2}\big)<-\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{Im}(\lambda)<-\tfrac{1}{2}(1-\varrho)<-\tfrac{1}{7}

when
\lambda\in\partial U_{\mathrm{i}}
. Therefore, we get from (
2.2.4
) that

\begin{pmatrix}A&(\mathrm{i}s^{\mathbb{R}}A+B^{\mathbb{R}})e^{2x\theta(\lambda)}\big(\frac{\lambda+\mathrm{i}}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\big)^{2m}\\
0&1/A\end{pmatrix}=I+\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{O}\big(|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}x^{m+\nu}e^{-x}\big)&\mathcal{O}\big(|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}e^{3m-\frac{1}{7}x}\big)\\
0&\mathcal{O}\big(|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}x^{m+\nu}e^{-x}\big)\end{pmatrix},

where the constants in
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
above are absolute. Now, one can readily check that for sufficiently large constant
D_{2}(m)
it holds that

\frac{1}{x}\geq|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}e^{3m-\frac{1}{7}x},\\
x^{m+\nu}e^{-x},\end{array}\right.

whenever
x\geq D_{2}(m)+8\ln|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}
. This estimate readily yields that

(3.3.10)

\|G_{Z}-I\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial U_{\mathrm{i}})}\leq C_{3}x^{-1}

for some constant
C_{3}=C_{3}(m)
. Clearly, an identical estimate holds on
\partial U_{-\mathrm{i}}
. By using the same reasoning that lead to (
3.3.10
) in (
3.3.7
), (
3.3.8
), (
3.3.9
) and gathering these four estimates together, we deduce that

(3.3.11)

\displaystyle\|G_{Z}-I\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{Z})\cap L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{Z})}\leq C_{4}x^{-1}

for some constant
C_{4}=C_{4}(m)
and all
x\geq D_{2}(m)+8\ln|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}
. Therefore, as in (
2.3.7
), the solution of RHP
8
exists for such
x
and has the form

(3.3.12)

\displaystyle Z(\lambda)=I+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{Z}}\frac{\rho(\lambda^{\prime})(G_{Z}(\lambda^{\prime})-I)}{\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda}d\lambda^{\prime},

where
\rho(\lambda)=(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma_{Z}})^{-1}(I)
and the operator
\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma_{Z}}
is defined as in (
2.3.8
) only on
\Gamma_{Z}
instead of
\Gamma
and with
G_{Y}(\lambda)
replaced by
G_{Z}(\lambda)
.

For the use in the next subsection, let us observe that

G_{Z}(\lambda)=\sigma_{1}G_{Z}(-\lambda)\sigma_{1},

which can be checked by straightforward computation. Therefore, since
Z(\lambda)
is the unique solution of RHP
8
it must satisfy

(3.3.13)

Z(\lambda)=\sigma_{1}Z(-\lambda)\sigma_{1}\quad\Rightarrow\quad Z(\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}z_{1}(\lambda)&z_{2}(-\lambda)\\
z_{2}(\lambda)&z_{1}(-\lambda)\end{pmatrix}.

3.4  Rational Prefactor

It readily follows from RHP
8
that (
3.3.4
) satisfies RHP
7
(1-3) with any matrices
B_{k},C_{k}
,
k\in\{1,2\}
. In this subsection we choose
B_{k},C_{k}
so that RHP
7
(4) is fulfilled.

Similarly to (
3.3.13
), if there exists a unique solution of RHP
7
, it must possess the same symmetry. Hence, we will apriori assume that
B_{k}=-\sigma_{1}B_{k}\sigma_{1}
and
C_{k}=\sigma_{1}C_{k}\sigma_{1}
. That is, we shall suppose that

(3.4.1)

\displaystyle B_{k}=\begin{pmatrix}b_{k}&-d_{k}\\
d_{k}&-b_{k}\end{pmatrix},\quad C_{k}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{k}&c_{k}\\
c_{k}&a_{k}\end{pmatrix},\quad k\in\{1,2\}.

Now, we immediately see from (
3.3.13
), (
3.4.1
), and relation
T_{i}^{(-\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)=\sigma_{1}T_{i}^{(\mathrm{i})}(-\lambda)\sigma_{1}
that if we can show that
\hat{Z}(\lambda)T_{i}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)^{-1}
is analytic at
\mathrm{i}
, then
\hat{Z}(\lambda)T_{i}^{(-\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)^{-1}
is automatically analytic at
-\mathrm{i}
. To show analyticity at
\mathrm{i}
, observe first that

(3.4.2)

\hat{Z}(\lambda)T_{i}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)^{-1}=\left(I+\frac{\lambda B_{1}}{\lambda^{2}+1}+\frac{C_{1}}{\lambda^{2}+1}\right)\left(I+\frac{\lambda B_{2}}{\lambda^{2}+1}+\frac{C_{2}}{\lambda^{2}+1}\right)Z(\lambda)\times\{*\},

where
\{*\}
stands for either

\begin{pmatrix}1&\displaystyle-\frac{t_{12}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda;x)}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\displaystyle-\frac{t_{21}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda;x)}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}&1\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{or}\quad\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\displaystyle-\frac{t_{21}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda;x)}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&\displaystyle-\frac{t_{12}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda;x)}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

depending on whether we are considering
T_{1}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)
or
T_{2}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)
. In the former case the following lemma holds.

Lemma 3.1
.

Recall (
3.3.1
). If
4(1-W_{1})^{2}+W_{2}^{2}\neq 0
, where

W_{1}:=\tau_{12}\det\begin{pmatrix}z_{1}(\mathrm{i})&z_{2}(\mathrm{i})\\
z_{1}^{\prime}(\mathrm{i})&z_{2}^{\prime}(\mathrm{i})\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad W_{2}:=\tau_{12}\det\begin{pmatrix}z_{1}(\mathrm{i})&z_{2}(\mathrm{i})\\
z_{2}(\mathrm{i})&z_{1}(\mathrm{i})\end{pmatrix},

then there exist constant matrices
B_{2}
and
C_{2}
as in (
3.4.1
) such that

Q(\lambda):=\left(I+\frac{\lambda B_{2}}{\lambda^{2}+1}+\frac{C_{2}}{\lambda^{2}+1}\right)Z(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}1&\displaystyle-\frac{t_{12}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda;x)}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

is analytic at
\lambda=\mathrm{i}
. Moreover, we have that

(3.4.3)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}b_{2}&=\displaystyle-2\tau_{12}\frac{4(1-W_{1})z_{1}(\mathrm{i})z_{2}(\mathrm{i})+\mathrm{i}W_{2}(z_{1}^{2}(\mathrm{i})+z_{2}^{2}(\mathrm{i}))}{4(1-W_{1})^{2}+W_{2}^{2}},\\
d_{2}&=\displaystyle-4\tau_{12}\frac{(1-W_{1})(z_{1}^{2}(\mathrm{i})+z_{2}^{2}(\mathrm{i}))+\mathrm{i}W_{2}z_{1}(\mathrm{i})z_{2}(\mathrm{i})}{4(1-W_{1})^{2}+W_{2}^{2}},\\
a_{2}&=\displaystyle\frac{-2W_{2}^{2}}{4(1-W_{1})^{2}+W_{2}^{2}},\quad\text{and}\quad c_{2}=\frac{4\mathrm{i}(1-W_{1})W_{2}}{4(1-W_{1})^{2}+W_{2}^{2}}.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

Computing the principal part of the Laurent expansion of
Q(\lambda)
at
\mathrm{i}
and setting it to zero gives a system of equations

(3.4.4)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 0&=x_{1}z_{1}(\mathrm{i})-x_{2}z_{2}(\mathrm{i}),\\
0&=x_{3}z_{1}(\mathrm{i})-x_{4}z_{2}(\mathrm{i}),\\
0&=x_{1}z_{2}(-\mathrm{i})-x_{2}z_{1}(-\mathrm{i})-\tau_{12}\big(z_{1}(\mathrm{i})+x_{1}z_{1}^{\prime}(\mathrm{i})-x_{2}z_{2}^{\prime}(\mathrm{i})+\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}x_{3}z_{2}(\mathrm{i})-\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}x_{4}z_{1}(\mathrm{i})\big),\\
0&=x_{3}z_{2}(-\mathrm{i})-x_{4}z_{1}(-\mathrm{i})-\tau_{12}\big(z_{2}(\mathrm{i})+x_{3}z_{1}^{\prime}(\mathrm{i})-x_{4}z^{\prime}_{2}(\mathrm{i})+\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}x_{1}z_{2}(\mathrm{i})-\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}x_{2}z_{1}(\mathrm{i})\big),\end{aligned}

where

(3.4.5)

\displaystyle x_{1}=\frac{b_{2}-\mathrm{i}a_{2}}{2},\quad x_{2}=\frac{d_{2}+\mathrm{i}c_{2}}{2},\quad x_{3}=\frac{d_{2}-\mathrm{i}c_{2}}{2}\quad x_{4}=\frac{b_{2}+\mathrm{i}a_{2}}{2}.

If
z_{2}(\mathrm{i})\neq 0
, then we can express
x_{2},x_{4}
through
x_{1},x_{3}
, respectively, via the first two equations and plug these expressions into the last two to get

(3.4.6)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}W_{1}-1&\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}W_{2}\\
\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}W_{2}&W_{1}-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{3}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\tau_{12}z_{1}(\mathrm{i})z_{2}(\mathrm{i})\\
\tau_{12}z_{2}^{2}(\mathrm{i})\end{pmatrix}

where we used equality
z_{1}(\mathrm{i})z_{1}(-\mathrm{i})-z_{2}(\mathrm{i})z_{2}(-\mathrm{i})=1
(recall that
\det Z(\lambda)\equiv 1
). The above system is solvable when
(W_{1}-1)^{2}+\tfrac{1}{4}W_{2}^{2}\neq 0
, in which case

(3.4.7)

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle=\tau_{12}z_{2}(\mathrm{i})\frac{(W_{1}-1)z_{1}(\mathrm{i})-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}W_{2}z_{2}(\mathrm{i})}{(1-W_{1})^{2}+\tfrac{1}{4}W_{2}^{2}},\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt

(3.4.8)

\displaystyle x_{2}

\displaystyle=\tau_{12}z_{1}(\mathrm{i})\frac{(W_{1}-1)z_{1}(\mathrm{i})-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}W_{2}z_{2}(\mathrm{i})}{(1-W_{1})^{2}+\tfrac{1}{4}W_{2}^{2}},\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt

(3.4.9)

\displaystyle x_{3}

\displaystyle=\tau_{12}z_{2}(\mathrm{i})\frac{(W_{1}-1)z_{2}(\mathrm{i})-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}W_{2}z_{1}(\mathrm{i})}{(1-W_{1})^{2}+\tfrac{1}{4}W_{2}^{2}},\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt

(3.4.10)

\displaystyle x_{4}

\displaystyle=\tau_{12}z_{1}(\mathrm{i})\frac{(W_{1}-1)z_{2}(\mathrm{i})-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}W_{2}z_{1}(\mathrm{i})}{(1-W_{1})^{2}+\tfrac{1}{4}W_{2}^{2}},

from which expressions for
a_{2},b_{2},c_{2},d_{2}
easily follow. Clearly, the case
z_{1}(\mathrm{i})\neq 0
leads to the same equations. Moreover,
z_{1}(\mathrm{i})
and
z_{2}(\mathrm{i})
cannot be simultaneously zero because
\det Z(\mathrm{i})=1
.
∎

Furthermore, that

(3.4.11)

\sigma_{1}\left(I+\frac{\lambda B_{2}}{\lambda^{2}+1}+\frac{C_{2}}{\lambda^{2}+1}\right)Z(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\displaystyle-\frac{t_{21}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda;x)}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}&1\end{pmatrix}\sigma_{1}=\\
\left(I-\frac{\lambda B_{2}}{\lambda^{2}+1}+\frac{C_{2}}{\lambda^{2}+1}\right)\begin{pmatrix}z_{1}(-\lambda)&z_{2}(\lambda)\\
z_{2}(-\lambda)&z_{1}(\lambda)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&\displaystyle-\frac{t_{21}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda;x)}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

This immediately shows that Lemma
3.1
should be replaced by the following statement if we choose
i=2
in (
3.3
) (of course, matrices
\hat{Z}(\lambda)
and
Z(\lambda)
do depend on the choice of
i
in (
3.3
); however, since all relevant asymptotic formulae are independent of this choice, we are not indicating this dependence explicitly; in particular,
Z(\lambda)
in (
3.4.11
) is
Z_{2}(\lambda)
).

Lemma 3.2
.

If
4(1-W_{1})^{2}+W_{2}^{2}\neq 0
, where

W_{1}:=\tau_{21}\det\begin{pmatrix}z_{1}(-\mathrm{i})&z_{2}(-\mathrm{i})\\
-z_{1}^{\prime}(-\mathrm{i})&-z_{2}^{\prime}(-\mathrm{i})\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad W_{2}:=\tau_{21}\det\begin{pmatrix}z_{1}(-\mathrm{i})&z_{2}(-\mathrm{i})\\
z_{2}(-\mathrm{i})&z_{1}(-\mathrm{i})\end{pmatrix},

then there exist constant matrices
B_{2}
and
C_{2}
as in (
3.4.1
) such that

Q(\lambda):=\left(I+\frac{\lambda B_{2}}{\lambda^{2}+1}+\frac{C_{2}}{\lambda^{2}+1}\right)Z(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\displaystyle-\frac{t_{21}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda;x)}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}&1\end{pmatrix}

is analytic at
\lambda=\mathrm{i}
. Moreover, expressions for
a_{2},c_{2}
are the same as in (
3.4.3
) while expressions for
b_{2},d_{2}
are also the same as in there except
\tau_{12}
needs to be replaced by
-\tau_{21}
.

For future use observe that in both cases we have that

(3.4.12)

\displaystyle 1+a_{2}\pm c_{2}=\frac{2(1-W_{1})\pm\mathrm{i}W_{2}}{2(1-W_{1})\mp\mathrm{i}W_{2}}.

Clearly, the systems of equations defining
B_{1},C_{1}
are essentially identical to the one defining
B_{2},C_{2}
. Examination of the proof of Lemma
3.1
(and therefore of Lemma
3.2
) shows that it did not use symmetry (
3.3.13
). This symmetry was only used to justify ansatz (
3.4.1
). What was important in the proof is that
\det Z(\lambda)\equiv 1
. Now, we have that

(3.4.13)

\displaystyle\det Q(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\det\left(I+\frac{\lambda B_{2}}{\lambda^{2}+1}+\frac{C_{2}}{\lambda^{2}+1}\right)

(3.4.14)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{2a_{2}+\det B_{2}}{\lambda^{2}+1}+\frac{\det C_{2}-\det B_{2}}{(\lambda^{2}+1)^{2}}.

One can readily verify that

-2a_{2}=\det C_{2}=\det B_{2}=\frac{4W_{2}^{2}}{(1-W_{1})^{2}+W_{2}^{2}}.

Thus,
\det Q(\lambda)\equiv 1
as desired and we immediately deduce the following.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\tilde{q}_{1}(\lambda)
and
\tilde{q}_{2}(\lambda)
be the entries of the first column of
\sigma_{1}Q(\lambda)\sigma_{1}
. If
4(1-W_{3})^{2}+W_{4}^{2}\neq 0
, where

W_{3}:=\tau_{21}\det\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{q}_{1}(\mathrm{i})&\tilde{q}_{2}(\mathrm{i})\\
\tilde{q}_{1}^{\prime}(\mathrm{i})&\tilde{q}_{2}^{\prime}(\mathrm{i})\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad W_{4}:=\tau_{21}\det\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{q}_{1}(\mathrm{i})&\tilde{q}_{2}(\mathrm{i})\\
\tilde{q}_{2}(\mathrm{i})&\tilde{q}_{1}(\mathrm{i})\end{pmatrix},

then there exist constant matrices
B_{1}
and
C_{1}
as in (
3.4.1
) such that

\left(I+\frac{\lambda B_{1}}{\lambda^{2}+1}+\frac{C_{1}}{\lambda^{2}+1}\right)Q(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\displaystyle-\frac{t_{21}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda;x)}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}&1\end{pmatrix}

is analytic at
\lambda=\mathrm{i}
. Moreover, we have that

(3.4.15)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}b_{1}&=\displaystyle 2\tau_{21}\frac{4(1-W_{3})\tilde{q}_{1}(\mathrm{i})\tilde{q}_{2}(\mathrm{i})+\mathrm{i}W_{4}(\tilde{q}_{1}^{2}(\mathrm{i})+\tilde{q}_{2}^{2}(\mathrm{i}))}{4(1-W_{3})^{2}+W_{4}^{2}},\\
d_{1}&=\displaystyle 4\tau_{21}\frac{(1-W_{3})(\tilde{q}_{1}^{2}(\mathrm{i})+\tilde{q}_{2}^{2}(\mathrm{i}))+\mathrm{i}W_{4}\tilde{q}_{1}(\mathrm{i})\tilde{q}_{2}(\mathrm{i})}{4(1-W_{3})^{2}+W_{4}^{2}},\\
a_{1}&=\displaystyle\frac{-2W_{4}^{2}}{4(1-W_{3})^{2}+W_{4}^{2}},\quad\text{and}\quad c_{1}=\frac{4\mathrm{i}(1-W_{3})W_{4}}{4(1-W_{3})^{2}+W_{4}^{2}}.\end{array}\right.

Clearly, the previous lemma addresses the case
i=1
in (
3.3
). The case
i=2
is then covered by the following lemma.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
q_{1}(\lambda)
and
q_{2}(\lambda)
be the entries of the first column of
Q(\lambda)
. If
4(1-W_{3})^{2}+W_{4}^{2}\neq 0
, where

W_{3}:=\tau_{12}\det\begin{pmatrix}q_{1}(\mathrm{i})&q_{2}(\mathrm{i})\\
q_{1}^{\prime}(\mathrm{i})&q_{2}^{\prime}(\mathrm{i})\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad W_{4}:=\tau_{12}\det\begin{pmatrix}q_{1}(\mathrm{i})&q_{2}(\mathrm{i})\\
q_{2}(\mathrm{i})&q_{1}(\mathrm{i})\end{pmatrix},

then there exist constant matrices
B_{1}
and
C_{1}
as in (
3.4.1
) such that

\left(I+\frac{\lambda B_{1}}{\lambda^{2}+1}+\frac{C_{1}}{\lambda^{2}+1}\right)Q(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}1&\displaystyle-\frac{t_{12}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda;x)}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

is analytic at
\lambda=\mathrm{i}
. Moreover, expressions for
a_{1},c_{1}
are the same as in (
3.4.15
) while expressions for
b_{1},d_{1}
are also the same as in there except
\tau_{21}
needs to be replaced by
-\tau_{12}
.

Similarly to (
3.4.12
) we also have that

(3.4.16)

\displaystyle 1+a_{1}\pm c_{1}=\frac{2(1-W_{3})\pm\mathrm{i}W_{4}}{2(1-W_{3})\mp\mathrm{i}W_{4}}.

3.5  Asymptotic Analysis

It follows from RHP
3
(3), (
3.3
), and (
3.3.4
) that

P_{0}=\hat{Y}(0)=(-1)^{m}\hat{Z}(0)=(-1)^{m}(I+C_{1})(I+C_{2})Z(0).

That is, it holds by the definition of
P_{0}
in RHP
1
(3) that

(3.5.1)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\cosh\tfrac{u}{2}\\
\sinh\tfrac{u}{2}\end{pmatrix}=(-1)^{m}\begin{pmatrix}(1+a_{1})(1+a_{2})+c_{1}c_{2}&c_{1}(1+a_{2})+c_{2}(1+a_{1})\\
c_{1}(1+a_{2})+c_{2}(1+a_{1})&(1+a_{1})(1+a_{2})+c_{1}c_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}z_{1}(0)\\
z_{2}(0)\end{pmatrix}.

This means that

e^{u}=\frac{\cosh\tfrac{u}{2}+\sinh\tfrac{u}{2}}{\cosh\tfrac{u}{2}-\sinh\tfrac{u}{2}}=\frac{(1+a_{1}+c_{1})(1+a_{2}+c_{2})(z_{1}(0)+z_{2}(0))}{(1+a_{1}-c_{1})(1+a_{2}-c_{2})(z_{1}(0)-z_{2}(0))}.

Respectively, we deduce from (
3.4.12
) and (
3.4.16
) that

(3.5.2)

\displaystyle e^{u}=\frac{(2(1-W_{1})+\mathrm{i}W_{2})^{2}(2(1-W_{3})+\mathrm{i}W_{4})^{2}(z_{1}(0)+z_{2}(0))}{(2(1-W_{1})-\mathrm{i}W_{2})^{2}(2(1-W_{3})-\mathrm{i}W_{4})^{2}(z_{1}(0)-z_{2}(0))}.

Exactly as in (
2.3.10
), (
3.3.11
) yields that

\|\rho-I\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{Z})}\leq C_{5}x^{-1}

for some constant
C_{5}=C_{5}(m)
. Thus, similarly to (
2.3.12
), (
3.3.12
) gives

Z(\pm\mathrm{i})=I+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{Z}}\frac{G_{Z}(\lambda^{\prime})-I}{\lambda^{\prime}\mp\mathrm{i}}d\lambda^{\prime}+\mathcal{O}_{m}\big(x^{-1}\big)=I+\mathcal{O}_{m}\big(x^{-1}\big),

where we used (
3.3.11
) and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality twice. That is,

(3.5.3)

\displaystyle\big(z_{1}(\lambda)-1\big)^{(k)}_{|\lambda=\pm\mathrm{i}},\big(z_{2}(\lambda)\big)^{(k)}_{|\lambda=\pm\mathrm{i}}=\mathcal{O}_{m}\big(x^{-1}\big)

in the notation of (
3.3.13
) for any
k\geq 0
, where we need to use the Cauchy integral formula for derivatives when
k>0
. Recall also that the jump matrix
G_{Z}(\lambda)
around the origin is simply
G_{Y}(\lambda)
conjugated by a bounded function. Hence, similarly to (
2.3.12
), we can conclude that

(3.5.4)

\displaystyle Z(0)=I+\mathcal{O}_{m}\big(x^{-1}\big)\quad\Rightarrow\quad z_{1}(0)\pm z_{2}(0)=1+\mathcal{O}_{m}\big(x^{-1}\big).

Next, recall that
\tau_{12}\tau_{21}=mx^{-1}
and that we must have
x>m
by (
3.3.8
) and (
3.3.9
) in order for the norm (
3.3.11
) to be small. Hence, either
|\tau_{12}|<1
or
|\tau_{21}|<1
and this is what determined the choice of the index
i
in (
3.3
). Hence, it follows from (
3.5.3
) that

(3.5.5)

\displaystyle W_{1}=\mathcal{O}_{m}\big(x^{-1}\big)\quad\text{and}\quad W_{2}=\tau+\mathcal{O}_{m}\big(x^{-1}\big),

see Lemmas
3.1
and
3.2
, where
\tau
is either
\tau_{12}
or
\tau_{21}
depending on which quantity has the smallest modulus. In particular, it holds that

(3.5.6)

\displaystyle 2(1-W_{1})\pm\mathrm{i}W_{2}=2\pm\mathrm{i}\tau+\mathcal{O}_{m}(x^{-1})

and this quantity is at least
\frac{1}{2}
in absolute value for all
x\geq D_{2}(m)+8\ln|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}
for an appropriately chosen
D_{2}(m)
. Respectively, it follows from Lemmas
3.1
and
3.2
, as well as (
3.5.5
) that

(3.5.7)

\displaystyle a_{2},b_{2},c_{2},d_{2}=\mathcal{O}(1).

Let now
Q(\lambda)
be as in Lemma
3.1
or Lemma
3.2
. Similarly, to the proof of those lemmas, we can write down explicit expressions for
