\tilde{q}_{1}(\mathrm{i})
,
\tilde{q}_{2}(\mathrm{i})
,
q_{1}(\mathrm{i})
and
q_{2}(\mathrm{i})
. These expressions will involve
a_{2},b_{2},c_{2},d_{2}
and the values of the derivatives of
z_{i}(\lambda)
,
i\in\{1,2\}
, and
t^{(\mathrm{i})}_{12}(\lambda;x)
or
t^{(\mathrm{i})}_{21}(\lambda;x)
at
\pm\mathrm{i}
. Hence,

q_{1}(\mathrm{i})-1,q_{2}(\mathrm{i})=\mathcal{O}_{m}\big(x^{-1}\big)\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{q}_{1}(\mathrm{i})-1,\tilde{q}_{2}(\mathrm{i})=\mathcal{O}_{m}\big(x^{-1}\big)

by (
3.5.3
). Thus, similarly to (
3.5.6
) and using the notation of Lemmas
3.3
and
3.4
, we get that

(3.5.8)

\displaystyle 2(1-W_{3})\pm\mathrm{i}W_{4}=2\pm\mathrm{i}\tau^{*}+\mathcal{O}_{m}\big(x^{-1}\big),

where
\{\tau,\tau^{*}\}=\{\tau_{12},\tau_{21}\}
. By plugging the estimates (
3.5.6
) and (
3.5.8
) into (
3.5.2
) and using (
3.5.4
), we obtain that

u(x)=2\ln\left(\frac{(2+\mathrm{i}\tau_{12})(2+\mathrm{i}\tau_{21})+\mathcal{O}_{m}(x^{-1})}{(2-\mathrm{i}\tau_{12})(2-\mathrm{i}\tau_{21})+\mathcal{O}_{m}(x^{-1})}\right)

for all
x\geq D_{2}(m)+8\ln|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}
. To deduce the desires asymptotics (
1.0.11
) it only remains to recall (
2.1.5
) and that
\tau_{12}\tau_{21}=mx^{-1}
.

4 Proof of Theorem
1.3

The proof of Theorem
1.3
is the same as the proof of Theorem
1.2
. The only difference is that we are able to do a deeper analysis of the local parametrices
P^{(\pm\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)
introduced in Section
3.2
when
m=0
. In particular, our starting point for this proof is RHP
3
. Recall also that in this section

B^{\mathbb{R}}=e^{-\varkappa x}=x^{\nu}e^{-x},\quad|\nu|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}.

4.1  Local Parametrices

Recall (
3.2.1
)–(
3.2.4
). For each
n\geq 1
we set

(4.1.1)

\mathcal{H}_{0,n}(z):=-\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\pi}}\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\frac{(1/2)_{m}}{z^{2m+1}}.

It readily follows from (
3.2.4
) that

(\mathcal{H}_{0}-\mathcal{H}_{0,n})(z)=\mathcal{O}_{n}\big(z^{-2n-1}\big)\quad\text{as}\quad z\to\infty.

Observe also that this difference is holomorphic in
\mathbb{C}^{*}
with a pole of order
2n-1
at the origin. Recall (
3.2.11
). We now replace (
3.2.14
) by

(4.1.2)

\displaystyle P_{n}^{(\mathrm{i})}(\lambda):=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\displaystyle-\frac{x^{\nu}}{A}(\mathcal{H}_{0}-\mathcal{H}_{0,n})(\sqrt{x}\phi_{\mathrm{i}}(\lambda))&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\lambda\in U_{\mathrm{i}},

and, similarly to (
3.2.25
), set

(4.1.3)

\displaystyle P_{n}^{(-\mathrm{i})}(\lambda):=\sigma_{1}P_{n}^{(\mathrm{i})}(-\lambda)\sigma_{1},\quad\lambda\in U_{-\mathrm{i}}.

RHP
5
and RHP
6
are now replaced by the following Riemann-Hilbert problems.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 9
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
P_{n}^{(\pm\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)
such that

(1)

P_{n}^{(\pm\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)
is analytic in
U_{\pm\mathrm{i}}\setminus S^{1}
and
P_{n}^{(\pm\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}\big((\lambda\mp\mathrm{i})^{-2n+1}\big)
as
\lambda\to\pm\mathrm{i}
;

(2)

one-sided traces
P^{(\pm\mathrm{i})}_{n\pm}(\lambda)
are continuous on
U_{\pm\mathrm{i}}\cap(S^{1}\setminus\{\pm\mathrm{i}\})
and satisfy RHP
5
,
6
(2);

(3)

it holds uniformly on
\partial U_{\pm\mathrm{i}}
that
\displaystyle P_{n}^{(\pm\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)=I+\mathcal{O}_{n}\big(x^{\nu-n-\frac{1}{2}}\big)
.

The reason we improve RHP
5
,
6
(3) to RHP
9
(3) in such a simple fashion is the triangular structure of
He_{0}(z)
. It is theoretically possible to replace matrices
T_{i}^{(\pm\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)
in RHP
5
,
6
(3) by their proper modifications to achieve a better error rate than
x^{-1}
, but practically, we found the required computations to be too complicated except in the above case
m=0
.

4.2  Small Norm Problem

Similarly to (
3.3
), we look for the solution of RHP
3
in the form

\displaystyle\hat{Y}(\lambda)=\hat{Z}_{n}(\lambda)\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}P_{n}^{(\pm\mathrm{i})}(\lambda),&\lambda\in U_{\pm\mathrm{i}},\\
I,&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Then,
\hat{Z}_{n}(\lambda)
must solve the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Figure 7
.
The contour
\Gamma_{Z}
and the jump matrices
G_{\hat{Z}_{n}}(\lambda)
for RHP
11
. Note that
G_{\hat{Y}}(\lambda)
is stated on Figure
5
.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 10
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
\hat{Z}_{n}(\lambda)
such that

(1)

\hat{Z}_{n}(\lambda)
is analytic for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(\Gamma_{Z}\cup\{\pm\mathrm{i}\})
, where
\Gamma_{Z}
is depicted on Figure
7
;

(2)

one-sided traces
\hat{Z}_{n\pm}(\lambda)
exist a.e. on
\Gamma_{Z}
, belong to
L^{2}(\Gamma_{Z})
, and satisfy

\hat{Z}_{n+}(\lambda)=\hat{Z}_{n-}(\lambda)G_{\hat{Z}_{n}}(\lambda),

where the jump matrices
G_{\hat{Z}_{n}}(\lambda)
are as on Figure
7
;

(3)

it holds that
\hat{Z}_{n}(\lambda)=I+\mathcal{O}(1/\lambda)
as
\lambda\to\infty
;

(4)

\hat{Z}_{n}(\lambda)
has poles of order
2n-1
at
\pm\mathrm{i}
with principal parts such that the products
\hat{Z}_{n}(\lambda)P_{n}^{(\pm\mathrm{i})}(\lambda)
are analytic in the respective disks
U_{\pm\mathrm{i}}
.

We look for
\hat{Z}_{n}(\lambda)
in the following form:

(4.2.3)

\hat{Z}_{n}(\lambda)=B_{n}(\lambda)Z_{n}(\lambda)D^{1-2n}(\lambda),\quad D(\lambda):=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda-\mathrm{i}&0\\
0&\lambda+\mathrm{i}\end{pmatrix},

where
B_{n}(\lambda)
is a matrix polynomial of degree
2n-1
chosen so that
B_{n}(\lambda)=D^{2n-1}(\lambda)+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2n-2})
as
\lambda\to\infty
and RHP
10
(4) is while
Z_{n}(\lambda)
must solve the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 11
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
Z_{n}(\lambda)
such that

(1)

Z_{n}(\lambda)
is analytic for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{Z}
;

(2)

one-sided traces
Z_{n\pm}(\lambda)
exist a.e. on
\Gamma_{Z}
, belong to
L^{2}(\Gamma_{Z})
, and satisfy

Z_{n+}(\lambda)=Z_{n-}(\lambda)D^{1-2n}(\lambda)G_{\hat{Z}_{n}}(\lambda)D^{2n-1}(\lambda);

(3)

it holds that
Z_{n}(\lambda)=I+\mathcal{O}(1/\lambda)
as
\lambda\to\infty
.

As in the case of RHP
8
, we show that RHP
11
is a small norm problem and therefore has a solution for all sufficiently large
x
by the small norm theorem. To this end, notice that

(4.2.4)

\displaystyle G_{Z_{n}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=D^{1-2n}(\lambda)G_{\hat{Z}_{n}}(\lambda)D^{2n-1}(\lambda)

(4.2.5)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\lambda+\mathrm{i}}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\right)^{(n-\frac{1}{2})\sigma_{3}}G_{\hat{Z}_{n}}(\lambda)\left(\frac{\lambda+\mathrm{i}}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\right)^{-(n-\frac{1}{2})\sigma_{3}}.

Hence, the jump matrices
G_{Z}(\lambda)
and
G_{Z_{n}}(\lambda)
are the same on
(\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}\cup S^{1})\cap\Gamma_{Z}
upon replacing
m
by
n-\tfrac{1}{2}
, see Figure
6
. Therefore, estimates (
3.3.7
), (
3.3.8
), and (
3.3.9
) remains valid upon replacing
m
by
n-\tfrac{1}{2}
in the exponentials and changing
x^{m+\nu}
to
x^{\nu}
. Moreover, we can repeat all the steps leading to (
3.3.10
) to get that

\|G_{Z_{n}}-I\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial U_{\pm\mathrm{i}})}\leq cx^{\nu-n-\frac{1}{2}},

for some constant
c=c(n)
. Hence, as in (
3.3.11
), we see that the above estimate holds for
L^{2}(\Gamma_{Z})\cap L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{Z})
-norm as well, perhaps with a modified constant
c
. Therefore, the solution of RHP
11
exists for all
x\geq D_{3}(n)+8\ln|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}
and has the form as in (
3.3.12
) with
G_{Z}(\lambda^{\prime})
replaced by
G_{Z_{n}}(\lambda^{\prime})
. Finally, as in the case of (
3.3.13
), one can readily verify that

(4.2.6)

\displaystyle Z_{n}(\lambda)=\sigma_{1}Z_{n}(-\lambda)\sigma_{1}\quad\Rightarrow\quad Z_{n}(\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}z_{n,1}(\lambda)&z_{n,2}(-\lambda)\\
z_{n,2}(\lambda)&z_{n,1}(-\lambda)\end{pmatrix}.

4.3  Polynomial Prefactor

In this subsection, we construct a polynomial matrix function
B_{n}(\lambda)
such that
\hat{Z}_{n}(\lambda)
given by (
4.2.3
) solves RHP
10
. Clearly, RHP
10
(1,2) are immediately satisfied. RHP
10
(3) follows from our requirement that
B_{n}(\lambda)=D^{2n-1}(\lambda)+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2n-2})
as
\lambda\to\infty
. Hence, we only need to satisfy RHP
10
(4). It can be rewritten as

(4.3.1)

B_{n}(\lambda)Z_{n}(\lambda)D^{1-2n}(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\displaystyle\frac{x^{\nu}}{A}\mathcal{H}_{0,n}(\sqrt{x}\phi_{\mathrm{i}}(\lambda))&1\end{pmatrix}

must be analytic in
U_{\mathrm{i}}
and

(4.3.2)

B_{n}(\lambda)Z_{n}(\lambda)D^{1-2n}(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}1&\displaystyle\frac{x^{\nu}}{A}\mathcal{H}_{0,n}(\sqrt{x}\phi_{-\mathrm{i}}(\lambda))\\
\displaystyle 0&1\end{pmatrix}

must be analytic in
U_{-\mathrm{i}}
. Similarly to Section
3.4
, we assume that

(4.3.3)

\displaystyle B_{n}(\lambda)=-\sigma_{1}B_{n}(-\lambda)\sigma_{1}

and observe that (
4.3.2
) is automatically satisfied when (
4.3.1
) is due to (
4.2.6
).

Since
B_{n}(\lambda)
is a matrix polynomial of degree
2n-1
and
B_{n}(\lambda)=D^{2n-1}(\lambda)+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2n-2})
as
\lambda\to\infty
, it admits the following representation in terms of
D(\lambda)
:

(4.3.4)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} B_{n}(\lambda)&=D^{2n-1}(\lambda)+\sum_{m=1}^{2n-1}B_{n,m}D^{2n-1-m}(\lambda)\\
&=\left(I+\sum_{m=1}^{2n-1}B_{n,m}D^{-m}(\lambda)\right)D^{2n-1}(\lambda),\end{aligned}

where the coefficient matrices
B_{n,m}
are constant in
\lambda
and, due to (
4.3.3
), satisfy

(4.3.5)

\displaystyle B_{n,m}=(-1)^{m}\sigma_{1}B_{n,m}\sigma_{1}\quad\Rightarrow\quad B_{n,m}=\begin{pmatrix}b_{n,m}&(-1)^{m}d_{n,m}\\
d_{n,m}&(-1)^{m}b_{n,m}\end{pmatrix}.

Recalling (
4.2.6
), we can readily compute that the analyticity of the
(1,1)
-entry of the product in (
4.3.1
) is equivalent to the equality of the principal parts of

(4.3.6)

\left(\sum_{m=1}^{2n-1}\frac{b_{n,m}}{(\lambda-\mathrm{i})^{m}}\right)\left(z_{n,1}(\lambda)+z_{n,2}(-\lambda)\left(\frac{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}{\lambda+\mathrm{i}}\right)^{2n-1}\frac{x^{\nu}}{A}\mathcal{H}_{0,n}(\sqrt{x}\phi_{\mathrm{i}}(\lambda))\right)

and

(4.3.7)

\left(\sum_{m=1}^{2n-1}\frac{(-1)^{m}d_{n,m}}{(\lambda+\mathrm{i})^{m}}\right)\left(-z_{n,1}(-\lambda)\frac{x^{\nu}}{A}\mathcal{H}_{0,n}(\sqrt{x}\phi_{\mathrm{i}}(\lambda))-\left(\frac{\lambda+\mathrm{i}}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\right)^{2n-1}z_{n,2}(\lambda)\right).

Let
T_{n}
be the following upper triangular Toeplitz matrix:

(4.3.8)

\displaystyle T_{n}:=[t_{n,j-i}]_{i,j=1}^{2n-1}

with
i
being the row index and
j
being the column one, where
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}t_{n,m}(\lambda-\mathrm{i})^{m}
is the Taylor expansion of the second factor in (
4.3.6
) and
t_{n,m}=0
if
m<0
.
Write
\vec{b}_{n}=(b_{n,1},\ldots,b_{n,2n-1})^{\mathsf{T}}
. Then, the principal part of (
4.3.6
) can be written as

\sum_{m=1}^{2n-1}\frac{(T_{n}\vec{b}_{n})_{m}}{(\lambda-\mathrm{i})^{m}},

where
(T_{n}\vec{b}_{n})_{m}
is the
m
-th entry of the vector
T_{n}\vec{b}_{n}
. Moreover,

\sum_{m=1}^{2n-1}\frac{(-1)^{m}d_{n,m}}{(\lambda+\mathrm{i})^{m}}=\sum_{k=1}^{2n-1}(L_{n}\vec{d}_{n})_{k}(\lambda-\mathrm{i})^{k-1}+\mathcal{O}\big((\lambda-\mathrm{i})^{2n-1}\big),

where we set
\vec{d}_{n}=(d_{n,1},\ldots,d_{n,2n-1})^{\mathsf{T}}
and
L_{n}
was defined in (
1.0.15
). Further, let
H_{n}
be the following triangular Hankel matrix:

(4.3.9)

\displaystyle H_{n}:=[h_{n,i+j-1}]_{i,j=1}^{2n-1}

with
i
being the row index and
j
being the column one, where
\sum_{m=1}^{2n-1}h_{n,m}(\lambda-\mathrm{i})^{-m}
is the principal part of the second factor in (
4.3.7
) and
h_{n,m}=0
if
m\geq 2n
. Therefore, the principal part of (
4.3.7
) is given by

\sum_{m=1}^{2n-1}\frac{(H_{n}L_{n}\vec{d}_{n})_{m}}{(\lambda-\mathrm{i})^{m}}.

That is, we arrive at the equation
T_{n}\vec{b}_{n}=H_{n}L_{n}\vec{d}_{n}
.
Similarly, the analyticity of the
(2,1)
-entry of the product in (
4.3.1
) is equivalent to the equality of the principal parts of

\left(\sum_{m=1}^{2n-1}\frac{d_{n,m}}{(\lambda-\mathrm{i})^{m}}\right)\left(z_{n,1}(\lambda)+z_{n,2}(-\lambda)\left(\frac{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}{\lambda+\mathrm{i}}\right)^{2n-1}\frac{x^{\nu}}{A}\mathcal{H}_{0,n}(\sqrt{x}\phi_{\mathrm{i}}(\lambda))\right)

and

\left(1+\sum_{m=1}^{2n-1}\frac{(-1)^{m}b_{n,m}}{(\lambda+\mathrm{i})^{m}}\right)\left(-z_{n,1}(-\lambda)\frac{x^{\nu}}{A}\mathcal{H}_{0,n}(\sqrt{x}\phi_{\mathrm{i}}(\lambda))-\left(\frac{\lambda+\mathrm{i}}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}}\right)^{2n-1}z_{n,2}(\lambda)\right).

As in the case of
(1,1)
-entry, we can rewrite the above condition as a linear system. Altogether, we arrive at the equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}T_{n}\vec{b}_{n}&=H_{n}L_{n}\vec{d}_{n},\\
T_{n}\vec{d}_{n}&=H_{n}(\vec{e}_{1}+L_{n}\vec{b}_{n}),\end{array}\right.

where
\vec{e}_{1}=(1,0,\ldots,0)^{\mathsf{T}}
. The above linear system can be equivalently rewritten as

(T_{n}-H_{n}L_{n})(\vec{d}_{n}+\vec{b}_{n})=H_{n}\vec{e}_{1}=(T_{n}+H_{n}L_{n})(\vec{d}_{n}-\vec{b}_{n}).

Thus, it is solvable if and only if the determinants of the matrices
T_{n}\pm H_{n}L_{n}
are non-zero. For future use, let us also point out that it follows from (
1.0.15
), (
4.3.8
), (
4.3.9
), and Proposition
B.1
used with
\ell=\mathrm{i}
and
H=\pm H_{n}
that

(4.3.10)

1+\sum_{m=1}^{2n-1}\mathrm{i}^{m}(b_{n,m}\pm d_{n,m})=\frac{\det(T_{n}\pm H_{n}L_{n})}{\det(T_{n}\mp H_{n}L_{n})}.

4.4  Asymptotic Analysis

It follows from RHP
3
(3), (
4.2
), and (
4.2.3
) that

P_{0}=\hat{Y}(0)=\hat{Z}_{n}(0)=\mathrm{i}^{2n-1}B_{n}(0)Z_{n}(0)\sigma_{3}.

We get from the top line of (
4.3.4
) that

\mathrm{i}^{2n-1}B_{n}(0)=\left(I+\sum_{m=1}^{2n-1}\mathrm{i}^{m}B_{n,m}\sigma_{3}^{m}\right)\sigma_{3}.

Thus, it holds by the definition of
P_{0}
in RHP
1
(3) that

(4.4.1)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\cosh\tfrac{u}{2}\\
\sinh\tfrac{u}{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1+\sum_{m=1}^{2n-1}\mathrm{i}^{m}b_{n,m}&\sum_{m=1}^{2n-1}\mathrm{i}^{m}d_{n,m}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
\sum_{m=1}^{2n-1}\mathrm{i}^{m}d_{n,m}&1+\sum_{m=1}^{2n-1}\mathrm{i}^{m}b_{n,m}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}z_{1}(0)\\
-z_{2}(0)\end{pmatrix}.

This means that

e^{u}=\frac{\cosh\tfrac{u}{2}+\sinh\tfrac{u}{2}}{\cosh\tfrac{u}{2}-\sinh\tfrac{u}{2}}=\frac{(1+\sum_{m=1}^{2n-1}\mathrm{i}^{m}(b_{n,m}+d_{n,m}))(z_{1}(0)-z_{2}(0))}{(1+\sum_{m=1}^{2n-1}\mathrm{i}^{m}(b_{n,m}-d_{n,m}))(z_{1}(0)+z_{2}(0))}.

Therefore, we get from (
4.3.10
) that

(4.4.2)

\displaystyle u(x)=2\ln\left(\frac{\det(T_{n}+H_{n}L_{n})}{\det(T_{n}-H_{n}L_{n})}\right)+\ln\left(\frac{z_{1}(0)-z_{2}(0)}{z_{1}(0)+z_{2}(0)}\right).

Now, exactly as in (
3.5.4
) we can reach the conclusion that

z_{1}(0)\pm z_{2}(0)=1+\mathcal{O}_{n}\big(x^{\nu-n-\frac{1}{2}}\big).

Hence, we now need to analyze the asymptotic behavior of the above determinants.

Recall (
1.0.14
). Let us show that

(4.4.3)

\mathcal{H}_{0,n}(\sqrt{x}\phi_{\mathrm{i}}(\lambda))=-\sum_{m=1}^{2n-1}\frac{c_{n,m}}{(\lambda-\mathrm{i})^{m}}+\text{holomorphic part}.

Indeed, it follows from (
3.2.11
) that

\frac{1}{\phi_{\mathrm{i}}^{2m+1}(\lambda)}=\frac{2^{m+\frac{1}{2}}}{(\lambda-\mathrm{i})^{2m+1}}(-\mathrm{i}\lambda)^{m+\frac{1}{2}},

where one needs to recall that we have chosen the branch of the square root such that
(-\mathrm{i}\lambda)^{\frac{1}{2}}_{|\mathrm{i}}=-1
. It holds that

(4.4.4)

\displaystyle\left((-\mathrm{i}\lambda)^{m+\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{(l)}_{|\mathrm{i}}=(-\mathrm{i})^{l}l!\binom{m+\frac{1}{2}}{l}(-\mathrm{i}\lambda)^{m-l+\frac{1}{2}}_{|\mathrm{i}}=-(-\mathrm{i})^{l}l!\binom{m+\frac{1}{2}}{l}.

Therefore, we get from (
4.1.1
) that

\mathcal{H}_{0,n}(\sqrt{x}\phi_{\mathrm{i}}(\lambda))=\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\frac{(1/2)_{m}}{\sqrt{2\pi x}}\left(\frac{2}{x}\right)^{m}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\binom{m+\frac{1}{2}}{l}\frac{(-\mathrm{i})^{l+1}}{(\lambda-\mathrm{i})^{2m+1-l}},

which, after setting
2m+1-l=k
, gives (
1.0.14
).

It now follows from (
2.2.4
) that

(4.4.5)

\frac{x^{\nu}}{A}\mathcal{H}_{0,n}(\sqrt{x}\phi_{\mathrm{i}}(\lambda))=-x^{\nu}\sum_{m=1}^{2n-1}\frac{c_{n,m}(1+\mathcal{O}(x^{\nu}e^{-x}))}{(\lambda-\mathrm{i})^{m}}+\text{hol. part}.

Thus, we see from (
1.0.14
) that the Laurent coefficients above are of order
x^{\nu-\tfrac{1}{2}}
. As
|\nu|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
, they are always bounded.

Recall that
t_{n,m}
are the Taylor coefficients of the second factor in (
4.3.6
) and
h_{n,m}
were defined as the Laurent coefficients of the principal part of the second factor in (
4.3.7
). Similarly to (
3.5.3
), we observe that

(z_{n,1}(\lambda)-1)^{(k)}_{|\lambda=\pm\mathrm{i}},(z_{n,2}(\lambda))^{(k)}_{|\lambda=\pm\mathrm{i}}=\mathcal{O}_{n}\big(x^{\nu-n-\frac{1}{2}}\big)

for any
k\geq 0
. Hence, it holds that

(4.4.6)

\displaystyle t_{n,m}

\displaystyle=\delta_{m,0}+\mathcal{O}_{n}\big(x^{\nu-n-\frac{1}{2}}\big),

(4.4.7)

\displaystyle h_{n,m}

\displaystyle=x^{\nu}c_{n,m}+\mathcal{O}_{n}\big(x^{\nu-n-\frac{1}{2}}\big),

where
\delta_{i,j}
is the Kronecker symbol. Thus, we can write

T_{n}\pm H_{n}L_{n}=I\pm x^{\nu}C_{n}L_{n}+\mathcal{O}_{n}\big(x^{\nu-n-\frac{1}{2}}\big).

Denote the entries of
C_{n}L_{n}
by
m_{n;i,j}
. Then,

(4.4.8)

\displaystyle D_{n}^{\pm}

\displaystyle:=\det(T_{n}\pm H_{n}L_{n})=\det\left(I\pm x^{\nu}C_{n}L_{n}+\mathcal{O}_{n}\big(x^{\nu-n-\frac{1}{2}}\big)\right)

(4.4.9)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma}(-1)^{\sign(\sigma)}\prod_{i=1}^{2n-1}\left(\delta_{i,\sigma(i)}\pm x^{\nu}m_{n;i,\sigma(i)}+\mathcal{O}_{n}\big(x^{\nu-n-\frac{1}{2}}\big)\right),

where the sum is taken over all permutations of
\{1,2,\ldots,2n-1\}
. In the next paragraph we explain that
m_{n;i,j}=\mathcal{O}(x^{-\frac{1}{2}})
. As
\nu\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
, this gives that

\displaystyle D_{n}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma}(-1)^{\sign(\sigma)}\prod_{i=1}^{2n-1}\big(\delta_{i,\sigma(i)}\pm x^{\nu}m_{n;i,\sigma(i)}\big)+\mathcal{O}_{n}\big(x^{\nu-n-\frac{1}{2}}\big)

\displaystyle=\det(I\pm x^{\nu}C_{n}L_{n})+\mathcal{O}_{n}\big(x^{\nu-n-\frac{1}{2}}\big)=P_{n}(\pm x^{\nu})+\mathcal{O}_{n}\big(x^{\nu-n-\frac{1}{2}}\big).

The next paragraph shows that
P_{n}(\pm x^{\nu})=\mathcal{O}_{n}(1)
as
x\to\infty
. This finishes the proof of (
1.0.13
) in view of (
4.4.2
).

It only remains to estimate the size of the coefficients of
P_{n}(t)
. We have that

P_{n}(t)=\sum_{\sigma}(-1)^{\sign(\sigma)}\prod_{i=1}^{2n-1}\big(\delta_{i,\sigma(i)}-tm_{n;i,\sigma(i)}\big).

Hence,
p_{n,l}
, the coefficients next to
t^{l}
, is a sum (with coefficients
\pm 1
) of products of the form
\prod_{i\in S}m_{n;i,\sigma(i)}
, where the cardinality of
S\subset\{1,2,\ldots,2n-1\}
is
l
. One can readily see from (
1.0.14
) that

c_{n,k}=\mathcal{O}\big(x^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)\quad\text{and}\quad c_{n,2k},c_{n,2k+1}=\mathcal{O}\big(x^{-k-\frac{1}{2}}\big)

for
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\}
. Therefore, it follows from the triangular form of
C_{n}
that

m_{n;1,j}=\mathcal{O}\big(x^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)\quad\text{and}\quad m_{n;2k,j},m_{n;2k+1,j}=\mathcal{O}\big(x^{-k-\frac{1}{2}}\big)

independently of
j
. Then, the worst asymptotic order of
\prod_{i\in S}m_{n;i,\sigma(i)}
is obtained by choosing
S=\{1,2,\ldots,l\}
. This, in particular, implies that

p_{n,1}=\mathcal{O}(x^{-\frac{1}{2}}),\quad p_{n,2k}=\mathcal{O}\big(p_{n,2k-1}x^{-k-\frac{1}{2}}\big)\;\text{and}\;p_{n,2k+1}=\mathcal{O}\big(p_{n,2k-1}x^{-2k-1}\big),

from which the desired claim easily follows.

5 Proof of Theorem
1.4

Similarly to (
2.2.1
), we take
\rho=4/x
in RHP
1
and apply the gauge transformation:

(5.0.1)

\displaystyle X(\lambda):=e^{x\ell\sigma_{3}}\hat{\Psi}\left(\frac{4\lambda}{x}\right)\begin{Bmatrix}I,&|\lambda|>1\\
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3},&|\lambda|<1\end{Bmatrix}e^{-xg(\lambda)\sigma_{3}},

for yet to be determined function
g(\lambda)
such that
g(\lambda)=\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\lambda+\ell+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-1})
as
\lambda\to\infty
.

Recall (
1.0.17
), (
1.0.18
), and (
1.0.19
). Throughout the proof, it will be useful for us to use the following constants:

(5.0.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}:=\mathsf{K}^{2}\mathsf{k}^{3}(\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{6},\quad\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{*}:=\mathsf{K}\sqrt{\mathsf{k}}(\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{2},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{**}:=\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathsf{k}\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{-\frac{1}{3}}.

5.1  The
g
-function

In what follows, we denote by
\Gamma(a_{1},a_{2})\subset S^{1}
the subarc of the unit circle
S^{1}
with endpoints
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
oriented from
a_{1}
to
a_{2}
.

5.1.1  Definition of
a

We need two preparatory steps before we can define and discuss properties of the desired function
g(\lambda)
. Here, we make the first one.

Lemma 5.1
.

The function

\widehat{\varkappa}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{2(\cos 2\theta-\cos 2t)}\,d\theta

is continuous and increasing on
(0,\tfrac{\pi}{2})
with range
(0,1)
. In particular, the inverse function is also increasing and continuous with domain
(0,1)
and range
(0,\tfrac{\pi}{2})
. Moreover,

(5.1.1)

\displaystyle\widehat{\varkappa}(t)=E(\sin t)-\cos^{2}t\,K(\sin t)>\frac{\pi}{4}\sin^{2}t,

where
K(\cdot)
and
E(\cdot)
are the complete elliptic integrals of the first and second kind.

Proof.

The first claim is obvious. It is also easy to see that

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\widehat{\varkappa}(t)=0\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{t\to\frac{\pi}{2}^{-}}\widehat{\varkappa}(t)=1.

Hence, the inverse function has the desired properties. Using
\cos 2t=1-2\sin^{2}t
gives

(5.1.2)

\displaystyle\widehat{\varkappa}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{\sin^{2}t-\sin^{2}\theta}\,d\theta.

Thus, if we let
k=\sin t
and
y=\frac{\sin\theta}{\sin t}
, then

\widehat{\varkappa}(t)=k^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\sqrt{1-y^{2}}}{\sqrt{1-k^{2}y^{2}}}\,dy=E(\sin t)-\cos^{2}t\,K(\sin t).

Finally, estimate
[
18
, Equation (19.9.7)]
gives

\widehat{\varkappa}(t)>\frac{\pi}{2}\frac{\sin^{2}t}{\sqrt{4-\sin^{2}t}},

which readily yields the last lower bound of the lemma.
∎

It follows from Lemma
5.1
that, given
\varkappa\in(0,1)
, there exists unique
\alpha\in(0,\tfrac{\pi}{2})
satisfying (
1.0.16
), i.e.,
\varkappa=\widehat{\varkappa}(\alpha)
. Moreover, (
1.0.17
) indeed takes place. Further, set

(5.1.3)

a:=e^{\mathrm{i}\alpha},\quad V(\varkappa):=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\alpha}^{\pi-\alpha}\sqrt{2(\cos 2\alpha-\cos 2\theta)}\,d\theta.

Below, we often simply write
V
for
V(\varkappa)
. Observe that

\displaystyle\pi V

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\alpha}^{\pi/2}\sqrt{2(\cos 2\alpha-\cos 2\theta)}\,d\theta=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\alpha}^{\pi/2}\sqrt{\cos^{2}\alpha-\cos^{2}\theta}\,d\theta

\displaystyle=E(\cos\alpha)-\sin^{2}\alpha\,K(\cos\alpha),

where we used the substitution
y=\frac{\cos\theta}{\cos\alpha}
.

Recall our notation (
1.0.19
). Notice that we can view
\varkappa
as a function of
\mathsf{k}
, which immediately gives
\pi V=\varkappa(\mathsf{k}^{\prime})\in(0,1)
. Moreover, we get from the last estimate of Lemma
5.1
that

(5.1.4)

\displaystyle(\mathsf{k}\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{2}<2\varkappa\pi V.

Furthermore, Legendre’s relation
[
18
, Equation (19.7.1)]
gives us

(5.1.5)

\displaystyle\varkappa\mathsf{K}^{\prime}+\pi V\mathsf{K}=\frac{\pi}{2}\quad\Rightarrow\quad xV=\frac{x-2\kappa\mathsf{K}^{\prime}}{2\mathsf{K}}.

5.1.2  Definition of
\sqrt{f}

Here, we make the second preparatory step. Let
f(\mu)=(\mu^{2}-a^{2})(\mu^{2}-\overline{a}^{2})
and define

(5.1.6)

\displaystyle\sqrt{f(\mu)}=\sqrt{(\mu^{2}-a^{2})(\mu^{2}-\overline{a}^{2})}=\sqrt{\mu^{4}-2\operatorname{Re}(a^{2})\mu^{2}+1}

as a function holomorphic away from

\Delta:=\Gamma(-\overline{a},-a)\cup\Gamma(\overline{a},a)

and such that
\sqrt{f(\mu)}<0
for
\mu\in(-1,1)
and
\sqrt{f(\mu)}>0
for
\mu\in(1,\infty)\cup(-\infty,-1)
, see Figure
8
.

Figure 8
.
The branch cut for
\sqrt{f(\mu)}
and its signs on the axes.

In particular, it holds that

(5.1.7)

\sqrt{f(\mu)}=\mu^{2}\left(1-\operatorname{Re}(a^{2})\mu^{-2}+\mathcal{O}\left(\mu^{-4}\right)\right),\quad\mu\to\infty.

Observe also that
\sqrt{f(\mu)}
is negative on the imaginary axis and

(5.1.8)

\sqrt{f(\mu)}=\sqrt{f(-\mu)}=-\mu^{2}\sqrt{f(\mu^{-1})}=\overline{\sqrt{f(\overline{\mu})}}.

Next, it clearly holds that

f(\mu)=2\mu^{2}\big(\operatorname{Re}(\mu^{2})-\operatorname{Re}(a^{2})\big),\quad\mu\in S^{1}.

Therefore, the trace of
\sqrt{f(\mu)}
on
S^{1}
from within the unit disk satisfies

(5.1.9)

\sqrt{f(\mu)}_{+}=\sqrt{2|\operatorname{Re}(\mu^{2})-\operatorname{Re}(a^{2})|}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\quad}l@{}}-\mu,&\mu\in\Gamma(\overline{a},a),\\
\mathrm{i}\mu,&\mu\in\Gamma(a,-\overline{a}),\\
\mu,&\mu\in\Gamma(-\overline{a},-a),\\
-\mathrm{i}\mu,&\mu\in\Gamma(-a,\overline{a}),\end{array}\right.

where the root on the right-hand side above is arithmetic. Of course, it also holds that

(5.1.10)

\sqrt{f(\mu)}_{-}=\sqrt{f(\mu)}_{+}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\quad}l@{}}-1,&\mu\in\Delta,\\
1,&\mu\in S^{1}\setminus\Delta.\end{array}\right.

5.1.3  Definition of
g

We are now ready to define the
g
-function used in (
5.0.1
). We set

(5.1.11)

\displaystyle g(\lambda):=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\int_{1}^{\lambda}\frac{\sqrt{f(\mu)}}{\mu^{2}}\,d\mu,\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}\setminus S^{1},

where the path of integration does not cross the unit circle and can wind arbitrarily around the origin or infinity because the integrated differential has double poles with zero residues at these two points by (
5.1.7
) and (
5.1.8
).

If we formally put
a=\mathrm{i}
in (
5.1.11
), which corresponds to
\varkappa=1
, we will get that

\displaystyle g(\lambda)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\quad}l@{}}\theta(\lambda),&|\lambda|>1,\\
-\theta(\lambda),&|\lambda|<1.\end{array}\right.

In this case, if we multiply
X(\lambda)
by
\sigma_{3}\sigma_{1}
in the unit disk, then it will be equal to
Y(\lambda)
, see (
2.2.1
) and (
5.0.1
). On the other hand, if we formally put
a=1
, which corresponds to
\varkappa=0
, then we get

g(\lambda)=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\left(\lambda+\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2},

which, up to trivial summand
-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}
, coincides with the function
\varphi(\lambda)
from (
6.0.1
) further below, which is used to transform
\hat{\Psi}(\lambda)
to study the case
\varkappa\to 0
as
x\to\infty
.

It readily follows from (
5.1.8
) that

(5.1.14)

g(\lambda)=g(\lambda^{-1})=-\overline{g(\overline{\lambda})},\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}\setminus S^{1}.

Moreover, we get from (
5.1.7
) that

(5.1.15)

g(\lambda)=\frac{\mathrm{i}\lambda}{4}+\ell+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\frac{\operatorname{Re}(a^{2})}{\lambda}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}\right),\quad\lambda\to\infty

(this also gives the first three terms of the Laurent series at
0
due to (
5.1.14
)), where

\ell=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\int_{1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\sqrt{f(\mu)}}{\mu^{2}}-1\right)d\mu\in\mathrm{i}\big(-\tfrac{1}{2},0\big)

(the last conclusion follows from the fact that
\sqrt{f(\mu)}>0
is a decreasing function of
\operatorname{Re}(a^{2})\in(-1,1)
for each
\mu\in(1,\infty)
). The definition of
g(\lambda)
readily yields

g_{-}(-1)=g(\lambda)+g(-\lambda)=\left(g(\lambda)-\frac{\mathrm{i}\lambda}{4}\right)+\left(g(-\lambda)+\frac{\mathrm{i}\lambda}{4}\right)=2\ell,

where we used (
5.1.15
) and independence from
\lambda
to get the last equality. Notice that the first symmetry in (
5.1.14
) gives that
g_{+}(-1)=g_{-}(-1)
. Thus,

(5.1.16)

g(\lambda)=2\ell-g(-\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}\setminus S^{1}.

5.1.4  Traces of
g
on
S^{1}

In what follows, it will be important for us to know the relations between the boundary values of
g(\lambda)
from both sides of the unit circle. To this end, let

(5.1.17)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}\Pi(\lambda)&:=g_{+}(\lambda)-g_{-}(\lambda),\\
\Omega(\lambda)&:=\mathrm{i}(g_{+}(\lambda)+g_{-}(\lambda)),\end{array}\right.\quad\lambda\in S^{1}.

Recall Lemma
5.1
. Extend
\widehat{\varkappa}(t)
to the interval
(-\tfrac{\pi}{2},\tfrac{\pi}{2})
as an odd function. Then, it readily follows from (
5.1.9
) and (
5.1.10
) that

\Pi(\lambda)=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\int_{1}^{\lambda}\frac{\sqrt{f(\mu)}_{+}}{\mu^{2}}\,d\mu=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\Arg\lambda}\sqrt{2(\cos 2\theta-\cos 2\alpha)}\,d\theta=\widehat{\varkappa}(\Arg\lambda)

for
\lambda\in\Gamma(\overline{a},a)
, where
\Arg(\lambda)\in(-\pi,\pi]
, the root is arithmetic, and we used
\mu=e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}
and
a=e^{\mathrm{i}\alpha}
. In particular,
\Pi(a)=\varkappa
and
\Pi(\overline{a})=-\varkappa
by the very definition (
1.0.16
). Using (
5.1.16
) we conclude that

(5.1.18)

\Pi(\lambda)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\quad}l@{}}\widehat{\varkappa}(\Arg\lambda),&\lambda\in\Gamma(\overline{a},a),\\
\varkappa,&\lambda\in\Gamma(a,-\overline{a}),\\
-\widehat{\varkappa}(\Arg(-\lambda)),&\lambda\in\Gamma(-\overline{a},-a),\\
-\varkappa,&\lambda\in\Gamma(-a,\overline{a}).\end{array}\right.

Observe that
\Pi(\lambda)
is real-valued. Further, let

\widehat{V}(t):=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\alpha}^{t}\sqrt{2(\cos 2\alpha-\cos 2\theta)}\,d\theta,\quad t\in(\alpha,\pi-\alpha).

Clearly,
\widehat{V}(t)
is an increasing function of
t
. Notice that
V=\widehat{V}(\pi-\alpha)
, see (
5.1.3
). We readily get from (
5.1.9
) and (
5.1.10
) that
\Omega(\lambda)=0
on
\Gamma(\overline{a},a)
and that

\Omega(\lambda)=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{a}^{\lambda}\frac{\sqrt{f(\mu)}}{\mu^{2}}\,d\mu=2\pi\widehat{V}(\Arg\lambda)

for
\lambda\in\Gamma(a,-\overline{a})
. Extend
\widehat{V}(t)
to
(\alpha-\pi,-\alpha)
as an even function. Then, it follows from (
5.1.14
) that

(5.1.19)

\Omega(\lambda)=2\pi\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\quad}l@{}}0,&\lambda\in\Gamma(\overline{a},a),\\
V,&\lambda\in\Gamma(-\overline{a},-a),\\
\widehat{V}(\Arg\lambda),&\lambda\in\Gamma(a,-\overline{a})\cup\Gamma(-a,\overline{a}).\end{array}\right.

As in the case of
\Pi(\lambda)
, the function
\Omega(\lambda)
is real-valued.

Relations (
5.1.17
), (
5.1.18
), and (
5.1.19
) show that
2g(\lambda)
maps the unit disk onto the complement of the rectangle with vertices
\varkappa,\varkappa-2\pi\mathrm{i}V,-\varkappa-2\pi\mathrm{i}V,-\varkappa
with the unit circle being mapped into the boundary of this rectangle in one-to-one fashion. It then follows from the Argument Principle that
g(\lambda)
is univalent in the unit disk and therefore in the complement of the closed unit disk as well (recall (
5.1.14
)).

Lemma 5.2
.

It holds that
2\mathrm{i}\ell=\pi V
.

Proof.

It readily follows from (
5.1.15
), (
5.1.17
), (
5.1.18
), and (
5.1.19
) that

\ell=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{S^{1}}g_{-}(\lambda)|d\lambda|=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{S^{1}}(\Pi(\lambda)+\mathrm{i}\Omega(\lambda))|d\lambda|=-\mathrm{i}\alpha V-\mathrm{i}\int_{\alpha}^{\pi-\alpha}\widehat{V}(t)dt.

Exchanging the order of integration gives

(5.1.20)

\displaystyle\int_{\alpha}^{\pi-\alpha}\widehat{V}(t)dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\alpha}^{\pi-\alpha}(\pi-\alpha-\theta)\sqrt{2(\cos 2\alpha-\cos 2\theta)}\,d\theta

(5.1.21)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\alpha}^{\pi-\alpha}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha\right)\sqrt{2(\cos 2\alpha-\cos 2\theta)}\,d\theta=\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha\right)V,

where the second equality follows by symmetry (integrals of functions that are odd with respect to the midpoint of the interval of integration are zero). The claim now follows from the above two equalities.
∎

5.1.5  Estimates of
\operatorname{Re}(g)

We close this subsection with one more observation that will become useful later.

Lemma 5.3
.

It holds that

2\operatorname{Re}(g(\lambda))\geq\varkappa+s({\Arg\lambda})\sinh^{2}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\ln|\lambda|\big),\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}|\lambda|\leq 1,&\Arg(\lambda)\in[\alpha,\pi-\alpha],\\
|\lambda|\geq 1,&\Arg(\lambda)\in[-\pi+\alpha,-\alpha],\end{array}\right.

and

2\operatorname{Re}(g(\lambda))\leq-\varkappa-s({\Arg\lambda})\sinh^{2}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\ln|\lambda|\big),\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}|\lambda|\geq 1,&\Arg(\lambda)\in[\alpha,\pi-\alpha],\\
|\lambda|\leq 1,&\Arg(\lambda)\in[-\pi+\alpha,-\alpha],\end{array}\right.

where

s(\theta):=\frac{1}{2^{1/4}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\quad}l@{}}\sin(2\theta),&|\theta|\in[0,\tfrac{\pi}{4}]\cup[\tfrac{3\pi}{4},\pi],\\
1,&|\theta|\in[\tfrac{\pi}{4},\tfrac{3\pi}{4}].\end{array}\right.

Proof.

Let
\lambda=|\lambda|e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}
, where
|\lambda|\leq 1
and
\theta\in[\alpha,\tfrac{\pi}{2}]
. It follows from (
5.1.17
) and (
5.1.18
) that

2\operatorname{Re}(g(\lambda))=\varkappa+\operatorname{Re}\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\int_{e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}}^{\lambda}\frac{\sqrt{f(\mu)}}{\mu^{2}}d\mu\right).

When
\mu=te^{\mathrm{i}\theta}
with
0<t<1
, we have that

\displaystyle f(\mu)

\displaystyle=e^{2\mathrm{i}\theta}t^{2}\big(e^{2\mathrm{i}\theta}t^{2}-2\operatorname{Re}\left(e^{2\mathrm{i}\alpha}\right)+e^{-2\mathrm{i}\theta}t^{-2}\big)

\displaystyle=e^{2\mathrm{i}\theta}t^{2}\big(\cos(2\theta)(t^{2}+t^{-2})-2\cos(2\alpha)-\mathrm{i}\sin(2\theta)(t^{-2}-t^{2})\big).

Notice that the imaginary part of the term in parentheses is negative. Recall also that
\sqrt{f(\mu)}
is holomorphic in the unit disk and is negative on the imaginary axis, i.e., when
\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{2}
. Hence,

\mu^{-1}\sqrt{f(\mu)}=-\sqrt{\cos(2\theta)(t^{2}+t^{-2})-2\cos(2\alpha)-\mathrm{i}\sin(2\theta)(t^{-2}-t^{2})},

where the branch of the square root is now principal. In particular, the above value lies in the second quadrant and therefore its imaginary part is non-negative. Thus,

2\operatorname{Re}(g(\lambda))=\varkappa-\operatorname{Im}\left(\int_{|\lambda|}^{1}\sqrt{\cos(2\theta)(t^{2}+t^{-2})-2\cos(2\alpha)-\mathrm{i}\sin(2\theta)(t^{-2}-t^{2})}\frac{dt}{2t}\right).

Observe that if
v,q>0
, then

-\sqrt{u-\mathrm{i}v}=p+\mathrm{i}q\quad\Rightarrow\quad 2q^{2}=\sqrt{u^{2}+v^{2}}-u\geq\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}v-u,\\
\displaystyle\frac{v^{2}}{2\sqrt{u^{2}+v^{2}}},\end{array}\right.

where all the square roots are principal. Let

u=\cos(2\theta)(t^{2}+t^{-2})-2\cos(2\alpha)\quad\text{and}\quad v=\sin(2\theta)(t^{-2}-t^{2}).

When
\theta\in[\tfrac{\pi}{4},\tfrac{\pi}{2}]
, in which case
\cos(2\theta)\leq\min\{\cos(2\alpha),0\}
, we have that

\displaystyle 2q^{2}

\displaystyle\geq v-u=\sin(2\theta)(t^{-2}-t^{2})-\cos(2\theta)(t^{2}+t^{-2})+2\cos(2\alpha)

\displaystyle\geq(\sin(2\theta)-\cos(2\theta))(t^{-1}-t)^{2}+2(\cos(2\alpha)-\cos(2\theta))\geq(t^{-1}-t)^{2}

for
t\in[0,1]
. On the other hand, when
\theta\in[\alpha,\tfrac{\pi}{4}]
(if this set is non-empty), we have

u^{2}+v^{2}\leq\big(t^{2}+t^{-2}+2\big)^{2}+\big(t^{-2}-t^{2}\big)^{2}\leq 2\big(t+t^{-1}\big)^{4}

and therefore

2q^{2}\geq\frac{v^{2}}{2\sqrt{2}(t+t^{-1})^{2}}=\frac{\sin^{2}(2\theta)}{2\sqrt{2}}(t^{-1}-t)^{2}

for
t\in[0,1]
. Altogether, it holds that

2\operatorname{Re}(g(\lambda))\geq\varkappa+\frac{s(\theta)}{4}\int_{|\lambda|}^{1}\left(\frac{1}{t^{2}}-1\right)dt=\varkappa+s(\theta)\sinh^{2}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\ln|\lambda|\big).

This finishes the proof of the lemma when
|\lambda|\leq 1
and
\Arg\lambda\in[\alpha,\tfrac{\pi}{2}]
. Since

\operatorname{Re}(g(-\overline{\lambda}))=\operatorname{Re}(g(\lambda))

by (
5.1.14
) and (
5.1.16
) as
\ell
is purely imaginary, we can extend the above estimate to
\Arg\lambda\in[\alpha,\pi-\alpha]
. The other three regions are again obtained via symmetry relations (
5.1.14
) and (
5.1.16
).
∎

5.2
X
-RHP and its Deformation

Let
X(\lambda)
be defined by (
5.0.1
). It solves the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 12
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
X(\lambda)
such that

(1)

X(\lambda)
is analytic for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma
, where the oriented contour
\Gamma
consists of the imaginary axis and the unit circle
S^{1}
oriented as on Figure
9
;

(2)

one-sided traces
X_{\pm}(\lambda)
exist a.e. on
\Gamma
, belong to
L^{2}(\Gamma)
, and satisfy

\displaystyle X_{+}(\lambda)=X_{-}(\lambda)G_{X}(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\Gamma,

where the jump matrices
G_{X}(\lambda)
on
\Gamma
are as on Figure
9
;

(3)

it holds that
2
2

2

Recall (
5.1.14
) and (
5.1.15
).

\displaystyle X(\lambda)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}I+\mathcal{O}(1/\lambda)&\text{as}\quad\lambda\to\infty,\\
e^{x\ell\sigma_{3}}P_{0}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}e^{-x\ell\sigma_{3}}(I+\mathcal{O}(\lambda))&\text{as}\quad\lambda\to 0.\end{array}\right.

Figure 9
.
The jump matrices
G_{X}(\lambda)
for RHP
12
.

Notice that on the imaginary axis away from
\pm\mathrm{i}
the jump matrix
G_{X}(\lambda)
is exponentially close to the identity matrix as
x\to\infty
due to Lemma
5.3
. Furthermore, it holds on
\Gamma(\overline{a},a)
that

(5.2.3)

G_{X}(\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}-B^{\mathbb{R}}e^{-x\Pi(\lambda)}&-\overline{A}\\
A&B^{\mathbb{R}}e^{x\Pi(\lambda)}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-e^{-x(\varkappa+\Pi(\lambda))}&-1+\mathcal{O}(|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}e^{-\varkappa x})\\
1+\mathcal{O}(|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}e^{-\varkappa x})&e^{-x(\varkappa-\Pi(\lambda))}\end{pmatrix},

where we used (
5.1.19
) as well as (
2.2.4
) to get the last equality. Observe that the diagonal entries are also exponentially close to zero away from
\overline{a},a
since
|\Pi(\lambda)|<\varkappa
by (
5.1.18
) and Lemma
5.1
. Similarly, it holds on
\Gamma(-\overline{a},-a)
that

(5.2.4)

\displaystyle G_{X}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-e^{-x(\varkappa+\Pi(\lambda))}&-Ae^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}xV}\\
\overline{A}e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}xV}&e^{-x(\varkappa-\Pi(\lambda))}\end{pmatrix}

(5.2.5)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-e^{-x(\varkappa+\Pi(\lambda))}&-e^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}xV}+\mathcal{O}(|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}e^{-\varkappa x})\\
e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}xV}+\mathcal{O}(|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}e^{-\varkappa x})&e^{-x(\varkappa-\Pi(\lambda))}\end{pmatrix},

where we used (
5.1.19
) and (
2.2.4
). Again, the diagonal entries are exponentially close to zero away from
-\overline{a},-a
. To discuss
G_{X}(\lambda)
on
\Gamma(a,-\overline{a})
and
\Gamma(-a,\overline{a})
, first observe that

\begin{cases}\Pi(\lambda)=\varkappa\quad\text{and}\quad-\mathrm{i}\Omega(\lambda)=2g_{+}(\lambda)-\varkappa=2g_{-}(\lambda)+\varkappa,&\lambda\in\Gamma(a,-\overline{a}),\\
\Pi(\lambda)=-\varkappa\quad\text{and}\quad-\mathrm{i}\Omega(\lambda)=2g_{+}(\lambda)+\varkappa=2g_{-}(\lambda)-\varkappa,&\lambda\in\Gamma(-a,\overline{a}),\end{cases}

by (
5.1.17
), (
5.1.18
), and (
5.1.19
). Therefore,

(5.2.6)

\displaystyle G_{X}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-(B^{\mathbb{R}})^{2}&-\overline{A}e^{-\mathrm{i}x\Omega(\lambda)}\\
Ae^{\mathrm{i}x\Omega(\lambda)}&1\end{pmatrix}

(5.2.7)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&-\overline{A}e^{x(\varkappa+2g_{-}(\lambda))}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
Ae^{x(\varkappa-2g_{+}(\lambda))}&1\end{pmatrix}=:S_{L_{1}}(\lambda)S_{R_{1}}(\lambda)

for
\lambda\in\Gamma(a,\mathrm{i})
, where we used the identity
|A|^{2}=1+(B^{\mathbb{R}})^{2}
. Quite similarly, it holds that

(5.2.8)

G_{X}(\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}1&-Ae^{x(\varkappa+2g_{-}(\lambda))}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\overline{A}e^{x(\varkappa-2g_{+}(\lambda))}&1\end{pmatrix}=:S_{L_{2}}(\lambda)S_{R_{2}}(\lambda)

for
\lambda\in\Gamma(\mathrm{i},-\overline{a})
. Next, we have that

(5.2.9)

\displaystyle G_{X}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-1&-Ae^{-\mathrm{i}x\Omega(\lambda)}\\
\overline{A}e^{\mathrm{i}x\Omega(\lambda)}&(B^{\mathbb{R}})^{2}\end{pmatrix}

(5.2.10)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\overline{A}e^{x(\varkappa-2g_{-}(\lambda))}&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&Ae^{x(\varkappa+2g_{+}(\lambda))}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

(5.2.11)

\displaystyle=:S_{L_{3}}(\lambda)(-I)\,S_{R_{3}}(\lambda)

for
\lambda\in\Gamma(-a,-\mathrm{i})
and finally

(5.2.12)

\displaystyle G_{X}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-Ae^{x(\varkappa-2g_{-}(\lambda))}&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&\overline{A}e^{x(\varkappa+2g_{+}(\lambda))}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

(5.2.13)

\displaystyle=:S_{L_{4}}(\lambda)(-I)\,S_{R_{4}}(\lambda)

\lambda\in\Gamma(-\mathrm{i},\bar{a})
. In (
5.2.7
), (
5.2.8
), (
5.2.11
), and (
5.2.13
), all upper and lower triangular matrices have fast oscillating off-diagonal entries as
x\to\infty
.

Let
\gamma_{i}^{in}
and
\gamma_{i}^{out}
,
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, be arcs as depicted on Figure
10
. That is, we assume that
\gamma_{1}^{in}\cup\gamma_{2}^{in}
and
\gamma_{3}^{in}\cup\gamma_{4}^{in}
are piecewise-smooth arcs that belong to the unit disk (except for the endpoints) oriented from
a
to
-\overline{a}
and
-a
to
\overline{a}
, respectively, while
\gamma_{1}^{out}\cup\gamma_{2}^{out}
and
\gamma_{3}^{out}\cup\gamma_{4}^{out}
are piecewise-smooth arcs that belong to the exterior of the closed unit disk (again, except for the endpoints) oriented from
a
to
-\overline{a}
and
-a
to
\overline{a}
, respectively, and

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}\gamma_{1}^{in},\gamma_{1}^{out}\subset\big\{\lambda:\Arg\lambda\in[\alpha,\tfrac{\pi}{2}]\big\},&\gamma_{3}^{in},\gamma_{3}^{out}\subset\big\{\lambda:\Arg\lambda\in[-\pi+\alpha,-\tfrac{\pi}{2}]\big\},\\
\gamma_{2}^{in},\gamma_{2}^{out}\subset\big\{\lambda:\Arg\lambda\in[\tfrac{\pi}{2},\pi-\alpha]\big\},&\gamma_{4}^{in},\gamma_{4}^{out}\subset\big\{\lambda:\Arg\lambda\in[-\tfrac{\pi}{2},-\alpha]\big\}.\end{array}\right.

In fact, we shall specify these arcs exactly when discussing local parametrices further below. Moreover, let
\mathcal{R}_{i}^{in}
(resp.
\mathcal{R}_{i}^{out}
) be domains bordered by the unit circle, imaginary axis, and
\gamma_{i}^{in}
(resp.
\gamma_{i}^{out}
),
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, see Figure
10
.

Figure 10
.
The jump
G_{\tilde{X}}(\lambda)
and the contour
\tilde{\Gamma}
for RHP
13
.

Notice that each
S_{R_{i}}(\lambda)
naturally extends as a holomorphic matrix function into
\mathcal{R}_{i}^{in}
and each
S_{L_{i}}(\lambda)
into
\mathcal{R}_{i}^{out}
. Hence, we can define a sectionally holomorphic function
\tilde{X}(\lambda)
by

(5.2.14)

\displaystyle\tilde{X}(\lambda):=\begin{cases}X(\lambda)S_{L_{i}},(\lambda)&\lambda\in\mathcal{R}_{i}^{out},\quad i\in\{1,2,3,4\},\\
X(\lambda)S_{R_{i}}^{-1},(\lambda)&\lambda\in\mathcal{R}_{i}^{in},\quad i\in\{1,2,3,4\},\\
X(\lambda),&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Observe that

(5.2.15)

\displaystyle S_{L_{1}}^{-1}(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathrm{i}s^{\mathbb{R}}e^{2xg(\lambda)}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}S_{L_{2}}(\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}1&\big(\mathrm{i}s^{\mathbb{R}}+(\overline{A}-A)e^{\varkappa x}\big)e^{2xg(\lambda)}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}=I

for
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{R}_{1}^{out}\cap\partial\mathcal{R}_{2}^{out}
, where we used (
2.1.6
). Similarly,

(5.2.16)

S_{R_{1}}(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}s^{\mathbb{R}}e^{-2xg(\lambda)}&1\end{pmatrix}S_{R_{2}}^{-1}(\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\big(-\mathrm{i}s^{\mathbb{R}}+(A-\overline{A})e^{\varkappa x}\big)e^{-2xg(\lambda)}&1\end{pmatrix}=I

for
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{R}_{1}^{in}\cap\partial\mathcal{R}_{2}^{in}
. Moreover, the computations showing that
\tilde{X}(\lambda)
has no jump on
\partial\mathcal{R}_{3}^{in}\cap\partial\mathcal{R}_{4}^{in}
and
\partial\mathcal{R}_{3}^{out}\cap\partial\mathcal{R}_{4}^{out}
are absolutely identical. Thus,
\tilde{X}(\lambda)
satisfies the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 13
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
\tilde{X}(\lambda)
such that

(1)

\tilde{X}(\lambda)
is analytic for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\tilde{\Gamma}
, where
\tilde{\Gamma}
is depicted on Figure
10
;

(2)

one-sided traces
\tilde{X}_{\pm}(\lambda)
exist a.e. on
\tilde{\Gamma}
, belong to
L^{2}(\tilde{\Gamma})
, and satisfy

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{+}(\lambda)=\tilde{X}_{-}(\lambda)G_{\tilde{X}}(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\tilde{\Gamma},

where the jump matrices
G_{\tilde{X}}(\lambda)
on
\tilde{\Gamma}
are as on Figure
10
;

(3)

it holds that

\displaystyle\tilde{X}(\lambda)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}I+\mathcal{O}(1/\lambda)&\text{as}\quad\lambda\to\infty,\\
e^{x\ell\sigma_{3}}P_{0}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}e^{-x\ell\sigma_{3}}(I+\mathcal{O}(\lambda))&\text{as}\quad\lambda\to 0.\end{array}\right.

One can readily see from Lemma
5.3
that the jump matrix
G_{\tilde{X}}(\lambda)
is exponentially close to the identity on the arcs
\gamma_{i}^{in},\gamma_{i}^{out}
away from their endpoints.

5.3  Global Parametrix

If we replace
G_{\tilde{X}}(\lambda)
by
I
everywhere it is close to
I
asymptotically as
x\to\infty
, we shall arrive at the following Riemann-Hilbert problem:

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 14
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
N(\lambda)
such that

(1)

N(\lambda)
is analytic for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma(-\overline{a},a)
;

(2)

one-sided traces
N_{\pm}(\lambda)
exist a.e. on
\Gamma(-\overline{a},a)
, belong to
L^{2}(\Gamma(-\overline{a},a))
, and satisfy

(5.3.1)

N_{+}(\lambda)=N_{-}(\lambda)\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\quad}l@{}}\begin{pmatrix}0&-e^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}xV}\\
e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}xV}&0\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\Gamma(-\overline{a},-a),\\
-I,&\lambda\in\Gamma(-a,\overline{a}),\\
\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\Gamma(\overline{a},a);\end{array}\right.

(3)

it holds that
N(\lambda)=I+\mathcal{O}(1/\lambda)
as
\lambda\to\infty
.

This type of Riemann-Hilbert problem is well-known, see for example
[
15
,
16
]
. We present its solution in full as the details of the construction are important to the final result and are always problem-specific. Moreover, in the process we obtain very technical asymptotic estimates of
N(\lambda)
needed for our proof.

5.3.1  Riemann Surface

Consider an elliptic curve

(5.3.2)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}=\{\boldsymbol{z}:=(z,w)\,|\,w^{2}=f(z)\},\quad f(z)=(z^{2}-a^{2})(z^{2}-\overline{a}^{2}),

which is a Riemann surface of genus one. We realize
\mathfrak{S}
as a two-sheeted ramified covering of the Riemann sphere
\overline{\mathbb{C}}
consisting of two copies of
\overline{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\Delta
,
\Delta=\Gamma(-\overline{a},-a)\cup\Gamma(\overline{a},a)
, which are glued crosswise along the corresponding cuts. Let

(5.3.3)

\displaystyle\pi,w:\mathfrak{S}\to\overline{\mathbb{C}},\quad\pi:\boldsymbol{z}\mapsto z,\quad w:\boldsymbol{z}\mapsto w.

We call
\pi
the canonical projection and often denote points in
\mathfrak{S}
as a preimage
\boldsymbol{z}=\pi^{-1}(z)
of
z\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}
under
\pi
. The function
w(\boldsymbol{z})
is meromorphic on
\mathfrak{S}
with simple zeros at the four ramification points
\{\pm\boldsymbol{a},\,\pm\overline{\boldsymbol{a}}\}
and double poles at
\infty^{(0)}
and
\infty^{(1)}
, the points on top of infinity in each of the sheets
\overline{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\Delta
. We label these sheets by
\mathfrak{S}^{(0)}
and
\mathfrak{S}^{(1)}
so that

(5.3.4)

\displaystyle w(\boldsymbol{z})=(-1)^{k}z^{2}+\mathcal{O}(1),\quad\boldsymbol{z}\to\infty^{(k)}\in\mathfrak{S}^{(k)}.

In particular, we shall write
z^{(k)}=\pi^{-1}(z)\cap\mathfrak{S}^{(k)}
,
k\in\{0,1\}
.

Figure 11
.
Homology basis for
\mathfrak{S}
depicted on
\mathfrak{S}^{(0)}
(which is identified with
\overline{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\Delta
).

We fix a homology basis,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
-cycle and
\boldsymbol{\beta}
-cycle, on
\mathfrak{S}
as indicated in Figure
11
. Opening
\mathfrak{S}
via the cut along a homology basis will give the planar picture, see Figure
12
, and we denote the resulting cut-surface by
\mathfrak{S}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha,\beta}}:=\mathfrak{S}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cup\boldsymbol{\beta}\}
.

Figure 12
.
Planar model of
\mathfrak{S}
and the
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
- and
\boldsymbol{\beta}
-cycles.

5.3.2  Abel-Jacobi map

Recall that the space of holomorphic differentials on any elliptic curve is one-dimensional, and we denote the unique (up to normalization) holomorphic differential on
\mathfrak{S}
by

(5.3.5)

\displaystyle\omega=\left(\oint_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\frac{dz}{w(\boldsymbol{z})}\right)^{-1}\frac{dz}{w(\boldsymbol{z})}

with normalized periods

(5.3.6)

\displaystyle\oint_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\omega=1\quad\text{and}\quad\oint_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\omega=:\tau.

According to our choice of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and the normalization of
w
, we have that

\oint_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\frac{dz}{w(\boldsymbol{z})}=2\int_{\Gamma(-\overline{a},-a)}\frac{d\mu}{\sqrt{f(\mu)}_{+}}=2\mathrm{i}\int_{\pi-\alpha}^{\pi+\alpha}\frac{d\theta}{\sqrt{2(\cos(2\theta)-\cos(2\alpha))}}=2\mathrm{i}\mathsf{K},

recall (
1.0.18
) and the notation introduced in (
1.0.19
), and

\displaystyle\oint_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\frac{dz}{w(\boldsymbol{z})}

\displaystyle=2\int_{\Gamma(-a,\overline{a})}\frac{d\mu}{\sqrt{f(\mu)}_{+}}=-2\int_{\pi+\alpha}^{2\pi-\alpha}\frac{d\theta}{\sqrt{2(\cos(2\alpha)-\cos(2\theta))}}

\displaystyle=-2\int_{0}^{\tfrac{\pi}{2}-\alpha}\frac{d\theta}{\sqrt{\cos^{2}\alpha-\sin^{2}\theta}}=-2\mathsf{K}^{\prime},

where we used (
5.1.9
), see also Lemma
5.1
and its proof. In particular,

(5.3.7)

\tau=\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathsf{K}^{\prime}}{\mathsf{K}}.

By (
5.3.5
), the elliptic integral of the first kind

(5.3.8)

\displaystyle\mathsf{a}(\boldsymbol{z})=\int_{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\boldsymbol{z}}\omega,\quad\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathfrak{S},

where
\boldsymbol{a}=\pi^{-1}(a)
, defines a locally single-valued but globally additively multi-valued holomorphic function. More precisely, if we restrict the path of integration to
\mathfrak{S}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}
, then
\mathsf{a}(\boldsymbol{z})
is a well-defined function and it follows from (
5.3.6
) that

(5.3.9)

\displaystyle\mathsf{a}_{+}(\boldsymbol{z})-\mathsf{a}_{-}(\boldsymbol{z})=\begin{cases}-\tau,&\boldsymbol{z}\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}\setminus\{-\boldsymbol{a}\},\\
1,&\boldsymbol{z}\in\boldsymbol{\beta}\setminus\{-\boldsymbol{a}\}.\end{cases}

The above relations also means that if we let
\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z}
, the Abel-Jacobi map from
\mathfrak{S}
into the Jacobi variety
\mathbb{C}/\Lambda
, given by

(5.3.10)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}:\mathfrak{S}\to\mathbb{C}/\Lambda,\quad\boldsymbol{z}\mapsto\mathfrak{a}(\boldsymbol{z})=\left[\mathsf{a}(\boldsymbol{z})\right]=\left[\int_{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\boldsymbol{z}}\omega\right],

is well-defined, where
[s]=[s+l+\tau m:l,m\in\mathbb{Z}]
. Since
\mathfrak{S}
is a genus one Riemann surface, (
5.3.10
) is a biholomorphism, see
[
21
, Theorem 21.10]
or a more recent book
[
37
, Chapter 10]
.

By considering
\mathsf{a}(\boldsymbol{z})
as a function in
\mathfrak{S}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}
only, it readily follows from its definition that

(5.3.11)

\mathsf{a}\big(z^{(0)}\big)+\mathsf{a}\big(z^{(1)}\big)=0,\quad z\notin\Gamma(-\overline{a},a),

and from (
5.3.4
) and (
5.3.8
) that

(5.3.12)

\displaystyle\mathsf{a}\big(z^{(0)}\big)=\mathsf{a}\big(\infty^{(0)}\big)-\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}\mathsf{K}}\frac{1}{z}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{z^{3}}\right),\quad z\to\infty.

To compute
\mathsf{a}\big(\infty^{(0)}\big)
, observe that

\mathsf{a}\big(\infty^{(0)}\big)=\int_{\gamma}\omega=\int_{-\gamma}\omega+\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}\mathsf{K}}\int_{\Gamma(a,-a)}\frac{d\mu}{\sqrt{f(\mu)}_{-}}

where
\gamma
is any arc that connects
\boldsymbol{a}
to
\infty^{(0)}
within
\mathfrak{S}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(0)}
. Since
\sqrt{f(\mu)}
is an even function, see (
5.1.8
), it holds that
\int_{\gamma}\omega+\int_{-\gamma}\omega=0
and therefore

(5.3.13)

\mathsf{a}(\infty^{(0)})=\frac{1}{4\mathrm{i}\mathsf{K}}\left(\int_{\Gamma(-\overline{a},-a)}-\int_{\Gamma(-a,\overline{a})}\right)\frac{d\mu}{\sqrt{f(\mu)}_{-}}=-\frac{1}{4}-\frac{\tau}{4},

where we used evenness once more. Quite in the same way we also get that

(5.3.14)

\mathsf{a}(0^{(0)})=\frac{1}{4\mathrm{i}\mathsf{K}}\left(\int_{\Gamma(-\overline{a},-a)}-\int_{\Gamma(-a,\overline{a})}\right)\frac{d\mu}{\sqrt{f(\mu)}_{+}}=\frac{1}{4}-\frac{\tau}{4}.

Figure 13
.
Image of
\mathfrak{S}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}
under the map
\mathsf{a}
. The black labels
\Gamma(-\bar{a},\bar{a})_{\pm}
and
\mathfrak{S}^{(0,1)}
denote the domain of
\mathsf{a}
.

To close this subsection, we show that

\mathsf{a}(\mathfrak{S}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}})=\big\{z~|~2|\operatorname{Re}(z)|<1,~2|\operatorname{Im}(z)|<|\tau|\big\},

see Figure
13
. Indeed, for
z=e^{\mathrm{i}t}\in\Gamma(a,-\overline{a})
, using (
5.1.9
), we get that

\mathsf{a}(z^{(0)})=\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}\mathsf{K}}\int_{\alpha}^{t}\frac{d\theta}{\sqrt{2(\cos(2\alpha)-\cos(2\theta))}}

which is a negative imaginary number and is equal to
-\tfrac{\tau}{2}
when
z=-\overline{a}
(this is a similar computation to the one done to derive (
5.3.7
)).
When
z=e^{\mathrm{i}t}\in\Gamma(-\overline{a},-a)_{\pm}
, we have that

\mathsf{a}(z^{(0)})=-\frac{\tau}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2\mathsf{K}}\int_{\pi-\alpha}^{t}\frac{d\theta}{\sqrt{2(\cos(2\theta)-\cos(2\alpha))}}

again, by (
5.1.9
), where the last term above is positive and reaches the value
\tfrac{1}{2}
when
z=-a
(
t=\pi+\alpha
). That is, the images of
\Gamma(-\overline{a},-a)_{+}
and
\Gamma(-\overline{a},-a)_{-}
are the bottom horizontal lines (solid red and dashed red, respectively) on Figure
13
. Proceeding in the same fashion, one can check that
\Gamma(-a,\overline{a})_{\pm}
are mapped into vertical segments
\pm\tfrac{1}{2}+[-\tfrac{\tau}{2},0]
(solid and dashed green lines in the lower half-plane on Figure
13
) and that
\Gamma(\overline{a},a)_{\pm}
are mapped into horizontal directed segments
[\pm\tfrac{1}{2},0]
. Symmetry (
5.3.11
) then finishes the description of
\mathsf{a}(\mathfrak{S}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}})
.

5.3.3  Riemann Theta Function

As standard, to describe the solution of RHP
14
, we shall need the Riemann theta function associated with the period
\tau
. It is defined by

(5.3.15)

\displaystyle\vartheta(z):=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{\pi\mathrm{i}k^{2}\tau+2\pi\mathrm{i}kz}=1+2\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}e^{\pi\mathrm{i}k^{2}\tau}\cos(2\pi kz),

and is an entire function of
z\in\mathbb{C}
. It is well-known that
\vartheta(z)
is quasi-periodic:

(5.3.16)

\displaystyle\vartheta(z+n+m\tau)=e^{-\pi\mathrm{i}m^{2}\tau-2\pi\mathrm{i}mz}\vartheta(z),\quad z\in\mathbb{C},\quad n,m\in\mathbb{Z},

and that
\vartheta(z)=0
if and only if
[z]=\left[\frac{1+\tau}{2}\right]
.

Recall, see
[
34
]
, that in McKean and Moll’s notation
\vartheta_{3}(z\,|\,\tau)=\vartheta(z)
and

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}\displaystyle\vartheta_{0}(z\,|\,\tau)\equiv\vartheta_{4}(z\,|\,\tau)=\vartheta_{3}\left(z+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big|\,\tau\right),\\
\displaystyle\vartheta_{1}(z\,|\,\tau)=-\mathrm{i}e^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}\tau+\pi\mathrm{i}z}\vartheta_{3}\left(z+\tfrac{1+\tau}{2}\,\big|\,\tau\right),\\
\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}(z\,|\,\tau)=e^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}\tau+\pi\mathrm{i}z}\vartheta_{3}\left(z+\tfrac{\tau}{2}\,\big|\,\tau\right).\end{array}\right.

To solve RHP
14
, we shall need the following functions

(5.3.20)

\displaystyle\Theta_{k}(\boldsymbol{z})=e^{\pi\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}(\boldsymbol{z})}\frac{\vartheta(\mathsf{a}(\boldsymbol{z})+xV+\tfrac{\tau}{2}-\mathsf{a}(\infty^{(k)})-\tfrac{1+\tau}{2})}{\vartheta(\mathsf{a}(\boldsymbol{z})-\mathsf{a}(\infty^{(k)})-\tfrac{1+\tau}{2})},

\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}
,
k\in\{0,1\}
. Clearly,
\Theta_{k}(\boldsymbol{z})
is a meromorphic function in the domain of its definition with a single and simple pole at
\infty^{(k)}
(at any pole
\boldsymbol{z}_{*}
it must hold
\mathfrak{a}(\boldsymbol{z}_{*})=\mathfrak{a}(\infty^{(k)})
; as
\mathfrak{a}
is biholomorphic,
\boldsymbol{z}_{*}=\infty^{(k)}
). It readily follows from (
5.3.9
) and (
5.3.16
) that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}\Theta_{k+}(\boldsymbol{z})=e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}xV}\Theta_{k-}(\boldsymbol{z}),&\boldsymbol{z}\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{a}\},\\
\Theta_{k+}(\boldsymbol{z})=-\Theta_{k-}(\boldsymbol{z}),&\boldsymbol{z}\in\boldsymbol{\beta}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{a}\}.\end{array}\right.

Let
\Theta_{k,l}(z):=\Theta_{k}(z^{(l)})
,
k,l\in\{0,1\}
, denote the pull-backs of the functions
\Theta_{k}(\boldsymbol{z})
to the complex plane. Then,
\Theta_{0,1}(z)
and
\Theta_{1,0}(z)
are analytic in
\overline{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\Gamma(-\overline{a},a)
while
\Theta_{0,0}(z)
and
\Theta_{1,1}(z)
are analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma(-\overline{a},a)
, each having a simple pole at infinity. Of course, all functions have continuous traces on both sides of
\Gamma(-\overline{a},a)
. Moreover,

(5.3.21)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}\Theta_{k,0\pm}(z)=e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}xV}\Theta_{k,1\mp}(z),&z\in\Gamma(-\overline{a},-a),\\
\Theta_{k,l+}(z)=-\Theta_{k,l-}(z),&z\in\Gamma(-a,\overline{a}),\\
\Theta_{k,0\pm}(z)=\Theta_{k,1\mp}(z),&z\in\Gamma(\overline{a},a).\end{array}\right.

We already know that
\Theta_{0,0}(z)
and
\Theta_{1,1}(z)
have simple poles at infinity. On the other hand, we get from (
5.3.11
), (
5.3.13
), and (
5.3.16
) that

(5.3.22)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}\Theta_{1,0}(\infty)&=\displaystyle e^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}(3\tau-1)+2\pi\mathrm{i}xV}\frac{\vartheta(xV+\tfrac{\tau}{2})}{\vartheta(0)}=e^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}(2\tau-1)+\pi\mathrm{i}xV}\frac{\vartheta_{2}(xV)}{\vartheta_{3}(0)},\\
\Theta_{0,1}(\infty)&=\displaystyle e^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}(\tau+1)}\frac{\vartheta(xV+\tfrac{\tau}{2})}{\vartheta(0)}=e^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}-\pi\mathrm{i}xV}\frac{\vartheta_{2}(xV)}{\vartheta_{3}(0)},\end{array}\right.

where we used (
5.3.3
) on the last steps. It is clear that the above values vanish simultaneously, and this happens if and only if
xV=n+\tfrac{1}{2}
. Recall (
1.0.20
). It follows from the second part of (
5.1.5
) that

\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}=\left\{(\varkappa,x)\,|\,|xV-n-\tfrac{1}{2}|<\delta,\;n\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

Later on, we shall make use of the following lemma.

Lemma 5.4
.

Set
q:=e^{\pi\mathrm{i}\tau}=e^{-\pi|\tau|}
. Then, for each
\delta\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
and all
(\varkappa,x)\notin\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
it holds that

|\Theta_{0,1}(\infty)|,q^{-\frac{1}{2}}|\Theta_{1,0}(\infty)|\geq\frac{4\delta}{\pi}\sqrt{\mathsf{k}\mathsf{k}^{\prime}}\mathsf{K}.

Proof.

When
xV\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
,
xV=n+\tfrac{1}{2}+u
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\delta\leq|u|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
. We get from (
5.3.3
) and
[
18
, Equations (20.5.1) and (20.5.3)]
that

\frac{\vartheta_{2}(u+\tfrac{1}{2})}{\vartheta_{3}(0)}=-\frac{\vartheta_{1}(u)}{\vartheta_{3}(0)}=-2q^{\frac{1}{4}}\sin(\pi u)\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1-2\cos(2\pi u)q^{2n}+q^{4n}}{1+2q^{2n-1}+q^{4n-2}}.

Since
u
is real, it clearly holds that

\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1-2\cos(2\pi u)q^{2n}+q^{4n}}{1+2q^{2n-1}+q^{4n-2}}\geq\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1-q^{2n}}{1+q^{2n-1}}\right)^{2}=\frac{q^{-\frac{1}{4}}}{\pi}\sqrt{\mathsf{k}\mathsf{k}^{\prime}}\mathsf{K},

see
[
38
, Example 21.10]
. Since
\sin(\pi|u|)\geq 2|u|
when
|u|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
and
|u|\geq\delta
, the claim of the lemma follows.
∎

Next, we deduce from (
5.3.13
), (
5.3.14
), (
5.3.16
), and (
5.3.3
) that

(5.3.23)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}\Theta_{1,0}(0)&=\displaystyle e^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}(1+3\tau)+2\pi\mathrm{i}xV}\frac{\vartheta(xV+\tfrac{1+\tau}{2})}{\vartheta(\tfrac{1}{2})}=e^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}(3+2\tau)+\pi\mathrm{i}xV}\frac{\vartheta_{1}(xV)}{\vartheta_{0}(0)},\\
\Theta_{0,0}(0)&=\displaystyle e^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}(1-\tau)}\frac{\vartheta(xV)}{\vartheta(\tfrac{\tau}{2})}=e^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}}\frac{\vartheta_{3}(xV)}{\vartheta_{2}(0)}.\end{array}\right.

Finally, we estimate
\Theta_{k,l}(z)
on
\Gamma(-\overline{a},a)
(recall that these functions are analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma(-\overline{a},a)
, two analytic even at infinity and two having a simple pole there).

Lemma 5.5
.

As in Lemma
5.4
, let
q=e^{-\pi|\tau|}
. It holds for
z\in\Gamma(-\overline{a},a)_{\pm}
that

(5.3.24)

\displaystyle|\Theta_{0,0}(z)|,|\Theta_{0,1}(z)|,q^{-\frac{1}{2}}|\Theta_{1,0}(z)|,q^{-\frac{1}{2}}|\Theta_{1,1}(z)|\leq\sqrt{\frac{8}{\mathsf{k}^{\prime}}}.

Proof.

As in (
5.3.22
), we get from (
5.3.20
) combined with (
5.3.13
) and (
5.3.3
) that

\Theta_{0,1}(z)=e^{\pi\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}(z^{(1)})}\frac{\vartheta(u+xV+\tfrac{\tau}{2})}{\vartheta(u)}=e^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}-\pi\mathrm{i}xV}\frac{\vartheta_{2}(u+xV)}{\vartheta_{3}(u)},

where
u=\mathsf{a}(z^{(1)})-\tfrac{1+\tau}{4}
. Thus, we obtain from
[
18
, Equations (20.5.2-3)]
that

|\Theta_{0,1}(z)|=2q^{\frac{1}{4}}|\cos(\pi u+\pi xV)|\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\left|\frac{1+q^{2n}\cos(2\pi u+2\pi xV)+q^{4n}}{1+q^{2n-1}\cos(2\pi u)+q^{4n-2}}\right|.

We have explained around Figure
13
that when
z\in\Gamma(-\overline{a},a)_{\pm}
,
u
belongs to the boundary of the rectangle with vertices
-\tfrac{3}{4}\pm\tfrac{\tau}{4},\tfrac{1}{4}\pm\tfrac{\tau}{4}
. In particular, it holds that

(5.3.25)

\displaystyle|\cos(2\pi u)|,|\cos(2\pi u+2\pi xV)|\leq\cosh\left(\frac{\pi|\tau|}{2}\right)=\frac{q^{\frac{1}{2}}+q^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{2}

and similarly
|\cos(\pi u+\pi xV)|\leq q^{-\frac{1}{4}}
. Therefore, we get for
z\in\Gamma(-\overline{a},a)_{\pm}
that

\displaystyle|\Theta_{0,1}(z)|

\displaystyle\leq 2\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(1+q^{2n-\frac{1}{2}})(1+q^{2n+\frac{1}{2}})}{(1-q^{2n-1-\frac{1}{2}})(1-q^{2n-1+\frac{1}{2}})}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{1+q^{\frac{1}{2}}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1+q^{\frac{1}{2}(2n-1)}}{1-q^{\frac{1}{2}(2n-1)}}=\frac{2}{1+q^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(\frac{\vartheta_{3}(0|\tfrac{\tau}{2})}{\vartheta_{0}(0|\tfrac{\tau}{2})}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where we used
[
18
, Equations (20.5.3-4)]
on the last step. Formulae
[
18
, Equations (20.7.5), (20.7.12), (20.7.14), and (20.9.1)]
now give

(5.3.26)

\frac{\vartheta_{3}(0|\tfrac{\tau}{2})}{\vartheta_{0}(0|\tfrac{\tau}{2})}=\left(\frac{\vartheta_{3}^{4}(0|\tfrac{\tau}{2})}{\vartheta_{3}^{4}(0|\tfrac{\tau}{2})-\vartheta_{2}^{4}(0|\tfrac{\tau}{2})}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}=\left(\frac{\vartheta_{3}^{2}(0)+\vartheta_{2}^{2}(0)}{\vartheta_{3}^{2}(0)-\vartheta_{2}^{2}(0)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{1+\mathsf{k}}{\mathsf{k}^{\prime}}

(recall that
\mathsf{k}=\sin\alpha,\mathsf{k}^{\prime}=\cos\alpha
are the elliptic moduli corresponding to the nome
q
). Clearly, the last two displays prove (
5.3.24
) for
\Theta_{0,1}(z)
.

Next, the top and bottom relations in (
5.3.21
) immediately tell us that
|\Theta_{0,0}(z)|
satisfies (
5.3.24
) on
\Delta_{\pm}
. Thus, we only need to estimate it on
\Gamma(-a,\overline{a})
. Due to
1
-periodicity, it is enough to consider
z
for which
\mathsf{a}(z^{(0)})=\tfrac{1}{2}+(y-\tfrac{1}{4})\tau
,
|y|\leq\tfrac{1}{4}
(the lower-half plane part of the right vertical line on Figure
13
). Notice that

\Theta_{0,0}(z)=e^{\pi\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}(z^{(0)})}\frac{\vartheta(u+xV)}{\vartheta(u-\tfrac{\tau}{2})}=e^{\pi\mathrm{i}(\mathsf{a}(z^{(0)})-2u)}\frac{\vartheta(u+xV)}{\vartheta(u+\tfrac{\tau}{2})}=e^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}}\frac{\vartheta_{3}(u+xV)}{\vartheta_{2}(u)},

where
u=\mathsf{a}(z^{(0)})-\tfrac{1+\tau}{4}+\tfrac{\tau}{2}=\tfrac{1}{4}+y\tau
and we used (
5.3.16
), (
5.3.3
). Thus,

|\Theta_{0,0}(z)|=\frac{q^{-\frac{1}{4}}}{2|\cos(\pi u)|}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\left|\frac{1+q^{2n-1}\cos(2\pi u+2\pi xV)+q^{4n-2}}{1+q^{2n}\cos(2\pi u)+q^{4n}}\right|.

Observe that we still can use (
5.3.25
). On the other hand, since
\operatorname{Re}(u)=\tfrac{1}{4}
, we get that

|\cos(\pi u)|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\cosh(2\pi|y\tau|)\geq\frac{1}{4}e^{2\pi|y\tau|}\geq\frac{1}{4}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Hence, it holds that

|\Theta_{0,0}(z)|\leq\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(1+q^{2n-1-\frac{1}{2}})(1+q^{2n-1+\frac{1}{2}})}{(1-q^{2n-\frac{1}{2}})(1-q^{2n+\frac{1}{2}})}=(1-q^{\frac{1}{2}})\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1+q^{\frac{1}{2}(2n-1)}}{1-q^{\frac{1}{2}(2n-1)}},

where now we can utilize (
5.3.26
) to deduce (
5.3.24
).

Let
u=\mathsf{a}(z^{(0)})+\tfrac{1+\tau}{4}
. Then, we get from (
5.3.20
), (
5.3.16
), and (
5.3.3
) that

\displaystyle\Theta_{1,0}(z)

\displaystyle=e^{\pi\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}(z^{(0)})}\frac{\vartheta(u+xV+\tfrac{\tau}{2}-1-\tau)}{\vartheta(u-1-\tau)}=e^{\pi\mathrm{i}(\mathsf{a}(z^{(0)})+\tau+2xV)}\frac{\vartheta(u+xV+\tfrac{\tau}{2})}{\vartheta(u)}

\displaystyle=q^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}+\pi\mathrm{i}xV}\frac{\vartheta_{2}(u+xV)}{\vartheta_{3}(u)}.

Since
u
lies on the boundary of the rectangle with vertices
-\tfrac{1}{4}\pm\tfrac{\tau}{4},\tfrac{3}{4}\pm\tfrac{\tau}{4}
, which belongs to the same horizontal strip as the rectangle in the first part of the proof, the estimate of
q^{-\frac{1}{2}}|\Theta_{1,0}(z)|
follows exactly as the one of
|\Theta_{0,1}(z)|
.

The validity of (
5.3.24
) for
q^{-\frac{1}{2}}|\Theta_{1,1}(z)|
on
\Delta_{\pm}
is a consequence of the top and bottom relations in (
5.3.21
). On
\Gamma(-a,\overline{a})_{\pm}
, due to
1
-periodicity, it is enough to consider
z
for which
\mathsf{a}(z^{(1)})=\tfrac{1}{2}+(y+\tfrac{1}{4})\tau
,
|y|\leq\tfrac{1}{4}
. Then,

\Theta_{1,1}(z)=e^{\pi\mathrm{i}(\mathsf{a}(z^{(1)})-2u)}\frac{\vartheta(u+xV)}{\vartheta(u+\tfrac{\tau}{2})}=q^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{\frac{3\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}}\frac{\vartheta_{3}(u+xV)}{\vartheta_{2}(u)}

by (
5.3.16
) and (
5.3.3
), where
u=\mathsf{a}(z^{(1)})-\tfrac{3(1+\tau)}{4}+\tfrac{\tau}{2}=-\tfrac{1}{4}+y\tau
. We now can proceed exactly as in the case of
\Theta_{0,0}(z)
.
∎

5.3.4  Solution of
{N}
-RHP

Let
\gamma(\lambda)
be a function holomorphic in
\overline{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\Delta
and given by

(5.3.27)

\displaystyle\gamma(\lambda):=\left(\frac{(\lambda-a)(\lambda+a)}{(z-\overline{a})(\lambda+\overline{a})}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}},

where the branch is fixed so that
\gamma(\infty)=1
. Using any continuous determination of the argument of
\gamma^{4}(\lambda)
on the imaginary axis and the normalization
\gamma(\infty)=1
, one can check that
\gamma(0)=a
. We further can see that
\gamma_{+}(\lambda)=\mathrm{i}\gamma_{-}(\lambda)
,
\lambda\in\Delta
.

Next, define holomorphic functions

(5.3.28)

\displaystyle A(\lambda):=\frac{\gamma(\lambda)+\gamma^{-1}(\lambda)}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad B(\lambda):=-\frac{\gamma(\lambda)-\gamma^{-1}(\lambda)}{2\mathrm{i}}

for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Delta
. The functions
A(\lambda)
and
B(\lambda)
satisfy

•

A(\lambda)B(\lambda)\neq 0
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Delta
;

•

A(\infty)=1
and
B(\infty)=0
, where
\infty
is the zero of order
2
;

•

A(0)=\cos\alpha=\mathsf{k}^{\prime}
and
B(0)=-\sin\alpha=-\mathsf{k}
;

•

A_{+}(\lambda)=B_{-}(\lambda)
and
A_{-}(\lambda)=-B_{+}(\lambda)
for
\lambda\in\Delta
.

Set

(5.3.29)

M(\lambda):=\begin{pmatrix}\Theta_{1,0}(\lambda)A(\lambda)&\Theta_{1,1}(\lambda)B(\lambda)\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
-\Theta_{0,0}(\lambda)B(\lambda)&\Theta_{0,1}(\lambda)A(\lambda)\end{pmatrix},\quad\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\Gamma(-\overline{a},a).

Clearly, this is a holomorphic matrix function in the domain of its definition (the simple poles of
\Theta_{0,0}(\lambda),\Theta_{1,1}(\lambda)
at infinity are canceled by the double zero of
B(\lambda)
there). Hence, it satisfies RHP
14
(1). The fact that it satisfies RHP
14
(2) can be readily checked using (
5.3.21
). We further notice that

(5.3.30)

\displaystyle M(\infty)=\begin{pmatrix}\Theta_{1,0}(\infty)&0\\
0&\Theta_{0,1}(\infty)\end{pmatrix},

which has non-zero diagonal entries when
(\varkappa,x)\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
for any
\delta>0
, see Lemma
5.4
. Thus, for each pair
(\varkappa,x)\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
, RHP
14
is solved by

(5.3.31)

\displaystyle N(\lambda):=[M(\infty)]^{-1}M(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\Gamma(-\overline{a},a).

The following lemma holds.

Lemma 5.6
.

For any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
such that
\{|\lambda-a|=\epsilon^{4}\}
belongs to the first quadrant and for all
(\varkappa,x)\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
, it holds that

|N_{i,j}(\lambda)|\leq\frac{6\pi}{\delta\epsilon}\frac{(\mathsf{k}\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{4}}}{\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{*}},\quad i,j\in\{1,2\},

when
\dist(\lambda,\{\pm a,\pm\overline{a}\})\geq\epsilon^{4}
, where
N_{i,j}(\lambda)
is the
(i,j)
-th entry of
N(\lambda)
and
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{*}
was introduced in (
5.0.2
).

Proof.

We start with preliminary estimates of
\gamma(\lambda)
. Assume that
\alpha\in(0,\tfrac{\pi}{4}]
. When
|\lambda-a|=\epsilon^{4}
, we get that

\left|\frac{\lambda^{2}-a^{2}}{\lambda^{2}-\overline{a}^{2}}\right|\geq\frac{2-|\lambda-a|}{|a+\overline{a}|+|\lambda-a|}\left|\frac{\lambda-a}{\lambda-\overline{a}}\right|\geq\frac{1}{3}\left|\frac{\lambda-a}{\lambda-\overline{a}}\right|\geq\frac{\epsilon^{4}}{24\mathsf{k}},

where the last inequality follows from the Koebe Distortion Theorem (also known as Koebe
\tfrac{1}{4}
-Theorem), see
[
36
, Theorem 1.3]
, and the trivial observation that
(\lambda-a)/(\lambda-\overline{a})
is conformal in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{\overline{a}\}
and its derivative at
a
is equal to
(a-\overline{a})^{-1}
. An absolutely analogous estimate holds on the circle
|\lambda+a|=\epsilon^{4}
. Thus, we get from the maximum modulus principle that

\epsilon|\gamma(\lambda)|^{-1}\leq(24\mathsf{k})^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq(24\sqrt{2}\mathsf{k}\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{4}}<2\sqrt{2}(\mathsf{k}\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{4}}

on
\dist(\lambda,\{\pm a\})\geq\epsilon^{4}
, where we used the restriction
\sqrt{2}\mathsf{k}^{\prime}=\sqrt{2}\cos\alpha\geq 1
. When
\alpha\in[\tfrac{\pi}{4},\tfrac{\pi}{2})
and
|\lambda-a|=\epsilon^{4}
, we similarly have that

\left|\frac{\lambda^{2}-a^{2}}{\lambda^{2}-\overline{a}^{2}}\right|\geq\frac{2-|\lambda-a|}{|a-\overline{a}|+|\lambda-a|}\left|\frac{\lambda-a}{\lambda+\overline{a}}\right|\geq\frac{1}{3}\left|\frac{\lambda-a}{\lambda+\overline{a}}\right|\geq\frac{\epsilon^{4}}{24\mathsf{k}^{\prime}}.

As this estimate is also valid on
|\lambda+a|=\epsilon^{4}
, we see that

\epsilon|\gamma(\lambda)|^{-1}\leq 2\sqrt{2}(\mathsf{k}\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{4}}

for
\dist(\lambda,\{\pm a\})\geq\epsilon^{4}
in this case. Clearly, we can deduce exactly the same estimate of
\epsilon|\gamma(\lambda)|
on the set
\dist(\lambda,\{\pm\overline{a}\})\geq\epsilon^{4}
. Hence, it holds that

\epsilon|A(\lambda)|\leq 2\sqrt{2}(\mathsf{k}\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{4}},\quad\dist(\lambda,\{\pm a,\pm\overline{a}\})\geq\epsilon^{4}.

Next, we factor
A(\lambda)
out of
M(\lambda)
and are left with estimating the functions

\frac{\Theta_{1,0}(\lambda)}{\Theta_{1,0}(\infty)},\;\;\frac{\Theta_{0,1}(\lambda)}{\Theta_{0,1}(\infty)},\;\;\frac{B(\lambda)}{A(\lambda)}\frac{\Theta_{1,1}(\lambda)}{\Theta_{1,0}(\infty)},\;\;\text{and}\;\;\frac{B(\lambda)}{A(\lambda)}\frac{\Theta_{0,0}(\lambda)}{\Theta_{0,1}(\infty)}.

Notice that all these functions are analytic in
\overline{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\Gamma(-\overline{a},a)
. Hence, we can estimate them using the maximum modulus principle. It readily follows from Lemmas
5.4
and
5.5
that these functions are bounded above in modulus by

\max\left\{1,\max_{\Delta}\left|\frac{B}{A}\right|\right\}\frac{1}{\delta}\frac{\pi}{\mathsf{K}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\mathsf{k}}\mathsf{k}^{\prime}}

when
(\varkappa,x)\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
. Thus, we only need to estimate
(B/A)(\lambda)
on
\Delta
. It holds that

\frac{B(\lambda)}{A(\lambda)}=\mathrm{i}\frac{\gamma^{4}(\lambda)-2\gamma^{2}(\lambda)+1}{\gamma^{4}(\lambda)-1}=-\frac{\lambda^{2}-\cos(2\alpha)-2\sqrt{f(\lambda)}}{\sin(2\alpha)}.

When
\lambda\in\Delta
, we have that
\lambda^{2}=\cos(2t)+\mathrm{i}\sin(2t)
with
|t|\leq\alpha
and therefore

|\lambda^{2}-\cos(2\alpha)|=\sqrt{1-2\cos(2t)\cos(2\alpha)+\cos^{2}(2\alpha)}\leq\sqrt{1-\cos^{2}(2\alpha)}=\sin(2\alpha).

Moreover, we get from (
5.1.9
) that

|\sqrt{f(\lambda)}|=\sqrt{2(\cos(2t)-\cos(2\alpha))}\leq\sqrt{2(1-\cos(2\alpha))}=2\sin\alpha.

Hence, the triangle inequality implies that

\left|\frac{B(\lambda)}{A(\lambda)}\right|\leq\frac{\sin(2\alpha)+2\sin\alpha}{\sin(2\alpha)}\leq\frac{3}{\cos\alpha}=\frac{3}{\mathsf{k}^{\prime}},

which finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

5.4  Local Parametrices

Although we have observed that all the jump matrices in RHP
13
converge to the ones in RHP
14
as
x\to\infty
, the convergence is not uniform around
\pm a,\pm\overline{a}
. To resolve this issue, one needs to construct local parametrices in some neighborhoods of these points that approximately solve RHP
13
locally within the neighborhoods and match the global parametrix on the boundary.

5.4.1  Airy Parametrix

The construction relies on the now classical Airy model parametrix. It was introduced (in a slightly different form) in
[
15
,
16
]
and has been used ever since in countless publications. Up to a trivial prefactor and slightly different choice of the rays, we follow
[
4
]
. Let

(5.4.1)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{\Ai}(\zeta):=\sqrt{2\pi}e^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{6}}\begin{pmatrix}\Ai(\zeta)&\Ai(e^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{2\pi}{3}}\zeta)\\
-\mathrm{i}\Ai^{\prime}(\zeta)&-\mathrm{i}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{2\pi}{3}}\Ai^{\prime}(e^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{2\pi}{3}}\zeta)\end{pmatrix}e^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{6}\sigma_{3}}\sigma_{1},

which is an entire matrix function. It holds that

\tilde{\Phi}_{\Ai}(\zeta)=\sqrt{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&\mathrm{i}\end{pmatrix}A_{0}(\zeta),

where
A_{0}(\zeta)
is the matrix from
[
4
, Equation (3.29)]
. Set

\Phi_{\Ai}(\zeta):=\tilde{\Phi}_{\Ai}(\zeta)\begin{cases}I,&\arg\zeta\in\left(0,\tfrac{3\pi}{4}\right),\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\arg\zeta\in\left(\tfrac{3\pi}{4},\tfrac{5\pi}{4}\right),\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{pmatrix},&\arg\zeta\in\left(\tfrac{5\pi}{4},2\pi\right).\end{cases}

It is a sectionally holomorphic matrix function whose traces satisfy

(5.4.2)

\Phi_{\Ai+}(\zeta)=\Phi_{\Ai-}(\zeta)\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\arg\zeta=\tfrac{3\pi}{4},\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-1&1\end{pmatrix},&\arg\zeta=\tfrac{5\pi}{4},\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},&\arg\zeta=0,\end{cases}

where the orientation of the rays is depicted on Figure
14
(in
[
4
]
the chosen angles are
\tfrac{2\pi}{3}
and
\tfrac{4\pi}{3}
).

Figure 14
.
The jump matrices and the jump contour for
\Phi_{\Ai}(\zeta)
.

Moreover, it holds that

(5.4.3)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\Ai}(\zeta)=\frac{\zeta^{-\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{3}}}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}&1\end{pmatrix}+\mathcal{O}\left(\zeta^{-\frac{3}{2}}\right)\right)\sigma_{1}\,e^{\frac{2}{3}\zeta^{\frac{3}{2}}\sigma_{3}}

uniformly as
\zeta\to\infty
, where
\arg(\zeta^{\gamma})=\gamma\arg\zeta
for
\arg\zeta\in(0,2\pi)
(unlike similar asymptotic formula stated in
[
15
,
16
]
where the roots are principal).

5.4.2  Conformal Map

To use Airy parametrix from the previous subsection, we need to introduce a conformal map of a neighborhood of
a
(resp.
\overline{a},-a,-\overline{a}
) to the
\zeta
-plane where
\Phi_{\Ai}(\zeta)
is defined. In this section, we provide a detailed construction around
a
, understanding that the other points can be handled similarly.

Let
\mathcal{Q}_{1}:=\{\lambda~|~\operatorname{Re}(\lambda),\operatorname{Im}(\lambda)>0\}
be the first quadrant. For
\lambda\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}\setminus\Gamma(1,a)
, define

(5.4.4)

\displaystyle h_{1}(\lambda):=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\int_{a}^{\lambda}\frac{\sqrt{f(\mu)}}{\mu^{2}}d\mu,

which is an analytic function in the domain of its definition. It readily follows from (
5.1.9
) and (
5.1.10
) that

(5.4.5)

\displaystyle h_{1-}(\lambda)=-h_{1+}(\lambda)>0,\quad\lambda\in\Gamma(1,a).

Moreover, we obtain from (
5.1.11
), (
5.1.17
), (
5.1.18
), and (
5.1.19
) that

(5.4.6)

2h_{1}(\lambda)=2g(\lambda)+\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\quad}l@{}}-\varkappa,&|\lambda|<1,\\
\varkappa,&|\lambda|>1,\end{array}\right.\quad\lambda\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}.

Consider
\lambda\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}\cap\{|\lambda|<1\}
(shaded region on the left panel of Figure
15
). It follows from (
5.4.6
) and the discussion after (
5.1.19
) that
\tfrac{3}{2}xh_{1}(\lambda)
bijectively maps this region into the shaded region depicted on the right panel of Figure
15
:

Figure 15
.
The interior of the unit disk in the first quadrant (left panel) and its image under
\tfrac{3}{2}xh_{1}(\lambda)
(right panel).

the arc
\Gamma(1,a)
is mapped into the horizontal segment
(-\tfrac{3}{4}\varkappa x,0)
; the arc
\Gamma(a,\mathrm{i})
is mapped into the vertical segment
(0,-\tfrac{3}{4}\pi\mathrm{i}Vx)
; the segment
\mathrm{i}(1,0)
is mapped into the horizontal half line
-\tfrac{3}{4}\pi\mathrm{i}Vx+(0,\infty)
; and the segment
(0,1)
is mapped into vertical half line
-\tfrac{3}{4}\varkappa x+\mathrm{i}(\infty,0)
.

Next, select the cubic root of
h_{1}(\lambda)
according to the rule

(5.4.7)

\arg\big(h_{1}^{\frac{1}{3}}(\lambda)\big)=\frac{1}{3}\arg(h_{1}(\lambda))+\frac{2\pi}{3},\quad\arg(h_{1}(\lambda))\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\pi\right).

In this case the map
(\tfrac{3}{2}xh_{1}(\lambda))^{\frac{1}{3}}
takes
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\cap\{|\lambda|<1\}
bijectively onto the shaded region depicted on the left panel of Figure
16
(the dashed lines on this figure are rays with angles
\tfrac{2\pi}{3}
and
\tfrac{5\pi}{6}
).

Figure 16
.
Images of
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\cap\{|\lambda|<1\}
under the maps
(\frac{3}{2}xh_{1}(\lambda))^{\frac{1}{3}}
(left panel) and
\big((\tfrac{3}{2}xh_{1}(\lambda))^{\frac{1}{3}}\big)^{2}
(right panel).

Now, we define

(5.4.8)

\zeta_{1}(\lambda):=\big((\tfrac{3}{2}xh_{1}(\lambda))^{\frac{1}{3}}\big)^{2},

which maps
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\cap\{|\lambda|<1\}
conformally into the shaded region depicted on the right panel of Figure
16
(the dashed lines are rays with angles
\tfrac{4\pi}{3}
and
\tfrac{5\pi}{3}
).

Performing the same analysis in
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\cap\{|\lambda|>1\}
and/or with the help of symmetry

g(\lambda)=-\overline{g(1/\overline{\lambda})}

see (
5.1.14
), we see that
\zeta_{1}(\lambda)
in (
5.4.8
) extends to a conformal map of the entire first quadrant (in (
5.4.7
) the argument of
h_{1}(\lambda)
needs to be selected in
(-2\pi,-\tfrac{\pi}{2})
so that it is continuous across
\Gamma(a,\mathrm{i})
). The set
\zeta_{1}(\mathcal{Q}_{1})
is a strip domain consisting of the shaded region depicted on the right panel of Figure
16
, its reflection across the real axis, and the open real interval bordering both regions.

We now select the arc
\gamma_{1}^{in},\gamma_{1}^{out}
in the following way:

\zeta_{1}(\gamma_{1}^{out})=\{\arg\zeta=\tfrac{3\pi}{4}\}\cap\zeta_{1}(\mathcal{Q}_{1})\quad\text{and}\quad\zeta_{1}(\gamma_{1}^{in})=\{\arg\zeta=\tfrac{5\pi}{4}\}\cap\zeta_{1}(\mathcal{Q}_{1})

(notice that this definition is independent of
x
and that these arcs are reflections of each other across the unit circle). Further, let
\zeta_{*}\neq 0
be the point where the ray
\{\arg\zeta=\tfrac{3\pi}{4}\}
intersects
\zeta_{1}(\mathcal{Q}_{1})
(orange point on the right panel of Figure
16
is
\overline{\zeta}_{*}
). We select domain
\mathcal{D}_{1}
so that

\zeta_{1}(\mathcal{D}_{1})=\zeta_{1}(\mathcal{Q}_{1})\cap\{-\operatorname{Im}(\zeta_{*})<\operatorname{Im}(\zeta)<\operatorname{Im}(\zeta_{*})\}.

Again, this domain is independent of
x
and is symmetric with respect to the reflection across
S^{1}
. The boundary of
\mathcal{D}_{1}\cap\{|\lambda|<1\}
is depicted on the left panel of Figure
15
(curvilinear rectangle with vertices
1,\mathrm{i}
, orange dot on the vertical axis, and pink dot on the horizontal axis). The right panel of Figure
15
as well as the left panel of Figure
16
show how it transforms under the corresponding maps.

Lemma 5.7
.

It holds that

|\zeta_{1}(\lambda)|^{\frac{3}{2}}\geq\frac{3x}{8}(\mathsf{k}\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad|\lambda|\geq d,\quad\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{D}_{1},

for some
d>0
independent of
\varkappa
and
x
.

Proof.

Consider vertical rays

\ell_{1}:=\left\{-(\tfrac{3\pi}{4}Vx)^{\frac{2}{3}}(1+\mathrm{i}y)~|~y>0\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad\ell_{2}:=\left\{(\tfrac{3}{4}\varkappa x)^{\frac{2}{3}}(1-\mathrm{i}y)~|~y>0\right\}.

Let
\ell_{1}^{*}
and
\ell_{2}^{*}
be their corresponding preimages under the map
z\mapsto z^{2/3}
, where the cubic root is the same as in (
5.4.7
). It is an exercise in elementary complex analysis to show that

\operatorname{Im}(z)>-\tfrac{3\pi}{4}Vx,\;\;z\in\ell_{1}^{*},\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{Re}(z)>-\tfrac{3}{4}\varkappa x,\;\;z\in\ell_{2}^{*},

from which we can conclude that the curves
\ell_{1}^{*}
and
\ell_{2}^{*}
belong to the shaded region on the right panel of Figure
15
. Respectively,
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\subset\zeta_{1}(\mathcal{Q}_{1})
. It now readily follows, see Figure
17
, that

|\zeta_{1}(\lambda)|^{\frac{3}{2}}\geq\frac{3}{4}x\min\{\pi V,\varkappa\},\quad\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{D}_{1}.

Since
\varkappa,\pi V\in(0,1)
, the first claim of the lemma follows from (
5.1.4
).

To prove the second claim, we first explain why there exists
d_{*}>0
, independent of
\varkappa
and
x
, for which

(5.4.9)

|\zeta_{1}(\lambda)|^{\frac{3}{2}}\leq xd_{*},\quad\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{D}_{1}.
