Indeed, since the domains on the right panels of Figures
15
and
16
are connected by an appropriate branch of the map
z\mapsto z^{\frac{2}{3}}
, one can see from the both figures that the furthest points from the origin on
\partial\zeta_{1}(\mathcal{D}_{1})
are the points lying on the horizontal line
\{\zeta|\operatorname{Im}(\zeta)=-\operatorname{Im}(\zeta_{*})\}
(orange and pink dots on Figures
16
and
17
), one of them (the orange point) being
\overline{\zeta}_{*}
. It can be readily seen from the right panel of Figure
15
that

|\zeta_{*}|^{\frac{3}{2}}=\frac{3\pi Vx}{4}\left|\cot\left(\frac{\pi}{8}\right)+\mathrm{i}\right|.

Moreover, as in the first part of the proof, a tedious but straightforward computation shows that the ray
\{(3\varkappa x/4)^{\frac{2}{3}}+re^{\frac{5}{3}\pi\mathrm{i}}|r>0\}
lies outside of
\zeta_{1}(\mathcal{D}_{1})
(this is the dash-dot ray on Figure
17
). Hence, the second point of interest is no further from the origin than the point of intersection of this ray and the horizontal line
\{\zeta|\operatorname{Im}(\zeta)=-\operatorname{Im}(\zeta_{*})\}
. Thus, it is no further than

\left[|\operatorname{Im}(\zeta_{*})|^{2}+\left(\left(\frac{3}{4}\varkappa x\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}+\cot\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right)|\operatorname{Im}(\zeta_{*})|\right)^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

The last two displays clearly show the validity of (
5.4.9
). Now, we get from (
5.4.9
), (
5.4.8
), and (
5.4.6
) that

\frac{4}{3}d_{*}\geq|2h_{1}(\lambda)|=|2g(\lambda)-\varkappa|\geq\frac{(1-|\lambda|)^{2}}{2|\lambda|},

where the last inequality follows from the Koebe distortion theorem
[
36
, Theorem 1.3]
(
(2g(\lambda)-\varkappa)^{-1}
is univalent in
\{|\lambda|<1\}
with derivative
-2\mathrm{i}
at the origin, see (
5.1.14
) and (
5.1.15
)). The last display yields the second claim of the lemma.
∎

Figure 17
.
The vertical rays
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
and the distance
|\zeta_{1}(\lambda)|
,
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{D}_{1}
, which is always greater than
(\tfrac{3}{4}\pi Vx)^{\frac{2}{3}}
in the depicted case.

Recall (
5.0.2
). We shall also need the following lemma.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
R(\varkappa,x):=(\tfrac{x}{4}(\mathsf{k}\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{2})^{\frac{2}{3}}
and
0<R_{*}\leq R(\varkappa,x)
. It holds for all
(\varkappa,x)\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
that

N(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{x^{\frac{1}{6}}}{\delta\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{**}}\right)

for
\lambda\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\setminus\zeta_{1}^{-1}\big(\{|z|<R_{*}\}\big)
, where
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is independent of
\varkappa,x
, and
\delta
. Moreover,

N(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\delta\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{*}}\right)

for
\lambda\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
(\varkappa,x)\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
, where again
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is independent of
\varkappa,x
, and
\delta
.

Proof.

It follows from the first claim of Lemma
5.7
that
\zeta_{1}(\mathcal{D}_{1})
contains a disk of radius
R(\varkappa,x)
centered at the origin. Hence, we can apply the Koebe Distortion Theorem
[
36
, Theorem 1.3]
to
\zeta_{1}^{-1}(z)
on the disk of radius
R_{*}
centered at the origin, which gives

\min_{|\zeta_{1}(\lambda)|=R_{*}}|\lambda-a|\geq\frac{R_{*}}{4|\zeta_{1}^{\prime}(a)|}.

It can be readily computed using (
5.4.8
), (
5.4.4
), and the definition of
f(\mu)
that

|\zeta_{1}^{\prime}(a)|=\left(\frac{1}{2}{x^{2}}\mathsf{k}\mathsf{k}^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}.

Therefore,

\min_{|\zeta_{1}(\lambda)|=R_{*}}|\lambda-a|\geq\frac{x^{-\frac{2}{3}}R_{*}}{4(\mathsf{k}\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{3}}}.

Hence, the lower bound above can serve as
\epsilon^{4}
in Lemma
5.6
, which gives the first claim of the lemma. The second claim is obtained analogously by replacing
R_{*}
with
R(\varkappa,x)
and observing that

\min_{\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{D}_{1}}|\lambda-a|\geq\min_{|\zeta_{1}(\lambda)|=R(\varkappa,x)}|\lambda-a|\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{R(\varkappa,x)}{4|\zeta_{1}^{\prime}(a)|}=\frac{1}{8}\mathsf{k}\mathsf{k}^{\prime}.\qed

5.4.3  Local Parametrix Near
a

Let
\zeta_{1}(\lambda)
be as in the previous subsection. Selecting roots of this map needs to be done in accordance with the rule stated after (
5.4.3
). Since
\Gamma(1,a)_{-}
(as approached from outside of the unit disk) is mapped into
(0,\infty)_{+}
(as approached from upper half-plane), it must hold that
\zeta_{1-}^{\gamma}(\lambda)>0
on
\Gamma(1,a)
. Hence,

(5.4.10)

\displaystyle\zeta_{1}^{\frac{3}{2}}(\lambda)=\frac{3}{2}xh_{1}(\lambda)

by (
5.4.5
) and
\zeta_{1}^{\frac{1}{4}}(\lambda)
is holomorphic in
\mathcal{D}_{1}\setminus\Gamma(1,a)
satisfying

(5.4.11)

\zeta_{1-}^{\frac{1}{4}}(\lambda)=-\mathrm{i}\zeta_{1+}^{\frac{1}{4}}(\lambda)\geq 0,\quad\lambda\in\Gamma(1,a).

Define

E_{1}(\lambda):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}N(\lambda)\sigma_{1}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}\\
-\mathrm{i}&1\end{pmatrix}\zeta_{1}^{\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{3}}(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\mathcal{D}_{1}.

RHP
14
(2) shows that it holds on
\Gamma(1,a)
that

(5.4.12)

E_{1+}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}N_{-}(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\sigma_{1}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}\\
-\mathrm{i}&1\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{i}^{\sigma_{3}}\zeta_{1-}^{\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{3}}(\lambda)=E_{1-}(\lambda).

It is easy to see from (
5.3.27
) and (
5.3.29
) that
N(\lambda)
can have at most a quarter root singularity at
a
. Hence,
E_{1}(\lambda)
can have at most a square root singularity there. Combining this with (
5.4.12
) means that
E_{1}(\lambda)
is analytic in
\mathcal{D}_{1}
. Fix
0<R_{*}<R(\varkappa,x)
. It readily follows from the first estimate of Lemma
5.8
and the maximum modulus principle for analytic functions that

(5.4.13)

E_{1}(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{x^{\frac{1}{6}}}{\delta\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{**}}\right),\quad|\zeta_{1}(\lambda)|\leq R_{*},

for all
(\varkappa,x)\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
, where
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is independent of
\varkappa,x
, and
\delta
.

Set

(5.4.14)

H_{1}(\lambda):=E_{1}(\lambda)\Phi_{\Ai}(\zeta_{1}(\lambda))e^{-\frac{2}{3}\zeta_{1}^{\frac{3}{2}}(\lambda)\sigma_{3}},\quad\lambda\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\setminus\tilde{\Gamma}.

Then,
H_{1}(\lambda)
solves the following Riemann-Hilbert problem (this is not as much a formulation of a Riemann-Hilbert problem as an enumeration of properties of
H_{1}(\lambda)
).

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 15
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
H_{1}(\lambda)
such that

(1)

H_{1}(\lambda)
is analytic in
\lambda\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\setminus\tilde{\Gamma}
, where
\tilde{\Gamma}
is depicted on Figure
10
;

(2)

H_{1}(\lambda)
has bounded traces on
\tilde{\Gamma}\cap\mathcal{D}_{1}
that satisfy

H_{1+}(\lambda)=H_{1-}(\lambda)\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&e^{x(\Pi(\lambda)-\varkappa)}\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\Gamma(1,a),\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
e^{x(\varkappa-2g(\lambda))}&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\gamma_{1}^{in},\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&-e^{x(\varkappa+2g(\lambda))}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\gamma_{1}^{out};\end{cases}

(3)

it holds for all
(\varkappa,x)\notin\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
satisfying (
1.0.21
) and uniformly on
\partial\mathcal{D}_{1}
that

H_{1}(\lambda)=\left(I+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\delta^{2}x\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}}\right)\right)N(\lambda),

where
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is independent of
\varkappa,x
, and
\delta
;

(4)

it holds for all
(\varkappa,x)\notin\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
satisfying (
1.0.21
) and uniformly in
\mathcal{D}_{1}
that

H_{1}(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{x^{\frac{1}{6}}}{\delta\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{**}}\right),

where
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is independent of
\varkappa,x
, and
\delta
.

Indeed, RHP
15
(1) is straightforward. RHP
15
(2) follows from (
5.4.2
) and (
5.4.6
) (when the orientation of the rays is reversed, the jump matrices in (
5.4.2
) must be inverted). Since
\mathsf{k},\mathsf{k}^{\prime}<1
and
\mathsf{k}^{\prime}\mathsf{K}<E(\mathsf{k})\leq\tfrac{\pi}{2}
, see
[
18
, Equation (19.9.8)]
, it holds that

x(\mathsf{k}\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{2}\geq\frac{4}{\pi^{2}}x\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\geq c_{0}\frac{4}{\pi^{2}}D_{\delta}\geq c_{0}D_{4},

where we used Lemma
C.2
from Appendix
C
further below for the penultimate estimate (
c_{0}
is an absolute constant). Thus, with a proper choice of
D_{4}
, we see from the first claim of Lemma
5.7
that (
5.4.3
) is applicable. Hence, we get from the definition of
E_{1}(\lambda)
and Lemma
5.7
that

H_{1}(\lambda)=\left(I+N(\lambda)\,\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{x(\mathsf{k}\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{2}}\right)N^{-1}(\lambda)\right)N(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{D}_{1}.

Thus, RHP
15
(3) is a consequence of (
5.1.4
) and the second claim of Lemma
5.8
. To see the validity of RHP
15
(4), we first consider
\lambda
such that
|\zeta_{1}(\lambda)|\geq R_{*}
. Choosing
D_{4}
in (
1.0.21
) large enough, we can select
R_{*}
sufficiently large so that

\Phi_{\Ai}(\zeta)=\zeta^{-\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{3}}\mathcal{O}(1)\,e^{\frac{2}{3}\zeta^{\frac{3}{2}}\sigma_{3}},\quad|\zeta|>R_{*},

see again (
5.4.3
). The desired claim now follows from the definition of
E_{1}(\lambda)
and the first claim of Lemma
5.8
. On the other hand, when
|\zeta_{1}(\lambda)|\leq R_{*}
, we have that

\Phi_{\Ai}(\zeta),e^{-\frac{2}{3}\zeta^{\frac{3}{2}}\sigma_{3}}=\mathcal{O}(1),\quad|\zeta|\leq R_{*},

which, combined with (
5.4.13
), finishes the demonstration of RHP
15
(4).

5.4.4  Local Parametrix Near
-\overline{a}

The remaining three constructions are extremely similar to the previous one. Let
\mathcal{Q}_{2}:=\{\lambda~|~-\operatorname{Re}(\lambda),\operatorname{Im}(\lambda)>0\}
denote the second quadrant. Set

(5.4.15)

\displaystyle h_{2}(\lambda):=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\int_{{-\bar{a}}}^{\lambda}\frac{\sqrt{f(\mu)}}{\mu^{2}}d\mu,\quad\lambda\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}\setminus\Gamma(-\overline{a},-1).

It follows from (
5.1.9
) and (
5.1.10
) that

(5.4.16)

\displaystyle h_{2-}(\lambda)=-h_{2+}(\lambda)>0,\quad\lambda\in\Gamma(-\overline{a},1),

and from (
5.1.11
), (
5.1.17
), (
5.1.18
), and (
5.1.19
) that

(5.4.17)

2h_{2}(\lambda)=2g(\lambda)+\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\quad}l@{}}-\varkappa+2\pi\mathrm{i}V,&|\lambda|<1,\\
\varkappa+2\pi\mathrm{i}V,&|\lambda|>1,\end{array}\right.\quad\lambda\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}.

Moreover, we get from (
5.1.14
), (
5.1.16
), and Lemma
5.2
that

g(\lambda)=-\overline{g(\overline{\lambda})}=\overline{g(-\overline{\lambda})}-\pi\mathrm{i}V.

Thus, we see from (
5.4.6
) and (
5.4.17
) that
h_{2}(\lambda)=\overline{h_{1}(-\overline{\lambda})}
. Respectively, the desired conformal map on
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
is given by

(5.4.18)

\displaystyle\zeta_{2}(\lambda):=\left(-\frac{3}{2}\,x\,h_{2}(\lambda)\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}=\overline{\zeta_{1}(-\overline{\lambda})}.

Strictly speaking, there is no reason to put
-
sign in front of
h_{2}(\lambda)
in the above formula since its effect is erased by squaring. However, since
\zeta_{2}(\lambda)
takes the positive side of
\Gamma(-\overline{a},1)
into the positive side of
(0,\infty)
, unlike in the case of
\zeta_{1}(\lambda)
, we now need to have that
\zeta_{2+}^{\gamma}(\lambda)>0
on
\Gamma(-\overline{a},1)
. This, in view of (
5.4.16
), implies that

\zeta_{2}^{\frac{3}{2}}(\lambda)=-\frac{3}{2}xh_{2}(\lambda),

which we emphasize by having the sign
-
in (
5.4.18
). Furthermore, it holds that

(5.4.19)

\zeta_{2+}^{\frac{1}{4}}(\lambda)=-\mathrm{i}\zeta_{2-}^{\frac{1}{4}}(\lambda)\geq 0,\quad\lambda\in\Gamma(-\overline{a},1).

We now choose the arcs
\gamma_{2}^{in},\gamma_{2}^{out}
and domain
\mathcal{D}_{2}
to be the reflections across the vertical axis of the arcs
\gamma_{1}^{in},\gamma_{1}^{out}
and domain
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, respectively. Naturally, the claims of Lemma
5.7
remain valid for
\zeta_{2}(\lambda)
on
\partial\mathcal{D}_{2}
and of Lemma
5.8
for
N(\lambda)
in
\mathcal{D}_{2}\setminus\zeta_{2}^{-1}\big(\{|z|<R_{*}\}\big)
and
\overline{\mathcal{Q}_{2}}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{2}
.

Define

E_{2}(\lambda):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}N(\lambda)e^{-\pi\mathrm{i}xV\sigma_{3}}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}\\
-\mathrm{i}&1\end{pmatrix}\zeta_{2}^{\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{3}}(\lambda),

which is an analytic matrix function in
\mathcal{D}_{2}\setminus\Gamma(-\overline{a},-1)
. We get from RHP
14
(2) that

E_{2+}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}N_{-}(\lambda)e^{-\pi\mathrm{i}xV\sigma_{3}}\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}\\
-\mathrm{i}&1\end{pmatrix}(-\mathrm{i})^{\sigma_{3}}\zeta_{2-}^{\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{3}}(\lambda)=E_{2-}(\lambda)

for
\lambda\in\Gamma(-\overline{a},-1)
and therefore
E_{2}(\lambda)
is analytic in
\mathcal{D}_{2}
(again, the singularity at
-\overline{a}
is removable). Moreover, the analogue of the first claim of Lemma
5.8
now gives

(5.4.20)

E_{2}(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{x^{\frac{1}{6}}}{\delta\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{**}}\right),\quad|\zeta_{2}(\lambda)|\leq R_{*},

for all
(\varkappa,x)\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
, where
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is independent of
\varkappa,x
, and
\delta
.

Set

(5.4.21)

H_{2}(\lambda):=E_{2}(\lambda)\Phi_{\Ai}(\zeta_{2}(\lambda))\sigma_{1}e^{\frac{2}{3}\zeta_{2}^{\frac{3}{2}}(\lambda)\sigma_{3}}e^{\pi\mathrm{i}xV\sigma_{3}},\quad\lambda\in\mathcal{D}_{2}\setminus\tilde{\Gamma}.

Then,
H_{2}(\lambda)
solves the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 16
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
H_{2}(\lambda)
such that

(1)

H_{2}(\lambda)
is analytic in
\mathcal{D}_{2}\setminus\tilde{\Gamma}
;

(2)

H_{2}(\lambda)
has bounded traces on
\tilde{\Gamma}\cap\mathcal{D}_{2}
that satisfy

H_{2+}(\lambda)=H_{2-}(\lambda)\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}0&-e^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}xV}\\
e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}xV}&e^{x(\Pi(\lambda)-\varkappa)}\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\Gamma(-\overline{a},-1),\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
e^{x(\varkappa-2g(\lambda))}&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\gamma_{2}^{in},\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&-e^{x(\varkappa+2g(\lambda))}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\gamma_{2}^{out};\end{cases}

(3)

it holds for all
(\varkappa,x)\notin\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
satisfying (
1.0.21
) and uniformly on
\partial\mathcal{D}_{2}
that

H_{2}(\lambda)=\left(I+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\delta^{2}x\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}}\right)\right)N(\lambda),

where
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is independent of
\varkappa,x
, and
\delta
;

(4)

it holds for all
(\varkappa,x)\notin\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
satisfying (
1.0.21
) and uniformly on
\mathcal{D}_{2}
that

H_{2}(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{x^{\frac{1}{6}}}{\delta\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{**}}\right),

where
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is independent of
\varkappa,x
, and
\delta
.

As before, RHP
16
(1) is straightforward; RHP
16
(2) follows from (
5.4.2
) and (
5.4.17
); RHP
16
(3) is a consequence of (
5.4.3
), analogues of Lemmas
5.7
and
5.8
for
\zeta_{2}(\lambda)
, and the fact that
e^{\pi\mathrm{i}xV}
is a unimodular number; and RHP
16
(4) is deduced from (
5.4.3
), an analogue of Lemma
5.8
, and (
5.4.20
).

5.4.5  Local Parametrix Near
-a

Denote by
\mathcal{Q}_{3}:=\{\lambda~|~\operatorname{Re}(\lambda),\operatorname{Im}(\lambda)<0\}
the third quadrant. Set

(5.4.22)

\displaystyle h_{3}(\lambda):=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\int_{-a}^{\lambda}\frac{\sqrt{f(\mu)}}{\mu^{2}}d\mu,\quad\lambda\in\mathcal{Q}_{3}\setminus\Gamma(-1,-a).

In this case, it follows from (
5.1.9
) and (
5.1.10
) that

(5.4.23)

h_{3+}(\lambda)=-h_{3-}(\lambda)>0,\quad\lambda\in\Gamma(-1,-a).

Moreover, we get from (
5.1.11
), (
5.1.17
), (
5.1.18
), and (
5.1.19
) that

(5.4.24)

2h_{3}(\lambda)=2g(\lambda)+\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\quad}l@{}}\varkappa+2\pi\mathrm{i}V,&|\lambda|<1,\\
-\varkappa+2\pi\mathrm{i}V,&|\lambda|>1,\end{array}\right.\quad\lambda\in\mathcal{Q}_{3}.

Symmetry relation (
5.1.16
), Lemma
5.2
, and (
5.4.6
) now give

g(\lambda)=-g(-\lambda)-\pi\mathrm{i}V\quad\Rightarrow\quad h_{3}(\lambda)=-h_{1}(-\lambda).

Hence, we have that

(5.4.25)

\displaystyle\zeta_{3}(\lambda):=\left(-\frac{3}{2}\,x\,h_{3}(\lambda)\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}=\zeta_{1}(-\lambda).

Since
\zeta_{3}(\lambda)
maps the negative side of
\Gamma(-1,-a)
into the positive side of
(0,\infty)
, it must hold that
\zeta_{3-}^{\gamma}(\lambda)>0
on
\Gamma(-1,-a)
. Given (
5.4.23
), we see that

\zeta_{3}^{\frac{3}{2}}(\lambda)=-\frac{3}{2}xh_{3}(\lambda)

and that
\zeta_{3}^{\frac{1}{4}}(\lambda)
satisfies (
5.4.11
) on
\Gamma(-1,-a)
. The arcs
\gamma_{3}^{in},\gamma_{3}^{out}
and domain
\mathcal{D}_{3}
are now chosen to be the reflections across the origin of the arcs
\gamma_{1}^{in},\gamma_{1}^{out}
and domain
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, respectively. The claims of Lemma
5.7
remain valid for
\zeta_{3}(\lambda)
on
\partial\mathcal{D}_{3}
and of Lemma
5.8
remain valid for
N(\lambda)
in
\mathcal{D}_{3}\setminus\zeta_{3}^{-1}\big(\{|z|<R_{*}\}\big)
and
\overline{\mathcal{Q}_{3}}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{3}
.

Define

E_{3}(\lambda):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}N(\lambda)e^{-\pi\mathrm{i}xV\sigma_{3}}J(\lambda)\sigma_{3}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}\\
-\mathrm{i}&1\end{pmatrix}\zeta_{3}^{\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{3}}(\lambda),

which is an analytic matrix function in
\mathcal{D}_{1}\setminus S^{1}
, where

J(\lambda):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\quad}l@{}}-\mathrm{i}^{\sigma_{3}},&|\lambda|<1,\\
\mathrm{i}^{\sigma_{3}},&|\lambda|>1.\end{array}\right.

It holds that

E_{3+}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}N_{-}(\lambda)(-I)e^{-\pi\mathrm{i}xV\sigma_{3}}J_{-}(\lambda)(-I)\sigma_{3}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}\\
-\mathrm{i}&1\end{pmatrix}\zeta_{3}^{\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{3}}(\lambda)=E_{3-}(\lambda)

on
\Gamma(-a,-\mathrm{i})
and that

E_{3+}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}N_{-}(\lambda)e^{-\pi\mathrm{i}xV\sigma_{3}}J_{-}(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\sigma_{3}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}\\
-\mathrm{i}&1\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{i}^{\sigma_{3}}\zeta_{3-}^{\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{3}}(\lambda)=E_{3-}(\lambda)

on
\Gamma(-1,-a)
. Hence, as in the previous three cases
E_{3}(\lambda)
is analytic in the disk of its definition. Again, the analogue of the first claim of Lemma
5.8
now gives

(5.4.26)

E_{3}(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{x^{\frac{1}{6}}}{\delta\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{**}}\right),\quad|\zeta_{3}(\lambda)|\leq R_{*},

for all
(\varkappa,x)\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
, where
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is independent of
\varkappa,x
, and
\delta
.

Put

(5.4.27)

H_{3}(\lambda):=E_{3}(\lambda)\Phi_{\Ai}(\zeta_{3}(\lambda))J(\lambda)\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}e^{\frac{2}{3}\zeta_{3}^{\frac{3}{2}}(\lambda)\sigma_{3}}e^{\pi\mathrm{i}xV\sigma_{3}},\quad\lambda\in\mathcal{D}_{3}\setminus\tilde{\Gamma}.

Then,
H_{3}(\lambda)
solves the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 17
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
H_{3}(\lambda)
such that

(1)

H_{3}(\lambda)
is analytic in
\mathcal{D}_{3}\setminus\tilde{\Gamma}
;

(2)

H_{3}(\lambda)
has bounded traces on
\tilde{\Gamma}\cap\mathcal{D}_{3}
that satisfy

H_{3+}(\lambda)=H_{3-}(\lambda)\begin{cases}-I,&\lambda\in\Gamma(-a,-\mathrm{i}),\\
\begin{pmatrix}-e^{-x(\Pi(\lambda)+\varkappa)}&-e^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}xV}\\
e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}xV}&0\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\Gamma(-1,-a),\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&e^{x(\varkappa+2g(\lambda))}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\gamma_{3}^{in},\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-e^{x(\varkappa-2g(\lambda))}&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\gamma_{3}^{out};\end{cases}

(3)

it holds for all
(\varkappa,x)\notin\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
satisfying (
1.0.21
) and uniformly on
\partial\mathcal{D}_{3}
that

H_{3}(\lambda)=\left(I+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\delta^{2}x\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}}\right)\right)N(\lambda),

where
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is independent of
\varkappa,x
, and
\delta
;

(4)

it holds for all
(\varkappa,x)\notin\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
satisfying (
1.0.21
) and uniformly on
\mathcal{D}_{3}
that

H_{3}(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{x^{\frac{1}{6}}}{\delta\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{**}}\right),

where
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is independent of
\varkappa,x
, and
\delta
.

RHP
17
(1) is immediate; RHP
17
(2) follows from (
5.4.2
) and (
5.4.24
) (because of the reverse orientation of the rays, the jump matrices in (
5.4.2
) must be inverted); RHP
17
(3) is a consequence of (
5.4.3
), the analogues of Lemmas
5.7
and
5.8
for
\zeta_{3}(\lambda)
, and the fact that
e^{\pi\mathrm{i}xV}
is a unimodular number; and finally RHP
17
(4) is deduced from (
5.4.3
), an analogue of Lemma
5.8
, and (
5.4.26
) exactly as before.

5.4.6  Local Parametrix Near
\overline{a}

Finally, let
\mathcal{Q}_{4}:=\{\lambda~|~\operatorname{Re}(\lambda),-\operatorname{Im}(\lambda)>0\}
be the fourth quadrant. Set

(5.4.28)

\displaystyle h_{4}(\lambda):=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\int_{\bar{a}}^{\lambda}\frac{\sqrt{f(\mu)}}{\mu^{2}}d\mu,\quad\lambda\in\mathcal{Q}_{4}\setminus\Gamma(\overline{a},1).

In this case we get from (
5.1.9
) and (
5.1.10
) that

(5.4.29)

h_{4+}(\lambda)=-h_{4-}(\lambda)>0,\quad\lambda\in\Gamma(\overline{a},1),

and from (
5.1.11
), (
5.1.17
), (
5.1.18
), and (
5.1.19
) that

(5.4.30)

2h_{4}(\lambda)=2g(\lambda)+\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\quad}l@{}}\varkappa,&|\lambda|<1,\\
-\varkappa,&|\lambda|>1,\end{array}\right.\quad\lambda\in\mathcal{Q}_{4}.

Symmetry relations (
5.1.14
) and (
5.4.6
) yield

g(\lambda)=-\overline{g(\overline{\lambda})}\quad\Rightarrow\quad h_{4}(\lambda)=-\overline{h_{1}(\overline{\lambda})}.

Hence, we have that

(5.4.31)

\displaystyle\zeta_{4}(\lambda):=\left(\frac{3}{2}\,x\,h_{4}(\lambda)\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}=\overline{\zeta_{1}(\overline{\lambda})}.

Since
\zeta_{4}(\lambda)
maps the positive side of
\Gamma(\overline{a},1)
into the positive side of
(0,\infty)
, it must hold that
\zeta_{4+}^{\gamma}(\lambda)>0
on
\Gamma(\overline{a},1)
. Thus, we get from (
5.4.29
) that

\zeta_{4}^{3/2}(\lambda)=\frac{3}{2}xh_{4}(\lambda)

and that
\zeta_{4}^{\frac{1}{4}}(\lambda)
satisfies (
5.4.19
) on
\Gamma(\overline{a},1)
. The arcs
\gamma_{4}^{in},\gamma_{4}^{out}
and domain
\mathcal{D}_{4}
are chosen as the reflections across the horizontal axis of the arcs
\gamma_{1}^{in},\gamma_{1}^{out}
and domain
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, respectively. As usual, The claims of Lemma
5.7
still hold for
\zeta_{4}(\lambda)
on
\partial\mathcal{D}_{4}
and of Lemma
5.8
hold for
N(\lambda)
in
\mathcal{D}_{4}\setminus\zeta_{4}^{-1}\big(\{|z|<R_{*}\}\big)
and
\overline{\mathcal{Q}_{4}}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{4}
.

Recall the definition of
J(\lambda)
in the previous subsection. Define

E_{4}(\lambda):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}N(\lambda)J^{-1}(\lambda)\sigma_{3}\sigma_{1}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}\\
-\mathrm{i}&1\end{pmatrix}\zeta_{4}^{\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{3}}(\lambda),

which is an analytic matrix function in
\mathcal{D}_{4}\setminus S^{1}
. We get from RHP
14
(2) that

E_{4+}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}N_{-}(\lambda)(-I)J_{-}^{-1}(\lambda)(-I)\sigma_{3}\sigma_{1}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}\\
-\mathrm{i}&1\end{pmatrix}\zeta_{4}^{\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{3}}(\lambda)=E_{4-}(\lambda)

on
\Gamma(-\mathrm{i},\overline{a})
and

\displaystyle E_{4+}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}N_{-}(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}J_{+}^{-1}(\lambda)\sigma_{3}\sigma_{1}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}\\
-\mathrm{i}&1\end{pmatrix}(-\mathrm{i})^{\sigma_{3}}\zeta_{4-}^{\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{3}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}N_{-}(\lambda)J_{-}^{-1}(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}-1&\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}&-1\end{pmatrix}\zeta_{4-}^{\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{3}}(\lambda)=E_{4-}(\lambda)

on
\Gamma(\overline{a},1)
. Hence, as in all the previous subsections, we can conclude that
E_{4}(\lambda)
is analytic in
\mathcal{D}_{4}
and that the analogue of the first claim of Lemma
5.8
gives

(5.4.32)

E_{4}(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{x^{\frac{1}{6}}}{\delta\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{**}}\right),\quad|\zeta_{4}(\lambda)|\leq R_{*},

for all
(\varkappa,x)\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
, where
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is independent of
\varkappa,x
, and
\delta
.

Set

(5.4.33)

H_{4}(\lambda):=E_{4}(\lambda)\Phi_{\Ai}(\zeta_{4}(\lambda))J(\lambda)\sigma_{3}e^{-\frac{2}{3}\zeta_{4}^{\frac{3}{2}}(\lambda)\sigma_{3}}

for
\lambda\in\mathcal{D}_{4}\setminus\tilde{\Gamma}
. Then,
H_{4}(\lambda)
solves the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 18
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
H_{4}(\lambda)
such that

(1)

H_{4}(\lambda)
is analytic in
\mathcal{D}_{4}\setminus\tilde{\Gamma}
;

(2)

H_{4}(\lambda)
has bounded traces on
\tilde{\Gamma}\cap\mathcal{D}_{4}
that satisfy

H_{4+}(\lambda)=H_{4-}(\lambda)\begin{cases}-I,&\lambda\in\Gamma(-\mathrm{i},\overline{a}),\\
\begin{pmatrix}-e^{-x(\Pi(\lambda)+\varkappa)}&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\Gamma(\overline{a},1),\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&e^{x(\varkappa+2g(\lambda))}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\gamma_{4}^{in},\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-e^{x(\varkappa-2g(\lambda))}&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\gamma_{4}^{out};\end{cases}

(3)

it holds for all
(\varkappa,x)\notin\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
satisfying (
1.0.21
) and uniformly on
\partial\mathcal{D}_{4}
that

H_{4}(\lambda)=\left(I+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\delta^{2}x\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}}\right)\right)N(\lambda),

where
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is independent of
\varkappa,x
, and
\delta
;

(4)

it holds for all
(\varkappa,x)\notin\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
satisfying (
1.0.21
) and uniformly on
\mathcal{D}_{4}
that

H_{4}(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{x^{\frac{1}{6}}}{\delta\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{**}}\right),

where
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is independent of
\varkappa,x
, and
\delta
.

Again, RHP
18
(1) is straightforward; RHP
18
(2) follows from (
5.4.2
) and (
5.4.30
); RHP
18
(3) is a consequence of (
5.4.3
), analogues of Lemmas
5.7
and
5.8
for
\zeta_{4}(\lambda)
; RHP
18
(4) is a consequence of (
5.4.3
), an analogue of Lemma
5.8
, and (
5.4.32
).

5.5  Asymptotic Analysis

Let
\mathcal{D}:=\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{2}\cup\mathcal{D}_{3}\cup\mathcal{D}_{4}
and put
H(\lambda):=H_{i}(\lambda)
,
\lambda\in\mathcal{D}_{i}\setminus\tilde{\Gamma}
,
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
. We look for the solution of RHP
13
in the following form:

\displaystyle\tilde{X}(\lambda)=:R(\lambda)\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\quad}l@{}}H(\lambda),&\lambda\in\mathcal{D}\setminus\tilde{\Gamma},\\
N(\lambda),&\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(\tilde{\Gamma}\cup\mathcal{D}).\end{array}\right.

Then, the error function
R(\lambda)
must solve the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 19
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
R(\lambda)
such that

(1)

R(\lambda)
is analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{R}
, where
\Gamma_{R}:=\tilde{\Gamma}\cup\partial\mathcal{D}
, see Figure
18
;

(2)

one-sided traces
R_{\pm}(\lambda)
exist a.e. on
\Gamma_{R}
, belong to
L^{2}(\Gamma_{R})
, and satisfy

(5.5.3)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} R_{+}(\lambda)&=R_{-}(\lambda)G_{R}(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\Gamma_{R},\end{aligned}

where

\displaystyle G_{R}(\lambda)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\quad}l@{}}N(\lambda)H^{-1}(\lambda),&\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{D}\setminus(\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}\cup\mathbb{R}),\\
H_{-}(\lambda)H_{+}^{-1}(\lambda),&\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{D}\cap(\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}\cup\mathbb{R}),\\
H_{-}(\lambda)G_{\tilde{X}}(\lambda)H_{+}^{-1}(\lambda),&\lambda\in\tilde{\Gamma}\cap\mathcal{D},\\
N_{-}(\lambda)G_{\tilde{X}}(\lambda)N_{+}^{-1}(\lambda),&\lambda\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}\setminus\partial\mathcal{D};\end{array}\right.

(3)

it holds that
R(\lambda)=I+\mathcal{O}(1/\lambda)
as
\lambda\to\infty
.

Figure 18
.
The contour
\Gamma_{R}
for RHP
18
.

As in all the previous cases, we now discuss the size of
G_{R}(\lambda)-I
to show that RHP
19
is a small norm problem. In what follows, we assume without mentioning that
(\varkappa,x)\notin\mathcal{Z}_{\delta}
for some
\delta\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
and that (
1.0.21
) holds. All
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
terms appearing below are independent of
\varkappa,x
, and
\delta
.

It readily follows from RHP
15
(3), RHP
16
(3), RHP
17
(3), and RHP
18
(3) that

(5.5.8)

G_{R}(\lambda)=I+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\delta^{2}x\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}}\right),\quad\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{D}

(this is an estimate on the red parts of the contour on Figure
18
).

Next, let
\mathrm{i}y_{*}
be the common endpoint of the arcs
\gamma_{1}^{out}
and
\gamma_{2}^{out}
. Then, according to Lemma
5.3
and the second claim of Lemma
5.8
together with its analogues in
\mathcal{Q}_{2},\mathcal{Q}_{3}
, and
\mathcal{Q}_{4}
, we have on
\mathrm{i}[y_{*},\infty)
that

(5.5.9)

\displaystyle G_{R}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=I+e^{2xg(\lambda)}N(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathrm{i}s^{\mathbb{R}}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}N^{-1}(\lambda)

(5.5.10)

\displaystyle=I+\mathcal{O}\left(|s^{\mathbb{R}}|\delta^{-2}(\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{*})^{-2}e^{-x\left(\varkappa+\frac{1}{2}\sinh^{2}(\frac{1}{2}\ln|\lambda|)\right)}\right).

In fact, one can easily see from Lemma
5.3
and the form of
G_{\tilde{X}}(\lambda)
on the imaginary axis that the above estimate remains valid on
\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}\setminus\partial\mathcal{D}
. In particular, we have that

(5.5.11)

\|G_{R}-I\|_{L^{2}(\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}\setminus\partial\mathcal{D})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}\setminus\partial\mathcal{D})}=\mathcal{O}\left(|s^{\mathbb{R}}|\delta^{-2}(\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{*})^{-2}e^{-\varkappa x}\right)

(this is an estimate on the green parts of the contour on Figure
18
).

Next, it follows from (
5.2.3
), RHP
15
(2,4) and RHP
18
(2,4) that

(5.5.12)

\displaystyle G_{R}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=I+H_{-}(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{O}(|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}e^{-\varkappa x})&-e^{-x(\varkappa+\Pi(\lambda))}\\
e^{-x(\varkappa-\Pi(\lambda))}\mathcal{O}(|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}e^{-\varkappa x})&\mathcal{O}(|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}e^{-\varkappa x})\end{pmatrix}H_{-}^{-1}(\lambda)

(5.5.13)

\displaystyle=I+\mathcal{O}\left(|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}\delta^{-2}(\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{**})^{-2}x^{\frac{1}{3}}e^{-\varkappa x}\right),

on
\Gamma(\overline{a},a)
, where we used the fact that
-\varkappa\leq\Pi(\lambda)\leq\varkappa
on the considered arc by (
5.1.18
). An estimate on
\Gamma(-\overline{a},-a)
is absolutely analogous (we also need to use unimodularity of
e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}xV}
). On the other hand, on
\gamma_{1}^{out}
we get from the definition of
S_{L_{1}}(\lambda)
in (
5.2.7
) and RHP
15
(2,4) that

(5.5.14)

\displaystyle G_{R}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=I+e^{x(\varkappa+2g(\lambda))}H_{1-}(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}0&1-\overline{A}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}H_{1-}^{-1}(\lambda)

(5.5.15)

\displaystyle=I+\mathcal{O}\left(|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}\delta^{-2}(\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{**})^{-2}x^{\frac{1}{3}}e^{-\varkappa x}\right),

where we used (
2.2.4
) and the fact that
2\operatorname{Re}(g(\lambda))\leq-\varkappa
on the considered set by Lemma
5.3
. Again, one can straightforwardly verify that the same estimate holds on all
\gamma_{i}^{out}
and
\gamma_{i}^{in}
,
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
(this finishes an estimate on the blue parts of the lens on Figure
18
). Combining (
5.5.8
), (
5.5.11
), (
5.5.13
), and (
5.5.15
) gives

(5.5.16)

\|G_{R}-I\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{R})\cap L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{R})}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\delta^{2}x\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}}\right),

where we also used Lemma
C.2
of Appendix
C
. Lemma
C.2
also shows that

\delta^{2}x\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\geq c_{0}\delta^{2}D_{\delta}=c_{0}D_{4}

for some absolute constant
c_{0}
. Hence, as in the first part of this paper, we get from (
5.5.16
) and the small norm theorem that there exists a unique solution
R(\lambda)
of the RHP
19
if we choose
D_{4}
large enough. There is a technical difficulty here in that
\Gamma_{R}
is not a single contour but a family of contours that depend on
(\varkappa,x)
. Therefore, to see that a single constant
D_{4}
suffices, we need uniform boundedness of the norms of the corresponding Cauchy operators. This fact is explained in Appendix
A
. Then,

R(\lambda)=I+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{R}}\frac{\rho(\lambda^{\prime})(G_{R}(\lambda^{\prime})-I)}{\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda}d\lambda^{\prime},\quad\lambda\notin\Gamma_{R},

where
\displaystyle\rho(\lambda^{\prime})=R_{-}(\lambda^{\prime})
for
\lambda^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{R}
, see (
2.3.7
). Exactly as in (
2.3.10
), we get in this case that

\|\rho-I\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{R})}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\delta^{2}x\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}}\right).

Observe that
G_{R}(\lambda^{\prime})-I
vanishes exponentially at the origin by (
5.5.10
) and Lemma
5.3
. Hence, we get exactly as in the case of (
2.3.12
) that
R(0)
is well defined and is expressed as

R(0)=I+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{R}}\frac{G_{R}(\lambda^{\prime})-I}{\lambda^{\prime}}d\lambda^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{R}}\frac{(\rho(\lambda^{\prime})-I)(G_{R}(\lambda^{\prime})-I)}{\lambda^{\prime}}d\lambda^{\prime}.

Using the exponential vanishing of
G_{R}(\lambda^{\prime})-I
at the origin once more, we see that estimate (
5.5.11
) remains valid for
(G_{R}(\lambda^{\prime})-I)/\lambda^{\prime}
. Moreover, since
\partial\mathcal{D}
is separated away from the origin independently of
\varkappa
and
x
, see Lemma
5.7
, estimates (
5.5.8
), (
5.5.13
), and (
5.5.15
) remain true for
(G_{R}(\lambda^{\prime})-I)/\lambda^{\prime}
as well. Thus, we get from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that

(5.5.17)

R(0)=I+o(1),\quad o(1)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\delta^{2}x\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}}\right).

To finish the proof of Theorem
1.4
, we observe from RHP
13
(3) and (
5.5
) that

P_{0}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}=e^{-x\ell\sigma_{3}}R(0)N(0)e^{x\ell\sigma_{3}}.

Recall that
\ell
is purely imaginary, see Lemma
5.2
, and therefore
e^{x\ell}
is unimodular. We get from the definition of
P_{0}
in RHP
1
(3), (
5.3.29
), and (
5.3.30
) that

\begin{pmatrix}\sinh\tfrac{u}{2}&*\\
\cosh\tfrac{u}{2}&*\end{pmatrix}=\left(I+o(1)\right)\begin{pmatrix}\Theta_{1,0}^{-1}(\infty)&0\\
0&\Theta_{0,1}^{-1}(\infty)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{k}^{\prime}\,\Theta_{1,0}(0)&*\\
e^{2x\ell}\mathsf{k}\,\Theta_{0,0}(0)&*\end{pmatrix},

where, until the end of this subsection, the error rate
o(1)
is as in (
5.5.17
). Hence, we get that

e^{\pm\frac{u}{2}}=(1+o(1))e^{2x\ell}\mathsf{k}\frac{\Theta_{0,0}(0)}{\Theta_{0,1}(\infty)}\pm(1+o(1))\mathsf{k}^{\prime}\frac{\Theta_{1,0}(0)}{\Theta_{1,0}(\infty)},

which, with the help of Lemma
5.2
, (
5.3.22
), and (
5.3.23
), can be rewritten as

e^{\pm\frac{u}{2}}=(1+o(1))\mathsf{k}\frac{\vartheta_{3}(0)\vartheta_{3}(xV)}{\vartheta_{2}(0)\vartheta_{2}(xV)}\mp(1+o(1))\mathsf{k}^{\prime}\frac{\vartheta_{3}(0)\vartheta_{1}(xV)}{\vartheta_{0}(0)\vartheta_{2}(xV)}.

Writing
e^{u}
as the ratio of
e^{\frac{u}{2}}
and
e^{-\frac{u}{2}}
gives

e^{u}=\frac{1+o(1)-(1+o(1))\frac{\mathsf{k}^{\prime}}{\mathsf{k}}\frac{\vartheta_{2}(0)\vartheta_{1}(xV)}{\vartheta_{0}(0)\vartheta_{3}(xV)}}{1+o(1)+(1+o(1))\frac{\mathsf{k}^{\prime}}{\mathsf{k}}\frac{\vartheta_{2}(0)\vartheta_{1}(xV)}{\vartheta_{0}(0)\vartheta_{3}(xV)}}.

Since
\mathsf{k},\mathsf{k}^{\prime}
are elliptic moduli corresponding to the nome
q=e^{\pi\mathrm{i}\tau}=e^{-\pi\mathsf{K}^{\prime}/\mathsf{K}}
, see (
5.3.7
), we get from
[
18
, Equations (22.2.2) and (22.2.7)]
that

\mathsf{k}=\frac{\vartheta_{2}^{2}(0)}{\vartheta_{3}^{2}(0)}\quad\text{and}\quad\sd(2xV\mathsf{K},\mathsf{k})=\frac{\vartheta_{3}^{2}(0)}{\vartheta_{0}(0)\vartheta_{2}(0)}\frac{\vartheta_{1}(xV)}{\vartheta_{3}(xV)}.

Thus, it holds that

\frac{\mathsf{k}^{\prime}}{\mathsf{k}}\frac{\vartheta_{2}(0)\vartheta_{1}(xV)}{\vartheta_{0}(0)\vartheta_{3}(xV)}=\mathsf{k}^{\prime}\sd(2xV\mathsf{K},\mathsf{k}).

Since
\mathsf{k}^{\prime}|\sd(\cdot,\mathsf{k})|\leq 1
on the real line, it follows from (
5.1.5
) that

e^{u}=\frac{1-\mathsf{k}^{\prime}\sd(2xV\mathsf{K},\mathsf{k})+o(1)}{1+\mathsf{k}^{\prime}\sd(2xV\mathsf{K},\mathsf{k})+o(1)}=\frac{1-\mathsf{k}^{\prime}\sd(x-2\kappa\mathsf{K},\mathsf{k})+o(1)}{1+\mathsf{k}^{\prime}\sd(x-2\kappa\mathsf{K},\mathsf{k})+o(1)}.

Asymptotic formula (
1.0.22
) is now a consequence of (
2.1.5
) and (
5.5.17
).

6 Proof of Theorem
1.5

The proof of Theorem
1.5
closely follows the proof of Theorem
IMY
given in
[
30
]
. However, as compared to
[
30
]
, we use a slightly different jump factorization, which leads to a simpler construction of the global parametrix. On the other hand, since the monodromy parameter
B^{\mathbb{R}}
is now a function of
x
, we need a more precise analysis of the local parametrices. In that, we follow the line of arguments developed in
[
10
]
.

Similarly to all the previous sections, we start with a gauge transformation of RHP
1
. Again, we take
\rho=4/x
and now set

(6.0.1)

\displaystyle W(\lambda):=\hat{\Psi}\left(\frac{4\lambda}{x}\right)\begin{Bmatrix}I,&|\lambda|>1\\
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3},&|\lambda|<1\end{Bmatrix}e^{-x\varphi(\lambda)\sigma_{3}},\quad\varphi(\lambda):=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\left(\lambda+\frac{1}{\lambda}\right).

As we have pointed out before, if we take
\varkappa=0
in (
5.1.11
), then
\varphi(\lambda)=g(\lambda)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}
.

6.1  Deformed Riemann-Hilbert Problem

As expected, the matrix function
W(\lambda)
defined above solves a Riemann-Hilbert problem that is extremely similar to RHP
12
:

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 20
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
W(\lambda)
such that

(1)

W(\lambda)
is analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma
, where, as before,
\Gamma=\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}\cup S^{1}
, see Figure
19
;

(2)

one-sided traces
W_{\pm}(\lambda)
exist a.e. on
\Gamma
, belong to
L^{2}(\Gamma)
, and satisfy

\displaystyle W_{+}(\lambda)=W_{-}(\lambda)G_{W}(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\Gamma,

where the jump matrices
G_{W}(\lambda)
on
\Gamma
are as on Figure
19
;

(3)

it holds that

\displaystyle W(\lambda)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}I+\mathcal{O}(1/\lambda)&\text{as}\quad\lambda\to\infty,\\
P_{0}\,\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}(I+\mathcal{O}(\lambda))&\text{as}\quad\lambda\to 0.\end{array}\right.

Figure 19
.
The jump matrices
G_{W}(\lambda)
for RHP
20
.

We now proceed by deforming RHP
20
using certain factorizations of the jump matrices comprising
G_{W}(\lambda)
that are quite similar to (
5.2.7
)–(
5.2.13
). However, since
\varphi(\lambda)
is a rational function and therefore, unlike
g(\lambda)
, has no branch cut, the functions
\Pi(\lambda)
and
\Omega(\lambda)
from (
5.1.17
) are now simply
0
and
2\mathrm{i}\varphi(\lambda)
, respectively. This necessitates an appearance of a diagonal matrix
e^{\varkappa x\sigma_{3}}
in the formulae below, where one needs to recall that
B^{\mathbb{R}}=e^{-\varkappa x}
. More precisely, we have that

(6.1.3)

\displaystyle G_{W}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-B^{\mathbb{R}}&-\overline{A}e^{2x\varphi(\lambda)}\\
Ae^{-2x\varphi(\lambda)}&B^{\mathbb{R}}\end{pmatrix}

(6.1.4)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&-\overline{A}e^{x(\varkappa+2\varphi(\lambda))}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
Ae^{-x(\varkappa+2\varphi(\lambda))}&1\end{pmatrix}e^{\varkappa x\sigma_{3}}=:S_{L_{1}}^{*}(\lambda)S_{R_{1}}^{*}(\lambda)e^{\varkappa x\sigma_{3}}

for
\lambda\in S^{1}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}
, where, as before,
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
is the
i
-th quadrant. In a similar way we get that

(6.1.5)

\displaystyle G_{W}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-B^{\mathbb{R}}&-Ae^{2x\varphi(\lambda)}\\
\overline{A}e^{-2x\varphi(\lambda)}&B^{\mathbb{R}}\end{pmatrix}

(6.1.6)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&-Ae^{x(\varkappa+2\varphi(\lambda))}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\overline{A}e^{-x(\varkappa+2\varphi(\lambda))}&1\end{pmatrix}e^{\varkappa x\sigma_{3}}=:S_{L_{2}}^{*}(\lambda)S_{R_{2}}^{*}(\lambda)e^{\varkappa x\sigma_{3}}

for
\lambda\in S^{1}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{2}
. As in (
5.2.11
)–(
5.2.13
), we change the order of the triangular matrices in the lower half-plane. This way we obtain that

(6.1.7)

\displaystyle G_{W}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\overline{A}e^{x(\varkappa-2\varphi(\lambda))}&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&Ae^{-x(\varkappa-2\varphi(\lambda))}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\big(-e^{-\varkappa x\sigma_{3}}\big)

(6.1.8)

\displaystyle=:S_{L_{3}}^{*}(\lambda)S_{R_{3}}^{*}(\lambda)\big(-e^{-\varkappa x\sigma_{3}}\big)

for
\lambda\in S^{1}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{3}
and

(6.1.9)

\displaystyle G_{W}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-Ae^{x(\varkappa-2\varphi(\lambda))}&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&\overline{A}e^{-x(\varkappa-2\varphi(\lambda))}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\big(-e^{-\varkappa x\sigma_{3}}\big)

(6.1.10)

\displaystyle=:S_{L_{4}}^{*}(\lambda)S_{R_{4}}^{*}(\lambda)\big(-e^{-\varkappa x\sigma_{3}}\big)

for
\lambda\in S^{1}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{4}
. Moreover, similarly to (
5.2.15
) and (
5.2.16
), it follows from (
2.1.6
) that

(6.1.11)

\displaystyle S_{L_{1}}^{*}(\lambda)^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathrm{i}s^{\mathbb{R}}e^{2x\varphi(\lambda)}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}S_{L_{2}}^{*}(\lambda)=I

for
\lambda\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
with
\operatorname{Im}(\lambda)>1
and

(6.1.12)

\displaystyle S_{L_{3}}^{*}(\lambda)^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\mathrm{i}s^{\mathbb{R}}e^{-2x\varphi(\lambda)}&1\end{pmatrix}S_{L_{4}}^{*}(\lambda)=I

for
\lambda\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
with
\operatorname{Im}(\lambda)<-1
, as well as

(6.1.13)

\displaystyle S_{R_{1}}^{*}(\lambda)e^{\varkappa x\sigma_{3}}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}s^{\mathbb{R}}e^{-2x\varphi(\lambda)}&1\end{pmatrix}e^{-\varkappa x\sigma_{3}}S_{R_{2}}^{*}(\lambda)^{-1}=I

for
\lambda\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
with
\operatorname{Im}(\lambda)\in(0,1)
and

(6.1.14)

\displaystyle S_{R_{3}}^{*}(\lambda)e^{-\varkappa x\sigma_{3}}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}s^{\mathbb{R}}e^{2x\varphi(\lambda)}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}e^{\varkappa x\sigma_{3}}S_{R_{4}}^{*}(\lambda)^{-1}=I

for
\lambda\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
with
\operatorname{Im}(\lambda)\in(-1,0)
.

Figure 20
.
The zero level curve
\operatorname{Re}(2\varphi(\lambda))=0
(blue), the sign of
\operatorname{Re}(2\varphi(\lambda))
(gray), and the stationary contour
\operatorname{Im}(2\varphi)=\pm 1
(red).

The deformation of RHP
20
is done in the regions determined by the stationary curves of
\varphi(\lambda)
. More precisely, Let
\lambda=\xi+\mathrm{i}\eta
, where
\xi,\,\eta\in\mathbb{R}
. Then,

(6.1.15)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}(2\varphi(\lambda))=\frac{\eta\left(1-\xi^{2}-\eta^{2}\right)}{2\left(\xi^{2}+\eta^{2}\right)}\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{Im}(2\varphi(\lambda))=\frac{\xi\left(1+\xi^{2}+\eta^{2}\right)}{2\left(\xi^{2}+\eta^{2}\right)}.

Clearly,
\varphi(\lambda)
is purely imaginary on the unit circle and the stationary points of
2\varphi(\lambda)
are
\lambda=\pm 1
with
2\varphi(\pm 1)=\pm\mathrm{i}
. The stationary contour
\{\operatorname{Im}(2\varphi(\lambda))=\pm 1\}
is shown on Figure
20
. We set

\Gamma^{*}:=\{\operatorname{Im}(2\varphi(\lambda))=\pm 1\}\cup[-1,1],

whose orientation is shown on Figure
22
.

Figure 21
.
Definition of the matrix
W^{*}(\lambda)
. The solid contour is the stationary contour of
2\varphi(\lambda)
, see Figure
20
.

Let the matrix function
W^{*}(\lambda)
be defined by Figure
21
. Then,
W^{*}(\lambda)
is the solution of the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 21
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
W^{*}(\lambda)
such that

(1)

W^{*}(\lambda)
is analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma^{*}
;

(2)

one-sided traces
W_{\pm}^{*}(\lambda)
exist a.e. on
\Gamma^{*}
, belong to
L^{2}(\Gamma^{*})
, and satisfy

\displaystyle W^{*}_{+}(\lambda)=W^{*}_{-}(\lambda)G_{W^{*}}(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\Gamma^{*},

where the jump matrices
G_{W^{*}}(\lambda)
are as on Figure
22
;

(3)

it holds that

\displaystyle W^{*}(\lambda)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}I+\mathcal{O}(1/\lambda)&\text{as}\quad\lambda\to\infty,\\
\pm P_{0}\,\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}(I+\mathcal{O}(\lambda))e^{\mp\varkappa x\sigma_{3}}&\text{as}\quad\lambda\to 0,\;\;\pm\operatorname{Im}(\lambda)>0.\end{array}\right.

Figure 22
.
The contour
\Gamma^{*}
and the jump matrices
G_{W^{*}}(\lambda)
for RHP
21
.

RHP
21
(2) readily follows from the factorizations obtained above and RHP
20
(2). The limit at
0
in RHP
21
(3) is simply the result of the definition of
W^{*}(\lambda)
around the origin and of RHP
20
(3). The limit at infinity in RHP
21
(3) is the consequence of the fact that
S_{L_{i}}(\lambda)
uniformly converges to the identity matrix as
\lambda\to\infty
within the domain where
W^{*}(\lambda):=W(\lambda)S_{L_{i}}(\lambda)
because
\operatorname{Re}(\varphi(\lambda))=-\tfrac{1}{2}\eta+o(1)
as
\lambda=\xi+\mathrm{i}\eta\to\infty
.

Lastly, we point out that one can directly verify that

(6.1.18)

\displaystyle G_{W^{*}}(\lambda)=\sigma_{1}G_{W^{*}}(-\lambda)\sigma_{1},\quad\lambda\in\Gamma^{*}

(the reason we oriented
(-1,0)
from right to left is so that (
6.1.18
) holds everywhere on
\Gamma^{*}
).

6.2  Global Parametrix

It is not hard to convince oneself using (
6.1.15
) that the jump matrix
G_{W^{*}}(\lambda)
is approaching identity as
x\to\infty
everywhere on
\Gamma^{*}\setminus[-1,1]
. Therefore, the leading order behavior of
W^{*}(\lambda)
is determined by a matrix function that is analytic in
\overline{\mathbb{C}}\setminus[-1,1]
, is equal to
I
at infinity and whose boundary values on
(-1,1)
are related by the jump matrix
-e^{-2\varkappa x\sigma_{3}}
when
(-1,1)
has a single orientation from left to right. Clearly, the matrix function

(6.2.1)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}\right)^{\nu\sigma_{3}},\quad\nu=\frac{1}{2}+\mathrm{i}\frac{\varkappa x}{\pi},

possesses all these properties, where we take the principal branch of the power function (i.e., this matrix function is positive on the real line way from
[-1,1]
). Observe also that

(6.2.2)

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\to 0,\pm\operatorname{Im}(\lambda)>0}\left(\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}\right)^{\nu\sigma_{3}}=e^{\pm\pi\mathrm{i}\nu\sigma_{3}}.

6.3  Local Parametrices

The jump matrix
G_{W^{*}}(\lambda)
is uniformly close to the identity on
\Gamma^{*}\setminus[-1,1]
only away from
\pm 1
. Therefore, we separately solve RHP
21
locally around
\pm 1
. To this end, we denote by
U_{\pm 1}
disks centered at
\pm 1
and, hopefully without causing any ambiguity, we denote their common radius by
\varrho
(we also used
\varrho
as a common radius of disks
U_{\pm\mathrm{i}}
in Section
3.1
). In fact, we set

(6.3.1)

\displaystyle\varrho=\tfrac{1}{2}(\varkappa x)_{+}^{-\frac{1}{2}},\quad(\varkappa x)_{+}=\max\{1,\varkappa x\}

(the constant
\frac{1}{2}
is there simply to ensure the bound
\varrho\leq\frac{1}{2}
for all choices
(\varkappa,x)
). By now, the construction of a local parametrix using parabolic cylinder functions is rather standard. A detailed explanation of this construction in our setting can be found in
[
30
]
. Below, we only provide the most salient points.

6.3.1  Model Local Parametrix

Let
Pc(z)
be a
2\times 2
matrix function that solves the following Riemann-Hilbert problem:

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 22
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
Pc(z)
such that

(1)

Pc(z)
is analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{Pc}
, where
\Gamma_{Pc}
is a contour depicted in Figure
23
;

(2)

Pc(z)
has continuous traces on
\Gamma_{Pc}\setminus\{0\}
that satisfy

\displaystyle Pc_{+}(z)=Pc_{-}(z)G_{Pc}(z),\quad z\in\Gamma_{Pc}\setminus\{0\},

where the jump matrices comprising
G_{Pc}(z)
are as on Figure
23
, and

s_{1}:=Ae^{-\mathrm{i}x+\varkappa x},\quad s_{2}:=-\overline{A}e^{\mathrm{i}x+\varkappa x};

(3)

it holds that
Pc(z)=(I+\mathcal{O}(1/z))z^{-\nu\sigma_{3}}
as
z\to\infty
.

Figure 23
.
Contour
\Gamma_{Pc}
, which consists of the coordinate axes and the ray
\arg(z)=-\pi/4
, and the jump matrices
G_{Pc}(z)
for RHP
22
.

RHP
22
can be explicitly solved with the help of parabolic cylinder functions, see for example
[
30
, Section 4.2.3]
(and recall that
B^{\mathbb{R}}=e^{-\varkappa x}
). Its explicit form is also given further below in Appendix
D
. There, we explain that the asymptotics of
Pc(z)
in RHP
22
(3) can be improved to the following formula:

(6.3.2)

\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}\frac{a_{s}^{(-\nu)}}{(-z^{2})^{s}}+\mathcal{O}_{n}\left(\frac{a_{n}^{(-\nu)}}{z^{2n}}\right)&\displaystyle-\frac{\alpha}{z}\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}\frac{a_{s}^{(\nu-1)}}{z^{2s}}+\alpha\mathcal{O}_{n}\left(\frac{a_{n}^{(\nu-1)}}{z^{2n+1}}\right)\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\\
\displaystyle\frac{\beta}{z}\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}\frac{a_{s}^{(-\nu-1)}}{(-z^{2})^{s}}+\beta\mathcal{O}_{n}\left(\frac{a_{n}^{(-\nu-1)}}{z^{2n+1}}\right)&\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}\frac{a_{s}^{(\nu)}}{z^{2s}}+\mathcal{O}_{n}\left(\frac{a_{n}^{(\nu)}}{z^{2n}}\right)\end{pmatrix}z^{-\nu\sigma_{3}}

for
|z|\geq\max\{5,|\nu+1|\}
and any non-negative integer
n
(in fact, we can and will make different choices for different entries of this matrix), where
\mathcal{O}_{n}(\cdot)
terms are independent of
\nu
and
z
,

(6.3.3)

\displaystyle a_{s}^{(\nu)}=\frac{(-1)^{s}(-\nu)_{2s}}{2^{s}s!},\quad\alpha=-e^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}\nu}\frac{\mathrm{i}}{s_{2}}\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{\Gamma(\nu)},\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha\beta=-\nu.

Moreover, upon recalling (
2.1.6
) and the definition of
\nu
in (
6.2.1
) as well as using the identity
\pi|\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+\mathrm{i}\frac{\varkappa x}{\pi}\right)|^{-2}=\cosh(\varkappa x)
, we obtain that

(6.3.4)

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{3}{2}\varkappa x}e^{-\mathrm{i}(x+\arg(p\Gamma(\nu))+\frac{\pi}{2})}.

Recall the definition of
\varphi(\lambda)
in (
6.0.1
). To set up the correspondence between
\lambda
and
z
planes, we introduce the following conformal mapping on
U_{1}
:

(6.3.5)

\displaystyle z_{1}(\lambda):=e^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{2}}\sqrt{4\varphi(\lambda)-2\mathrm{i}}=e^{\frac{3\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}}\frac{\lambda-1}{\sqrt{\lambda}},

where we fix the branches of the square roots so that
z_{1}(\lambda)=e^{\frac{3\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}}(\lambda-1)\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\lambda-1\right)\right)
as
\lambda\to 1
.
Recall that the contour
\Gamma_{W^{*}}\cap U_{1}
consists of
(1-\varrho,1]
and the curves on which the imaginary part of
2\varphi(\lambda)-\mathrm{i}
vanishes. Hence, one can easily see that
x^{1/2}z_{1}(\lambda)
maps it into
\Gamma_{Pc}
for any
x>0
with the segment
(1-\varrho,1]
mapped into the ray
\arg(z)=-\frac{\pi}{4}
.

6.3.2  Local Parametrix around
1

We now define a local parametrix
P^{(1)}(\lambda)
by setting

(6.3.6)

\displaystyle P^{(1)}(\lambda):=f(\lambda;x)^{\sigma_{3}}\sigma_{1}Pc\left(x^{\frac{1}{2}}z_{1}(\lambda)\right)\sigma_{1},\quad\lambda\in U_{1},

where
f(\lambda;x)^{\sigma_{3}}
is a holomorphic prefactor given by

(6.3.7)

\displaystyle f(\lambda;x):=\left(\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}\right)^{\nu}\left(x^{\frac{1}{2}}z_{1}(\lambda)\right)^{-\nu}=x^{-\frac{1}{2}\nu}e^{-\frac{3\pi\mathrm{i}}{4}\nu}\frac{\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}\nu}}{(\lambda+1)^{\nu}}

and all the branches above are principal. Then,
P^{(1)}(\lambda)
is a solution of the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 23
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
P^{(1)}(\lambda)
such that

(1)

P^{(1)}(\lambda)
is analytic in
U_{1}\setminus\Gamma^{*}
;

(2)

one-sided traces
P^{(1)}_{\pm}(\lambda)
are continuous on
U_{1}\cap\Gamma^{*}
and satisfy

\displaystyle P^{(1)}_{+}(\lambda)=P^{(1)}_{+}(\lambda)G_{W^{*}}(\lambda);

(3)

it holds for
\lambda\in\partial U_{1}
that

P^{(1)}(\lambda)=T^{(1)}(\lambda)\left(I+\frac{1}{x}\begin{pmatrix}O\big(\varrho^{-6}\big)&\mathcal{O}\big(\varrho^{-3}\big)\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\\
\mathcal{O}\big(\varrho^{-7}\big)&\mathcal{O}\big(\varrho^{-6}\big)\end{pmatrix}\right)\left(\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}\right)^{\nu\sigma_{3}},

where the constants in
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
terms are absolute, and

T^{(1)}(\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\displaystyle\frac{f^{*}(\lambda;x)}{\lambda-1}&1\end{pmatrix},\quad f^{*}(\lambda;x)=-\frac{\alpha x^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{f^{2}(\lambda;x)}\frac{\lambda-1}{z_{1}(\lambda)}.

RHP
23
(1) is obvious and RHP
23
(2) is straightforward to check. Thus, only RHP
23
(3) requires an explanation. First of all, we get from (
1.0.24
) and (
6.3.1
) that

x^{\frac{1}{2}}\varrho\geq\tfrac{1}{2}D_{5}^{\frac{1}{6}}x^{\frac{1}{3}}.

Since
|z_{1}(\lambda)|\sim\varrho
on
\partial U_{1}
and we are interested in
Pc(x^{\frac{1}{2}}z_{1}(\lambda))
, the error formula (
6.3.2
) applies on
\partial U_{1}
. It is easy to see from (
6.3.2
) that

f^{\sigma_{3}}\sigma_{1}Pc(z;\alpha,\beta)=\sigma_{1}Pc\big(z;\alpha f^{-2},\beta f^{2}\big)f^{-\sigma_{3}},

where we write
Pc(z)=Pc(z;\alpha,\beta)
to emphasize the dependence on the parameters
\alpha,\beta
. Clearly, it also holds that

f(\lambda;x)^{-\sigma_{3}}\big(x^{\frac{1}{2}}z_{1}(\lambda)\big)^{-\nu\sigma_{3}}\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{1}\left(\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}\right)^{\nu\sigma_{3}}.

Given (
6.3.1
), one can readily check that

\max\left\{\big|a_{1}^{(\nu)}\big|,\big|a_{1}^{(\nu-1)}\big|,\big|a_{1}^{(-\nu)}\big|\right\}\leq(\varkappa x)_{+}^{2}=(2\varrho)^{-4}.

Then, by using (
6.3.2
) with
n=0
in (2,1) entry and
n=1
in all other entries, we get that

P^{(1)}(\lambda)=\left(T^{(1)}(\lambda)+\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{O}\big(x^{-1}\varrho^{-6}\big)&\mathcal{O}\big(\beta f^{2}x^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varrho^{-1}\big)\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\\
\mathcal{O}\big(\alpha f^{-2}x^{-\frac{3}{2}}\varrho^{-7}\big)&\mathcal{O}\big(x^{-1}\varrho^{-6}\big)\end{pmatrix}\right)\left(\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}\right)^{\nu\sigma_{3}}

for
\lambda\in\partial U_{1}
, where the constants in
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
terms are absolute. Next, we get from the definition of
\nu
in (
6.2.1
) and from (
6.3.7
) that

|f(x,\lambda)|^{2}=x^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{\frac{3}{2}\varkappa x-\frac{1}{\pi}\varkappa x(\arg(\lambda)-2\arg(1+\lambda))}\left|\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}}{\lambda+1}\right|.

When
\lambda\in\partial U_{1}
, i.e.,
\lambda=1+\varrho e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}
, we have that

(6.3.8)

\displaystyle\arg(\lambda)-2\arg(1+\lambda)

\displaystyle=\arctan\left(\frac{\varrho\sin\theta}{1+\varrho\cos\theta}\right)-2\arctan\left(\frac{\varrho\sin\theta}{2+\varrho\cos\theta}\right)

(6.3.9)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\varrho^{2}\sin\theta\cos\theta}{(1+\varrho\cos\theta)(2+\varrho\cos\theta)}+\mathcal{O}(\varrho^{3})=\mathcal{O}(\varrho^{2}),

where the constant in
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
term is absolute. Furthermore, we get from (
6.3.3
) and (
6.3.4
) that
|\alpha|=e^{\frac{3}{2}\varkappa x}
and
|\beta|=|\nu|e^{-\frac{3}{2}\varkappa x}
. Hence,

P^{(1)}(\lambda)=\left(T^{(1)}(\lambda)+\frac{1}{x}\begin{pmatrix}O\big(\varrho^{-6}\big)&\mathcal{O}\big(\varrho^{-3}\big)\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\\
\mathcal{O}\big(\varrho^{-7}\big)&\mathcal{O}\big(\varrho^{-6}\big)\end{pmatrix}\right)\left(\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}\right)^{\nu\sigma_{3}}

for
\lambda\in\partial U_{1}
, where we used (
6.3.1
). We also have that
|f^{*}(\lambda;x)|
is bounded above by an absolute constant on
\partial U_{1}
. Hence, the
(2,1)
entry of
T^{(1)}(\lambda)
is of size
\varrho^{-1}
. This observation and the above asymptotic formula immediately yield RHP
23
(3).

6.3.3  Local Parametrix around
-1

It readily follows from (
6.1.18
) that a desired local parametrix in
U_{-1}
can be defined as

(6.3.10)

\displaystyle P^{(-1)}(\lambda):=\sigma_{1}P^{(1)}(-\lambda)\sigma_{1},\quad\lambda\in U_{-1}.

Then,
P^{(-1)}(\lambda)
is a solution of the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 24
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
P^{(-1)}(\lambda)
such that

(1)

P^{(-1)}(\lambda)
is analytic in
\in U_{-1}\setminus\Gamma^{*}
;

(2)

one-sided traces
P^{(-1)}_{\pm}(\lambda)
are continuous on
U_{-1}\cap\Gamma^{*}
and satisfy

\displaystyle P^{(-1)}_{+}(\lambda)=P^{(-1)}_{+}(\lambda)G_{W^{*}}(\lambda);

(3)

it holds for
\lambda\in\partial U_{-1}
that

P^{(-1)}(\lambda)=T^{(-1)}(\lambda)\left(I+\frac{1}{x}\begin{pmatrix}O\big(\varrho^{-6}\big)&\mathcal{O}\big(\varrho^{-7}\big)\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\\
\mathcal{O}\big(\varrho^{-3}\big)&\mathcal{O}\big(\varrho^{-6}\big)\end{pmatrix}\right)\left(\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}\right)^{\nu\sigma_{3}},

where the constants in
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
terms are absolute, and
T^{(-1)}(\lambda)=\sigma_{1}T^{(1)}(-\lambda)\sigma_{1}
.

6.4  Small Norm Problem

We look for a solution of RHP
21
in the form

(6.4.1)

W^{*}(\lambda)=V^{*}(\lambda)\begin{cases}T^{(\pm 1)}(\lambda)^{-1}P^{(\pm 1)}(\lambda),&\lambda\in U_{\pm 1},\\
\left(\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}\right)^{\nu\sigma_{3}},&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Then,
V^{*}(\lambda)
must solve the following meromorphic Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 25
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
V^{*}(\lambda)
such that

(1)

V^{*}(\lambda)
is analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus(\Gamma_{V}\cup\{\pm 1\})
, where
\Gamma_{V}
is depicted on Figure
24
;

(2)

one-sided traces
V^{*}_{\pm}(\lambda)
exist a.e. on
\Gamma_{V}
, belong to
L^{2}(\Gamma_{V})
, and satisfy

\displaystyle V^{*}_{+}(\lambda)=V^{*}_{-}(\lambda)G_{V^{*}}(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\Gamma_{V},

where

(6.4.2)

\displaystyle G_{V^{*}}(\lambda)=\begin{cases}\left(\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}\right)^{\nu\sigma_{3}}G_{W^{*}}(\lambda)\left(\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}\right)^{-\nu\sigma_{3}},&\lambda\in\Gamma_{V}\setminus(\partial U_{1}\cup\partial U_{-1}),\\
T^{(\pm 1)}(\lambda)^{-1}P^{(\pm 1)}(\lambda)\left(\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}\right)^{-\nu\sigma_{3}},&\lambda\in\partial U_{\pm 1};\end{cases}

(3)

it holds that

\displaystyle V^{*}(\lambda)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}I+\mathcal{O}(1/\lambda)&\text{as}\quad\lambda\to\infty,\\
P_{0}\,\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}\,\mathrm{i}^{-\sigma_{3}}(I+\mathcal{O}(\lambda))&\text{as}\quad\lambda\to 0;\end{array}\right.

(4)

V^{*}(\lambda)=(V^{*(1)}(\lambda),V^{*(2)}(\lambda))
has simple poles at
\lambda=\pm 1
with residues

(6.4.3)

\displaystyle\underset{\lambda=1}{\res}\,V^{*(1)}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-\alpha^{*}\,V^{*(2)}(1),

(6.4.4)

\displaystyle\underset{\lambda=-1}{\res}\,V^{*(2)}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{*}\,V^{*(1)}(-1),

where
\alpha^{*}:=-f^{*}(1;x)
.

Figure 24
.
The contour
\Gamma_{V}
for RHP
25
.

Following dressing technique of
[
8
]
, we look for a solution of RHP
25
in the form

(6.4.5)

\displaystyle V^{*}(\lambda)=(\lambda I+B)V(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}\lambda-1&0\\
0&\lambda+1\end{pmatrix}^{-1},

where the matrix
B
will be specified shortly and
V(\lambda)
solves the following (small norm) Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Riemann-Hilbert Problem 26
.

Find a
2\times 2
matrix function
V(\lambda)
such that

(1)

V(\lambda)
is analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{V}
;

(2)

one-sided traces
V_{\pm}(\lambda)
exist a.e. on
\Gamma_{V}
, belong to
L^{2}(\Gamma_{V})
, and satisfy

\displaystyle V_{+}(\lambda)=V_{-}(\lambda)G_{V}(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\Gamma_{L},

where

(6.4.6)

\displaystyle G_{V}(\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda-1&0\\
0&\lambda+1\end{pmatrix}^{-1}G_{V^{*}}(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}\lambda-1&0\\
0&\lambda+1\end{pmatrix};

(3)

it holds that
V(\lambda)=I+\mathcal{O}(1/\lambda)
as
\lambda\to\infty
.

Indeed, let us show that this is a small norm problem. For
\lambda\in\partial U_{1}
, we readily get from RHP
25
(2) and RHP
23
(3) that

(6.4.7)

\displaystyle G_{V}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=I+\frac{1}{x}\begin{pmatrix}O\big(\varrho^{-6}\big)&\frac{\lambda+1}{\lambda-1}\mathcal{O}\big(\varrho^{-3}\big)\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\\
\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}\mathcal{O}\big(\varrho^{-7}\big)&\mathcal{O}\big(\varrho^{-6}\big)\end{pmatrix}

(6.4.8)

\displaystyle=I+\frac{1}{x}\begin{pmatrix}O\big(\varrho^{-6}\big)&\mathcal{O}\big(\varrho^{-4}\big)\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\\
\mathcal{O}\big(\varrho^{-6}\big)&\mathcal{O}\big(\varrho^{-6}\big)\end{pmatrix}=I+\mathcal{O}\left(\big(x\varrho^{6}\big)^{-1}\right),

where the constants in
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
terms are absolute. Clearly, the same estimate holds on
\partial U_{-1}
.

Next, let us consider the unbounded subarc of
\Gamma_{V}
that lies in the first quadrant. On it,
G_{W^{*}}(\lambda)=S_{L_{1}}^{*}(\lambda)
, see Figures
22
and
24
. We shall call it
\Gamma_{L_{1}}
. There, we have that

G_{V}(\lambda)=I-\overline{A}e^{x(\varkappa+2\varphi(\lambda))}\left(\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}\right)^{2\nu-1}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}

by (
6.1.4
). Recall (
6.1.15
). One can easily check that the unbounded arc of the stationary contour to which
\Gamma_{L_{1}}
belongs can be parametrized as

(6.4.9)

\displaystyle\lambda=\xi+\mathrm{i}\eta,\quad\xi=s+1,\quad\eta=s\sqrt{\frac{1+s}{1-s}},\quad s\in(0,1).

These relations easily yield that

\operatorname{Im}(2\varphi(\lambda))=1\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{Re}(2\varphi(\lambda))=-\frac{s^{2}}{\sqrt{1-s^{2}}}

as well as that

\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}=\frac{s+\mathrm{i}s\sqrt{1-s^{2}}}{2-s^{2}}=\frac{s}{\sqrt{2-s^{2}}}\exp\left\{\mathrm{i}\arctan\big(\sqrt{1-s^{2}}\big)\right\}.

Therefore,

\left|e^{x(\varkappa+2\varphi(\lambda))}\left(\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}\right)^{2\nu-1}\right|=\exp\left\{\varkappa x\left(1-\frac{2}{\pi}\arctan\big(\sqrt{1-s^{2}}\big)\right)-\frac{xs^{2}}{\sqrt{1-s^{2}}}\right\}.

Recall that
|\lambda-1|\geq\varrho
for
\lambda\in\Gamma_{L_{1}}
. Hence, it is sufficient for our purposes to consider
s\in(\frac{1}{4}\varrho,1)
. In this case, due to monotonicity in
s
, we get that

(6.4.10)

\displaystyle\left|e^{x(\varkappa+2\varphi(\lambda))}\left(\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}\right)^{2\nu-1}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left\{\frac{\varkappa x}{2}-\frac{x\varrho^{2}}{16}\right\}\leq\exp\left\{\frac{1}{8\varrho^{2}}-\frac{x\varrho^{2}}{16}\right\}

(6.4.11)

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{-\frac{x\varrho^{2}}{16}\left(1-\frac{2}{x\varrho^{4}}\right)\right\}\leq\exp\left\{-\frac{x\varrho^{2}}{32}\right\},

where the last inequality is valid because

\frac{2}{x\varrho^{4}}=2^{5}\frac{(\varkappa x)_{+}^{2}}{x}\leq 2^{5}D_{5}^{-\frac{2}{3}}x^{-\frac{1}{3}}\leq\frac{1}{2}

by (
1.0.24
) and (
6.3.1
) with the last inequality being true when
D_{5}
is taken to be sufficiently large. Moreover, since

(6.4.12)

\displaystyle|d\lambda|=\frac{\sqrt{2-s^{2}}}{\sqrt{1+s}}\frac{ds}{(1-s)^{3/2}}

on
\Gamma_{L_{1}}
, we similarly have that

(6.4.13)

\int_{\Gamma_{L_{1}}}\left|e^{x(\varkappa+2\varphi(\lambda))}\left(\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}\right)^{2\nu-1}\right|^{2}|d\lambda|\leq\sqrt{2}e^{\varkappa x}\int_{0}^{1}\exp\left\{-\frac{x\varrho^{2}}{16\sqrt{1-s}}\right\}\frac{ds}{(1-s)^{3/2}}\\
=\frac{32\sqrt{2}}{x\varrho^{2}}\exp\left\{\varkappa x-\frac{x\varrho^{2}}{16}\right\}\leq\frac{32\sqrt{2}}{x\varrho^{2}}\exp\left\{-\frac{x\varrho^{2}}{32}\right\},

where, again, the last inequality holds if we take
D_{5}
sufficiently large. As
|A|^{2}\leq 2
by (
2.1.6
) and
x\varrho^{2}>1
by (
1.0.24
) and (
6.3.1
), we get that

(6.4.14)

\displaystyle\|G_{V}-I\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{L_{1}})\cap L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{L_{1}})}=\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-\frac{1}{32}x\varrho^{2}}\right)

where the constant in
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is absolute and the estimate holds for all
(\varkappa,x)
satisfying (
1.0.24
) as long as
D_{5}
is sufficiently large.

Let
\Gamma_{R_{1}}
be subarc of
\Gamma_{V}
on which
G_{W^{*}}(\lambda)=S_{R_{1}}^{*}(\lambda)
, see Figures
22
and
24
. On it, we have that

G_{V}(\lambda)=I+Ae^{-x(\varkappa+2\varphi(\lambda))}\left(\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}\right)^{1-2\nu}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}.

Similarly to
\Gamma_{S_{1}}
,
\Gamma_{R_{1}}
can be parametrized as

(6.4.15)

\displaystyle\lambda=\xi+\mathrm{i}\eta,\quad\xi=s+1,\quad\eta=(-s)\sqrt{\frac{1+s}{1-s}},\quad s\in(-1,0).

It now holds that

\operatorname{Im}(2\varphi(\lambda))=1\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{Re}(2\varphi(\lambda))=\frac{s^{2}}{\sqrt{1-s^{2}}}

as well as that

\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}=\frac{s-\mathrm{i}s\sqrt{1-s^{2}}}{2-s^{2}}=\frac{|s|}{\sqrt{2-s^{2}}}\exp\left\{\mathrm{i}\left(\pi-\arctan\big(\sqrt{1-s^{2}}\big)\right)\right\}.

Therefore, we again get that

(6.4.16)

\left|e^{-x(\varkappa+2\varphi(\lambda))}\left(\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}\right)^{1-2\nu}\right|=\\
\exp\left\{\varkappa x\left(1-\frac{2}{\pi}\arctan\big(\sqrt{1-s^{2}}\big)\right)-\frac{xs^{2}}{\sqrt{1-s^{2}}}\right\}\leq\exp\left\{-\frac{x\varrho^{2}}{32}\right\}

(we do not need a separate
L^{2}
estimate as
\Gamma_{R_{1}}
is of finite length). Since the estimates in the remaining three quadrants are essentially identical, we can now conclude that

(6.4.17)

\displaystyle\|G_{V}-I\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{V})\cap L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{V})}=\mathcal{O}\left(\big(x\varrho^{6}\big)^{-1}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(D_{5}^{-1}\right)

by (
1.0.24
) and (
6.3.1
), where the constant in
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is absolute. That is, RHP
26
is indeed a small norm problem and respectively is uniquely solvable for
D_{5}
sufficiently large. Here, one needs to again observe that
\Gamma_{V}
is in fact dependent on
(\varkappa x)_{+}
. Hence, to see that a single constant
D_{5}
is sufficient (and respectively the constant in
O(\cdot)
term in (
6.6.3
) is absolute) we use uniform boundedness of the norms of the corresponding Cauchy operators. For the explanation of this fact see again Appendix
A
.

6.5  Linear Prefactor

The goal of this subsection is to find matrix
B
so that (
6.4.5
) solves RHP
25
. This matrix is algebraically determined by residue conditions (
6.4.3
) and (
6.4.4
). It readily follows from (
6.4.5
) that

\displaystyle\left(V^{*(1)}(\lambda),V^{*(2)}(\lambda)\right)=(\lambda I+B)\left(\frac{1}{\lambda-1}V^{(1)}(\lambda),\frac{1}{\lambda+1}V^{(2)}(\lambda)\right),

where we denote by
V^{(1)}(\lambda)
and
V^{(2)}(\lambda)
the first and second columns of
V(\lambda)
, respectively. Recall respective definitions of
G_{V^{*}}(\lambda)
and
G_{V}(\lambda)
in RHP
25
(2) and RHP
26
(2). Symmetry relations (
6.1.18
) and the connection formula (
6.3.10
) yield that

G_{V}(\lambda)=\sigma_{1}G_{V}(-\lambda)\sigma_{1}.

As a solution of a small norm Riemann-Hilbert problem,
V(\lambda)
is unique and therefore
V(\lambda)=\sigma_{1}V(-\lambda)\sigma_{1}
. Write
V(\lambda)=I+E(\lambda)
. Then,

(6.5.1)

E(\lambda)=\sigma_{1}E(-\lambda)\sigma_{1}.

Write
E(\lambda)=(E^{(1)}(\lambda),E^{(2)}(\lambda))
. Then, we get from (
6.4.3
) and (
6.4.4
) that

(6.5.2)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(I+B)\left(\mathbf{e}_{1}+E^{(1)}(1)\right)=-\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha^{*}(I+B)\left(\mathbf{e}_{2}+E^{(2)}(1)\right),\\
(-I+B)\left(\mathbf{e}_{2}+E^{(2)}(-1)\right)=-\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha^{*}(-I+B)\left(\mathbf{e}_{1}+E^{(1)}(-1)\right),\end{cases}

where
\mathbf{e}_{1}
and
\mathbf{e}_{2}
are the standard coordinate vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Thus, symmetry (
6.5.1
) at
\lambda=1
now implies that

b_{11}=-b_{22}\quad\text{and}\quad b_{12}=-b_{21},\quad B=\begin{pmatrix}b_{11}&b_{12}\\
b_{21}&b_{22}\end{pmatrix}.

It follows from (
6.3.4
), (
6.3.5
), and (
6.3.7
) that

(6.5.3)

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}=-f^{*}(1;x)=-2\mathrm{i}e^{-\mathrm{i}(x-\kappa\ln x+\phi)}\quad\Rightarrow\quad|\alpha^{*}|=2,

where
\phi=-\kappa\ln 4+\arg(p\,\Gamma(\nu))
and one needs to recall that
\kappa\pi=\varkappa x
. Hence, we can further deduce that

(6.5.4)

\displaystyle b_{11}=-b_{22}=-\frac{4+(\alpha^{*})^{2}+o(1)}{4-(\alpha^{*})^{2}+o(1)},\quad b_{12}=-b_{21}=\frac{4\alpha^{*}+o(1)}{4-(\alpha^{*})^{2}+o(1)}

provided the denominator above is non-zero, where
o(1)
is as big as the size of
E(1)
, which is estimated in the next subsection.

6.6  Asymptotic Analysis

By the small norm theorem, the unique solution
V(\lambda)
of RHP
26
is given by

(6.6.1)

\displaystyle V(\lambda)

\displaystyle=I+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{V}}\frac{\rho(\lambda^{\prime})(G_{V}(\lambda^{\prime})-I)}{\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda}d\lambda^{\prime}

(6.6.2)

\displaystyle=I+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{V}}\frac{G_{V}(\lambda^{\prime})-I}{\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda}d\lambda^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{V}}\frac{(\rho(\lambda^{\prime})-I)(G_{V}(\lambda^{\prime})-I)}{\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda}d\lambda^{\prime}

for
\lambda\notin\Gamma_{L}
, where
\displaystyle{\rho(\lambda^{\prime}):=V_{-}(\lambda^{\prime})}
,
\lambda^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{V}
, which itself is a solution of the corresponding integral equation. Exactly as in (
2.3.10
), the small norm theorem and (
6.4.17
) imply that

(6.6.3)

\displaystyle\|\rho-I\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{L})}=\mathcal{O}\left(\big(x\varrho^{6}\big)^{-1}\right),

where the constant in
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
is absolute. Let, for brevity,
J_{V}(\lambda)=G_{V}(\lambda)-I
. Now, observe that

(6.6.4)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{V}}\frac{J_{V}(\lambda^{\prime})}{\lambda^{\prime}-1}d\lambda^{\prime}\right|\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|J_{V}(1+\varrho e^{\mathrm{i}t})|dt+\frac{\varrho}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|J_{V}(-1+\varrho e^{\mathrm{i}t})|dt

(6.6.5)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\Gamma^{*}\cap\Gamma_{V}}\frac{|d\lambda^{\prime}|}{|\lambda^{\prime}-1|^{2}}\right)^{1/2}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\Gamma^{*}\cap\Gamma_{V}}|J_{V}(\lambda^{\prime})|^{2}|d\lambda^{\prime}|\right)^{1/2},

where
\Gamma^{*}\cap\Gamma_{V}=\Gamma_{V}\setminus\partial(U_{1}\cup U_{-1})
. Using (
6.4.9
) and (
6.4.12
) one can easily show that the integral of
|\lambda-1|^{-2}
is of order
\varrho^{-1}
. Hence, we get from (
6.4.8
) and similarly to (
6.4.14
) we can conclude that

\left|\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{V}}\frac{J_{V}(\lambda^{\prime})}{\lambda^{\prime}-1}d\lambda^{\prime}\right|=\mathcal{O}\left(\big(x\varrho^{6}\big)^{-1}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-\frac{1}{32}x\varrho^{2}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\big(x\varrho^{6}\big)^{-1}\right).

It is not hard to see that the second integral in (
6.6.2
) can be estimated similarly with the help of Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and of (
6.6.3
) to yield

(6.6.6)

\displaystyle E(1)=V(1)-I=\mathcal{O}\left(\big(x\varrho^{6}\big)^{-1}\right).

Similarly, we now need to estimate the size of
V(0)
.
Using (
6.4.15
) and similar parametrizations of the remaining three parts of
\Gamma_{V}
around the origin, one can verify that
\Gamma_{V}
is tangential to the imaginary axis at the origin and that
J_{V}(\lambda)
vanishes exponentially there. Indeed, we can see from (
6.4.15
) and (
6.4.16
) that

\left|\frac{1}{\lambda}e^{-x(\varkappa+2\varphi(\lambda))}\left(\frac{\lambda-1}{\lambda+1}\right)^{1-2\nu}\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{\xi}}\exp\left\{x^{\frac{1}{3}}-\frac{x}{2\sqrt{\xi}}\right\}\leq 2\sqrt{2}\exp\left\{x^{\frac{1}{3}}-x\right\}

for
\lambda=\xi+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\Gamma_{R_{1}}
for all
\xi\in(0,\frac{1}{4})
. Since
\varrho\leq 1
and
x\varrho^{6}\geq D_{5}
by (
1.0.24
) and (
6.3.1
), it holds that

e^{x^{\frac{1}{3}}-x}\leq e^{-\frac{1}{2}x}\leq e^{-\frac{1}{2}(x\varrho^{6})}\leq\big(x\varrho^{6}\big)^{-1}.

In particular, we can see that from the above vanishing and Plemelj-Sokhotski formulae that
V_{+}(0)=V_{-}(0)
, which means that
V(0)
is well defined. Since the estimates of the integrals in (
6.6.2
) away from the origin can be estimated with the help of Cauchy-Schwarz inequality exactly as was done for
V(1)
, we deduce that

(6.6.7)

\displaystyle V(0)=I+\mathcal{O}\left(\big(x\varrho^{6}\big)^{-1}\right).

It follows from RHP
25
(3) and (
6.4.5
) that

\displaystyle P_{0}

\displaystyle=-BV(0)\mathrm{i}^{\sigma_{3}}\sigma_{1}.

We now get from the definition of
P_{0}
in RHP
1
(3) that

e^{u}=\frac{\cosh(\frac{1}{2}u)+\sinh(\frac{1}{2}u)}{\cosh(\frac{1}{2}u)-\sinh(\frac{1}{2}u)}=\frac{(1+o(1))b_{12}-(1+o(1))b_{11}}{(1+o(1))b_{12}+(1+o(1))b_{11}},

where
o(1)=\mathcal{O}\left(\big(x\varrho^{6}\big)^{-1}\right)
by (
6.6.7
). Observe that the denominators in both expressions of (
6.5.4
) are the same. Hence, we get from (
6.5.3
) that

(6.6.8)

\displaystyle e^{u}=\frac{4\alpha^{*}+4+(\alpha^{*})^{2}+o(1)}{4\alpha^{*}-4-(\alpha^{*})^{2}+o(1)}=\frac{1-\sin(x-\kappa\ln x+\phi)+o(1)}{1+\sin(x-\kappa\ln x+\phi)+o(1)},

where
o(1)=\mathcal{O}\big(\big(x\varrho^{6}\big)^{-1}\big)
by (
6.6.6
), provided the common denominator in (
6.5.4
) is non-zero. Taking logarithms on both sides of (
6.6.8
) finishes the proof of (
1.0.25
) when
\varepsilon=1
. Assume now that
\varepsilon=-1
. Recall that the transformation
(A,s^{\mathbb{R}},B^{\mathbb{R}})\mapsto(-\iota A,-s^{\mathbb{R}},\iota B^{\mathbb{R}})
corresponds to
p\mapsto-p
by (
2.1.4
). Respectively, we get from (
1.0.3
) that
\phi\mapsto\phi+\pi
. Therefore, in this case we get from (
2.1.5
) and (
6.6.8
) that

(6.6.9)

\displaystyle u\big(x,(A,s^{\mathbb{R}},B^{\mathbb{R}})\big)

\displaystyle=-\ln\left(\frac{1-\sin(x-\kappa\ln x+\phi+\pi)+o(1)}{1+\sin(x-\kappa\ln x+\phi+\pi)+o(1)}\right)

(6.6.10)

\displaystyle=\ln\left(\frac{1-\sin(x-\kappa\ln x+\phi)+o(1)}{1+\sin(x-\kappa\ln x+\phi)+o(1)}\right),

which finishes the proof of Theorem
1.5
.

Appendix A
On Cauchy Operators

Let
\Sigma
be a locally rectifiable oriented contour. Given
\tau\in\Sigma
and
\epsilon>0
, let
\Sigma(\tau,\epsilon):=\Sigma\cap\{|s-\tau|<\epsilon\}
. Then, for any
f\in L^{1}(\Sigma)
, the limit

(\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}f)(\tau)=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{\Sigma\setminus\Sigma(\tau,\epsilon)}\frac{f(s)}{\tau-s}ds

exists for almost every
\tau\in\Sigma
, see
[
11
, Theorem 4.14]
. In the main text we denoted by
\mathcal{C}
the Cauchy operator

(Cf)(\lambda):=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Sigma}\frac{f(s)}{s-\lambda}ds

and by
\mathcal{C}_{\pm}
the boundary Cauchy operators

(\mathcal{C}_{\pm}f)(\tau):=\lim_{\lambda\to\tau\in\Sigma^{\pm}}(Cf)(\lambda),

where
\Sigma^{-}
and
\Sigma^{+}
are the left-hand and right-hand sides of
\Sigma
according to the chosen orientation. It is well-known (Plemelj-Sokhotski formulae) that

2(\mathcal{C}_{\pm}f)(\tau)=\pm f(\tau)+(\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}f)(\tau),

see
[
11
, Equation (5.72)]
. Therefore,
L^{2}(\Sigma)
-boundedness of
\mathcal{C}_{\pm}
operators is equivalent to
L^{2}(\Sigma)
-boundedness of the singular integral operator
\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}
.

Contour
\Sigma
is called a Carleson contour or Alhfors-David contour if

C_{\Sigma}:=\sup_{\tau\in\Sigma}\sup_{\epsilon>0}\epsilon^{-1}|\Sigma(\tau,\epsilon)|<\infty,

where
|\Sigma(\tau,\epsilon)|
is the arclength of
\Sigma(\tau,\epsilon)
. It is a deep and by now classical result of David that
\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}
is a bounded operator from
L^{p}(\Sigma)
for any
p\in(1,\infty)
into itself if and only if
\Sigma
is a Carleson contour, see
[
11
, Theorem 4.17]
. Unfortunately, we could not locate in the literature a quantitative version of this result stating that the norm of
\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}
depends only on
p
and the constant
C_{\Sigma}
. Therefore, we need to provide some explanations.

Assume for simplicity that
\Sigma=\cup_{n=1}^{N}\Sigma_{n}
, where each
\Sigma_{n}
is a smooth closed Jordan arc (in particular, a Carleson arc) and any two arcs
\Sigma_{n}
and
\Sigma_{m}
,
n\neq m
, are either disjoint or have only one point in common which is an endpoint for both arcs. Then, the operator
\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}
can be viewed as a matrix operator acting on
\oplus L^{p}(\Sigma_{n})
with entries

\mathcal{S}_{nm}=\chi_{n}\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}\chi_{m}:L^{p}(\Sigma_{n})\to L^{p}(\Sigma_{m}),

where
\chi_{n}
is the characteristic function of
\Sigma_{n}
. Since any two arcs
\Sigma_{n}
and
\Sigma_{m}
can be embedded into an unbounded Carleson arc, say
\Sigma_{nm}
, the norm of
\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}
depends on
N
and the norms of
\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{nm}}
(
[
11
, Section 4.5]
is devoted to a more detailed explanation of this reduction in full generality of arbitrary Carleson contours). It is stated in lecture notes
[
17
, Remark following Theorem 2.24]
that if
\Sigma
is an unbounded Carleson arc, then

\|\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}\|_{L^{p}(\Sigma)\to L^{p}(\Sigma)}\leq\phi_{p}(C_{\Sigma}),

where
\phi_{p}(t)\geq 0
is a continuous, increasing function with
\phi_{p}(0)\geq 0
independent of
\Sigma
. The primary reference in
[
17
]
is
[
11
]
. As we already mentioned,
[
11
, Corollary 5.42]
unfortunately is not stated in this quantitative way (unlike the case of Lipschitz graphs, see
[
11
, Theorem 5.29]
) and only careful examination of the proof shows that the above claim is indeed correct. Even though the above discussion was concerned only with operators on functions, it can be naturally extended to matrix functions as well.

In the ucoming considerations, we use the following general principle: if
\sigma_{\alpha}(t)
,
t\in[a,b]
, are parametrizations of a family of bounded arcs depending on
\alpha
and there exists a constant
\delta>0
such that

|\arg(\sigma_{\alpha}^{\prime}(t_{1}))-\arg(\sigma_{\alpha}^{\prime}(t_{0}))|\leq\tfrac{\pi}{3}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\operatorname{Re}e^{\mathrm{i}\arg(\sigma_{\alpha}^{\prime}(t_{1}))-\mathrm{i}\arg(\sigma_{\alpha}^{\prime}(t_{0}))}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}

for all
|t_{1}-t_{0}|\leq\delta
, then these arcs have uniformly bounded Carleson constants because

\frac{\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}|\sigma_{\alpha}^{\prime}(t)|dt}{\big|\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\sigma_{\alpha}^{\prime}(t)dt\big|}\leq\frac{\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}|\sigma_{\alpha}^{\prime}(t)|dt}{\big|\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}|\sigma_{\alpha}^{\prime}(t)|\operatorname{Re}e^{\mathrm{i}\arg(\sigma_{\alpha}^{\prime}(t))-\mathrm{i}\arg(\sigma_{\alpha}^{\prime}(t_{0}))}dt\big|}\leq 2,

where, trivially, the top integral on the left represents the arclength between
\sigma(t_{0})
and
\sigma(t_{1})
while the integral on the bottom represents the distance between these points (it is also easy to show that for bounded arcs it is enough to consider
t_{0},t_{1}
that are sufficiently close to each other to determine finiteness of the Carleson constant). Clearly, the above property holds as long as
\{\arg(\sigma_{\alpha}^{\prime}(t))\}
is a uniformly equicontinuous family of functions on the parametrizing interval. Furthermore, by Arzelà-Ascoli theorem, the latter property holds if
\arg(\sigma_{\alpha}^{\prime}(t))
converges uniformly to
\arg(\sigma_{\alpha_{0}}^{\prime}(t))
when
\alpha\to\alpha_{0}
.

We needed bounds on Cauchy operators on four contours:
\Gamma
in the proof of Theorem
1.1
, see Figure
4
;
\Gamma_{Z}
in the proof of Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
, see Figure
6
;
\Gamma_{R}
in the proof of Theorem
1.4
, see Figure
18
; and
\Gamma_{V}
in the proof of Theorem
1.5
, see Figure
24
. One can readily compute the Carleson constant
C_{\Sigma}
for a circle, which is
\pi
(hence, Carleson constant of any subrac of a circle is at most
\pi
). Since
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{Z}
separate into straight line segments and subarcs of circles, they are clearly Carleson contours.
\Gamma_{V}
consists of a stationary contour of
2\varphi(\lambda)
, see Figure
21
, and circles of variable radii around
\pm 1
(the part of the stationary contour inside the circles must be removed). It is clear that this stationary contour is a Carleson contour (one can even use the explicit expression for
\varphi(\lambda)
to estimate the Carleson constant). Hence, the Carleson constant of
\Gamma_{V}
is bounded above independently of the radii of the circles.

It only remains to discuss
\Gamma_{R}
. It consists of the imaginary axis, subarcs of the unit circle, subintervals of the real line, arcs
\gamma_{i}^{in},\gamma_{i}^{out}
, and arcs connecting the endpoints of
\gamma_{i}^{in},\gamma_{i}^{out}
to the real line, see Figure
18
. Clearly, only the latter two groups need to be studied. In fact, we only need to explain why the two arcs of
\Gamma_{R}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}\cap\{|\lambda|<1\}
have uniformly bounded Carleson constants as the remaining arcs are obtained by various reflections of these two. Recall Section
5.4.2
. By construction, the conformal maps

\zeta(\lambda;\alpha):=h_{1}(\lambda)^{\frac{2}{3}},

parametrized by
\alpha\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
, form a continuous family. Thus, we only need to study the limits as
\alpha\to 0
and
\alpha\to\frac{\pi}{2}
. We concentrate on the latter as the former can be described similarly. These maps take the arcs connecting
\gamma_{1}^{in}
to the real line into horizontal segments. Hence, we can use

\sigma_{\alpha}(t)=\zeta^{-1}(t;\alpha)

as functions parametrizing these arcs (here, superscript
-1
denotes the inverse function). Since
V\to 0
when
\alpha\to\frac{\pi}{2}
, these horizontal segments converge to
[0,2^{-\frac{2}{3}}]
(clearly, the above argument about finiteness of Carleson constants can be easily modified to include changing but converging parametrizing intervals). Observe that

\arg(\sigma_{\alpha}^{\prime}(t))=-\arg(\zeta^{\prime}(\sigma_{\alpha}(t);\alpha)).

Thus, we need to show that the above right-hand sides converge uniformly as
\alpha\to\frac{\pi}{2}
. Recall that the functions
h_{1}(\lambda)
themselves converge uniformly, including their derivatives, to
-\theta(\lambda)-\frac{1}{2}
by (
5.4.6
) and (
5.1.3
). From this, we readily deduce the desired conclusion.

The above argument needs a slight modification for the arcs
\gamma_{1}^{in}
