as they collapse into a point. Thus, we first need to perform some affine transformations (which, of course, do not change Carleson constants). The functions
V^{-1}h_{1}(\lambda)
from (
5.4.4
) map
\gamma_{1}^{in}
into a fixed segment
e^{-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{8}}[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\csc(\frac{\pi}{8})]
. Thus, we parametrize
\gamma_{1}^{in}
by the inverses of these functions. One can readily compute using (
5.1.3
) that
(\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha)^{2}/V
has a limit as
\alpha\to\frac{\pi}{2}
. Moreover,

\frac{1}{V}h_{1}(\lambda)=\frac{(\overline{a}+a)^{2}}{V}\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\int_{0}^{z}\frac{\sqrt{((\overline{a}+a)u+a-\overline{a})((\overline{a}+a)u+2a)}}{((\overline{a}+a)u+a)^{2}}\sqrt{u(u+1)}du,

where
z=\frac{\lambda-a}{\overline{a}+a}
and
\overline{a}+a=2\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}-\alpha)
. When
\alpha\to\frac{\pi}{2}
, the above functions converge uniformly (including their derivatives) to

c\int_{0}^{z}\sqrt{u(u+1)}du

for some constant
c
, where branch of the square root is chosen so that the integrand is analytic in the vertical strip
-1<\operatorname{Re}(u)<0
(notice that under the above substitution,
-\overline{a}
corresponds to
-1
). Now, we can again appeal to uniform convergence of the parametrizations to conclude uniform boundedness of the Carleson constants.

Appendix B
A Curious Determinantal Identity

In this appendix, we prove a proposition that was used to derive formula (
4.3.10
). The proof below uses a combinatorial identity

(B.0.1)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m}\binom{m+n}{k}x^{k}y^{m-k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\binom{-n}{k}(-x)^{k}(x+y)^{m-k}

(B.0.2)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^{n-1}\binom{-k-1}{n-1}x^{k}(x+y)^{m-k},

where the first equality can be found, for instance, in
[
22
, Equation (5.19)]
, and the second one is a straightforward consequence of the definition of the generalized binomial coefficient used with integer parameters.

Proposition B.1
.

Let
T=[t_{j-i}]_{i,j=1}^{N}
and
H=[h_{i+j-1}]_{i,j=1}^{N}
be a Toeplitz and a Hankel matrix, respectively, with
t_{k}=0
when
k<0
and
h_{k}=0
when
k>N
(in all matrices,
i
stands for the row index and
j
for the column one). Further, let

L=\left[\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)^{i+j-1}(-1)^{i-1}\binom{-j}{i-1}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}

for some constant
\ell
. Assume that
\det(T-HL)\neq 0
. If
\vec{v}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{N})^{\mathsf{T}}
is the solution of
(T-HL)\vec{v}=H\vec{e}_{1}
, where
\vec{e}_{1}=(1,0,\ldots,0)^{\mathsf{T}}
, then

1+\sum_{i=1}^{N}v_{i}\ell^{i}=\frac{\det(T+HL)}{\det(T-HL)}.

Proof.

Let
V
be the matrix whose first row is given by
\big(\ell,\ell^{2},\ldots,\ell^{N}\big)
and whose remaining entries are all zero. Then

\displaystyle 1+\sum_{i=1}^{N}v_{i}\ell^{i}

\displaystyle=\vec{e}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\big(I+V(T-HL)^{-1}H\big)\vec{e}_{1}

\displaystyle=\det\big(I+V(T-HL)^{-1}H\big)=\frac{\det(T+H(V-L))}{\det(T-HL)}.

Thus, we need to show that
\det(T+H(V-L))=\det(T+HL)
, i.e., that the matrix
T+HL
can be obtained by row and column operations from
T+H(V-L)
. We shall perform the following operations:

•

for
i
from
N
to
2
(starting with the last row and ending with the second row) add
\ell
multiple of the
i
-th row to the
(i-1)
-st one;

•

for
j
from
N-1
to
1
(starting with the penultimate column and ending with the first one) subtract
\ell
multiple of the
j
-th column from the
(j+1)
-st one.

Set
L_{i,j}:=I+\ell E_{i,j}
, where
E_{i,j}
is the matrix with all zero entries except for
(i,j)
-th entry, which is
1
. Since
I-\ell E_{i,j}=L_{i,j}^{-1}
, performing the suggested operations, is of course, equivalent to the multiplication on the left by
L_{1,2}\cdots L_{N-1,N}
and on the right by
L_{N-1,N}^{-1}\cdots L_{1,2}^{-1}
. That is, we would like to show that

L_{1,2}\cdots L_{N-1,N}(T+H(V-L))L_{N-1,N}^{-1}\cdots L_{1,2}^{-1}=T+HL.

It is straightforward to verify that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}L_{1,2}\cdots L_{N-1,N}T&=TL_{1,2}\cdots L_{N-1,N},\\
L_{1,2}\cdots L_{N-1,N}H&=HL_{N,N-1}\cdots L_{2,1}.\end{array}\right.

For instance, when
N=3
, one can readily see that

\begin{pmatrix}1&\ell&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&\ell\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}T=\begin{pmatrix}t_{0}&t_{1}+\ell t_{0}&t_{2}+\ell t_{1}+\ell^{2}t_{0}\\
0&t_{0}&t_{1}+\ell t_{0}\\
0&0&t_{0}\end{pmatrix}\\
=T\begin{pmatrix}1&\ell&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&\ell\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}.

and

\begin{pmatrix}1&\ell&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&\ell\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}H=\begin{pmatrix}h_{1}+\ell h_{2}+\ell^{2}h_{3}&h_{2}+\ell h_{3}&h_{3}\\
h_{2}+\ell h_{3}&h_{3}&0\\
h_{3}&0&0\end{pmatrix}\\
=H\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&\ell&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
\ell&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}.

Hence, to prove the proposition, we need to show that

L_{N,N-1}\cdots L_{2,1}(V-L)L_{N-1,N}^{-1}\cdots L_{1,2}^{-1}=L.

Observe that

L_{N,N-1}\cdots L_{2,1}V=\big[\ell^{i+j-1}\big]_{i,j=1}^{N}.

Therefore, the desired equality can be rewritten as

\big[\ell^{i+j-1}\big]_{i,j=1}^{N}=L_{N,N-1}\cdots L_{2,1}L+LL_{1,2}\cdots L_{N-1,N}.

The first summand on the right-hand side of the equality above represents successive additions of the
(i-1)
-st row multiplied by
\ell
to the
i
-th one starting with
i=2
. Thus, its
(i,j)
-th entry is given by

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{i}\ell^{i-k}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)^{k+j-1}(-1)^{k-1}\binom{-j}{k-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\ell^{i+j-1}}{2^{j}}\sum_{k=0}^{i-1}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\binom{-j}{k}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)^{i+j-1}\sum_{k=0}^{i-1}\binom{i+j-1}{k},

where we used the equality of the first and middle terms in (
B.0.1
) with
x=y=\tfrac{1}{2}
,
m=i-1
, and
n=j
. On the other hand, the second summand represents successive additions of the
(j-1)
-st column multiplied by
\ell
to the
j
-th one starting with
j=2
. Hence, its
(i,j)
-th entry is given by

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{j}\ell^{j-k}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)^{k+i-1}(-1)^{i-1}\binom{-k}{i-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\ell^{i+j-1}}{2^{i}}\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}(-1)^{i-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\binom{-k-1}{i-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)^{i+j-1}\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}\binom{i+j-1}{k},

where we used the equality of the first and last terms in (
B.0.1
) with
x=y=\tfrac{1}{2}
,
m=j-1
, and
n=i
. Since

\sum_{k=0}^{i-1}\binom{i+j-1}{k}+\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}\binom{i+j-1}{i+j-1-k}=\sum_{k=0}^{i+j-1}\binom{i+j-1}{k}=2^{i+j-1},

the claim of the proposition follows.
∎

Appendix C
On Elliptic Moduli

Recall (
1.0.18
). It is well known, see
[
18
, (19.5.1-2)]
, that

(C.0.1)

K(k)=\frac{\pi}{2}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{{\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)_{m}}{\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)_{m}}}{m!\;m!}{k}^{2m}\quad\text{and}\quad E(k)=\frac{\pi}{2}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{{\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)_{m}}{\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)_{m}}}{m!\;m!}{k}^{2m},

where the series converge for
|k|<1
. Recall also (
1.0.17
) and (
1.0.19
).

Lemma C.1
.

We have that

\varkappa\leq\mathsf{k}^{2}\mathsf{K}\quad\text{and}\quad 1-\varkappa\leq(\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{2}\mathsf{K}.

Moreover, it holds as
\varkappa\to 0
that

\mathsf{k}=2\left(\frac{\varkappa}{\pi}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\varkappa}{\pi}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}-\frac{5}{16}\left(\frac{\varkappa}{\pi}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\varkappa^{\frac{7}{2}}\right).

Proof.

By Lemma
5.1
, we have that

\varkappa=\mathsf{k}^{2}\mathsf{K}+(E(\mathsf{k})-K(\mathsf{k})).

Since
E(k)\leq\tfrac{\pi}{2}\leq K(k)
, see (
C.0.1
) and
[
18
, Equation (19.9.1)]
, the first claim of the lemma follows. On the other hand, we have that

1-\varkappa=1-E(\mathsf{k})+(\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{2}\mathsf{K}.

Since
E(k)\geq 1
, see again
[
18
, Equation (19.9.1)]
, we get the second claimed bound. Moreover, we readily get from (
C.0.1
) that

\varkappa=\frac{\pi}{2}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)_{m}}{m!}\right)^{2}\frac{2m}{(2m-1)^{2}}\mathsf{k}^{2m}.

By the Lagrange inversion theorem, one can write
\mathsf{k}^{2}
as a power series in
\varkappa
. A direct computation yields

\mathsf{k}^{2}=4\frac{\varkappa}{\pi}-2\left(\frac{\varkappa}{\pi}\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{\varkappa}{\pi}\right)^{3}+\mathcal{O}\left(\varkappa^{4}\right).

Since
\sqrt{1-y}=1-\tfrac{1}{2}y-\tfrac{1}{8}y^{2}+\mathcal{O}(y^{3})
, the last claim of the lemma follows.
∎

Recall (
1.0.21
) and (
5.0.2
).

Lemma C.2
.

Assuming (
1.0.21
) and
x\geq e
, there exists an absolute constant
c_{0}
such that

c_{0}D_{\delta}\leq x\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\leq|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}^{-1}e^{\varkappa x}\min\left\{(\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{*})^{2},x^{-1/3}(\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{**})^{2}\right\}.

Proof.

Let
k_{*}\in(0,1)
be such that
\ln x<\tfrac{1}{20}x
for
x\geq\tfrac{4}{k_{*}}
. It follows from
[
18
, Equation (19.9.2)]
that

\mathsf{K}\leq\frac{5}{4}\ln\frac{4}{\mathsf{k}^{\prime}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathsf{K}\leq\frac{1}{4\mathsf{k}^{\prime}},\quad\mathsf{k}^{\prime}\in(0,k_{*}].

Recall that
x\geq e
and
D_{\delta}\geq 4D_{4}\geq 1
(we can always assume that
D_{4}\geq\frac{1}{4}
). By using these estimates, the upper estimate in (
1.0.21
), Lemma
C.1
, and second inequality in the above display, we get that

\frac{1}{3}\ln\frac{1}{x}\leq\frac{1}{3}\ln\left(\frac{D_{\delta}\ln x}{x}\right)\leq\ln(1-\varkappa)\leq 2\ln\mathsf{k}^{\prime}+\ln\mathsf{K}\leq\ln\frac{\mathsf{k}^{\prime}}{4}.

We now get from Lemma
C.1
, the upper bound in (
1.0.21
), the first estimate of
\mathsf{K}
above, and the preceding inequality that

x\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\geq x\mathsf{k}^{3}\frac{(1-\varkappa)^{3}}{\mathsf{K}}\geq\mathsf{k}^{3}\frac{D_{\delta}\ln x}{\mathsf{K}}\geq\frac{4\mathsf{k}^{3}}{5}\frac{D_{\delta}\ln x}{\ln(4/\mathsf{k}^{\prime})}\geq\frac{12(1-k_{*}^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}{5}D_{\delta}.

On the other hand, when
\mathsf{k}^{\prime}\in[k_{*},1)
, we get from Lemma
C.1
, an estimate
\mathsf{K}\geq\tfrac{\pi}{2}
, see (
C.0.1
), and the lower bound in (
1.0.21
) that

x\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\geq(\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{6}\sqrt{\mathsf{K}}\,x\varkappa^{\frac{3}{2}}\geq(\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{6}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}x\varkappa^{\frac{3}{2}}\geq k_{*}^{6}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}D_{\delta},

which finishes the proof of the estimate
c_{0}D_{\delta}\leq x\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
.

It is a simple computation using (
5.0.2
) to verify that the second desired inequality is equivalent to showing that

(\mathsf{k}\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{2}\leq x^{-1}e^{\varkappa x-\ln|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}}\quad\text{and}\quad(\mathsf{k}\mathsf{k}^{\prime})^{\frac{8}{3}}\leq x^{-\frac{4}{3}}e^{\varkappa x-\ln|s^{\mathbb{R}}|_{+}}.

Since
\mathsf{k}\mathsf{k}^{\prime}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
, the lower bound in (
1.0.21
) yields the desired inequalities (recall that we always assume that
x\geq 1
and we always can adjust the constant
D_{4}
as it was simply a fixed constant sufficiently large for our purposes).
∎

Let
\sd(z,k)
be the Jacobian elliptic function we used in Theorem
1.4
.

Lemma C.3
.

It holds as
k\to 0^{+}
for all real
z
that

\sd(z,k)=\sin\left(z-\frac{k^{2}z}{4}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(k^{4}z\right).

Proof.

We get from (
C.0.1
) that

K(k)=\frac{\pi}{2}\left(1+\frac{k^{2}}{4}+\mathcal{O}\big(k^{4}\big)\right)\quad\text{and}\quad K(k^{\prime})=-\ln k+2\ln 2+\mathcal{O}\big(k^{2}\ln k\big)

as
k\to 0
. Hence, it holds that

(C.0.2)

\displaystyle q^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi}{2}\frac{K(k^{\prime})}{K(k)}\right\}=\exp\left\{\ln k-2\ln 2+\mathcal{O}\big(k^{2}\ln k\big)\right\}

(C.0.3)

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{4}\big(1+\mathcal{O}(k^{2}\ln k)\big)\quad\text{as}\quad k\to 0

(
q
is the nome corresponding to the elliptic modulus
k
; when
k\in(0,1)
, it holds that
q\in(0,1)
). In particular, we have that

\frac{2\pi q^{\frac{1}{2}}}{K(k)\,k\,k^{\prime}}=1+\mathcal{O}(k^{2}\ln k)\quad\text{as}\quad k\to 0^{+}.

It is known, see
[
18
, Equation (22.11.5)]
, that

(C.0.4)

\displaystyle\sd\left(z,k\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi}{K(k)\,k\,k^{\prime}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}q^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\sin\left((2n+1)\zeta\right)}{1+q^{2n+1}},\quad\zeta=\frac{\pi z}{2K(k)},

when
q\in(0,1)
. Hence, we get that

(C.0.5)

\displaystyle\sd\left(z,k\right)

\displaystyle=\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(k^{2}\ln k\right)\right)\left[\sin\left(\frac{\pi z}{2K(k)}\right)+\mathcal{O}(q)\right]

(C.0.6)

\displaystyle=\sin\left(\frac{\pi z}{2K(k)}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(k^{2}\ln k\right)\quad\text{as}\quad k\to 0^{+}.

One can readily compute from (
C.0.1
) that

\frac{\pi}{2K(k)}=1-\frac{1}{4}k^{2}+\mathcal{O}\big(k^{4}\big)\quad\text{as}\quad k\to 0^{+}.

Trivially,

\sin\left(\frac{\pi z}{2K(k)}\right)=\sin\left(z-\frac{k^{2}z}{4}\right)\cos\left(\mathcal{O}\big(k^{4}z\big)\right)-\cos\left(z-\frac{k^{2}z}{4}\right)\sin\left(\mathcal{O}\big(k^{4}z\big)\right).

To finish the proof of the lemma, it remains to observe that

\sin\left(\mathcal{O}\big(k^{4}z\big)\right)=\mathcal{O}\big(k^{4}z\big)\quad\text{and}\quad\cos\left(\mathcal{O}\big(k^{4}z\big)\right)=1+\mathcal{O}\left(k^{4}z\right).\qed

The following theorem allowed us to connect the behavior of solutions in Theorems
1.4
and
1.5
.

Theorem C.4
.

As in the main text, let
\pi\kappa=\varkappa x=\tfrac{1}{2}\ln(|p(\varkappa,x)|^{2}-1)
. Assume that
\pi\kappa=Dx^{1/3}
for some
D>0
. It holds that

(C.0.7)

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}^{\prime}\sd\left(x-2\kappa\mathsf{K}^{\prime},\mathsf{k}\right)=\varepsilon\sin(x-\kappa\ln x+\phi)+\mathcal{O}\left(x^{-1/3}D^{2}\ln x\right)

as
x\to\infty
, where, as in (
1.0.3
),
\phi=-\kappa\ln 4+\arg\left(p\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+\mathrm{i}\kappa\right)\right)
, and
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
term is independent of
D
and
x
.

Proof.

Let us write
z=x-2\kappa\mathsf{K}^{\prime}
. Since
\varkappa=\pi Dx^{-\frac{2}{3}}
, we see from Lemma
C.1
that
\mathsf{k}\to 0
and it holds that

\mathsf{k}^{2}=\frac{4}{\pi}\varkappa+\mathcal{O}(\varkappa^{2})=\mathcal{O}(Dx^{-\frac{2}{3}}).

We also get from
[
18
, Equation (19.12.1)]
that

-2\mathsf{K}^{\prime}=2\ln\mathsf{k}-2\ln 4+\mathcal{O}\big(\mathsf{k}^{2}\ln\mathsf{k}\big).

Therefore, we have that

\frac{\mathsf{k}^{2}z}{4}=\kappa+\mathcal{O}(x^{-\frac{1}{3}}D^{2}\ln x)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{k}^{4}z=\mathcal{O}(x^{-\frac{1}{3}}D^{2}).

If we define
\phi_{*}
via the relation

z-\frac{\mathsf{k}^{2}z}{4}=x-\kappa\ln x+\phi_{*},\quad

then it follows from Lemma
C.3
that

\sd\left(x-2\kappa\mathsf{K}^{\prime},\mathsf{k}\right)=\sin(x-\kappa\ln x+\phi_{*})+\mathcal{O}(x^{-\frac{1}{3}}D^{2})

as
x\to\infty
. Moreover, it holds that

(C.0.8)

\displaystyle\phi_{*}

\displaystyle=\kappa\ln x+2\kappa\ln\mathsf{k}-2\kappa\ln 4-\kappa+\mathcal{O}\big(x^{-\frac{1}{3}}D^{2}\ln x\big)

(C.0.9)

\displaystyle=-\kappa\ln 4+\kappa\ln\kappa-\kappa+\mathcal{O}\big(x^{-\frac{1}{3}}D^{2}\ln x\big)

as
x\to\infty
. Next, we choose

(C.0.10)

\displaystyle\arg\left(p\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+\mathrm{i}\kappa\right)\right)=\arg(p)+\operatorname{Im}\left(\log\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+\mathrm{i}\kappa\right)\right).

By (
1.0.5
), one has that

p=\varepsilon e^{\varkappa x}-\frac{\mathrm{i}s^{\mathbb{R}}}{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-\varkappa x}\right)\quad\Rightarrow\quad\arg(p)=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}\pi+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\pi\kappa})

On the other hand,
[
18
, Equation (5.11.1) and Table 24.2.1]
give that

(C.0.11)

\displaystyle\log\Gamma(s)=\left(s-\frac{1}{2}\right)\log s-s+\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi)+\frac{1}{12s}+\mathcal{O}\left(s^{-3}\right),\quad s\to\infty.

Applying this formula with
s=\tfrac{1}{2}+\mathrm{i}\kappa
after some computation gives

\operatorname{Im}\left(\log\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+\mathrm{i}\kappa\right)\right)=\kappa\ln\kappa-\kappa+\frac{1}{24\kappa}+\mathcal{O}\left(\kappa^{-3}\right).

Altogether, we have shown that there is a choice of the argument such that

\arg\left(p\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+\mathrm{i}\kappa\right)\right)=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}\pi+\kappa\ln\kappa-\kappa+\mathcal{O}\left(\kappa^{-1}\right).

Hence,
\phi_{*}=-\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}\pi+\phi+\mathcal{O}(x^{-\frac{1}{3}}D^{2}\ln x)
and therefore

\sin(x-\kappa\ln x+\phi_{*})=\varepsilon\sin(x-\kappa\ln x+\phi)+\mathcal{O}\big(x^{-\frac{1}{3}}D^{2}\ln x\big)

as
x\to\infty
. Since
\mathsf{k}^{\prime}=\sqrt{1-\mathsf{k}^{2}}=1+\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{k}^{2})=1+\mathcal{O}(Dx^{-\frac{2}{3}})
, this finishes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Appendix D
On Parabolic Cylinder Parametrix

Given a parabolic cylinder function
D_{\mu}(w)
, it can be written as a Kummer
U(a,b,z)
confluent hypergeometric function in the following way

D_{\mu}(w)=2^{\mu/2}U\big(-\tfrac{\mu}{2},\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{w^{2}}{2}\big)e^{-\frac{1}{4}w^{2}},

see
[
18
, Equation (12.7.14)]
. Therefore, it admits a series expansion of the form

(D.0.1)

\displaystyle D_{\mu}(w)=w^{\mu}e^{-\frac{1}{4}w^{2}}\left(\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}\frac{a_{s}^{(\mu)}}{w^{2s}}+R_{n}(w;\mu)\right),\quad a_{s}^{(\mu)}:=\frac{(-1)^{s}(-\mu)_{2s}}{2^{s}s!},

for any
n\geq 0
, see
[
18
, Equation (13.7.4)]
. We shall now use notation and material of
[
18
, §13(ii)]
. Assume that

\sigma:=\frac{|1+2\mu|}{|w|^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Set
\rho:=\frac{1}{4}|\mu^{2}+\mu+1|+\frac{\sigma(4+\sigma)}{4(1-\sigma)^{2}}
. Then, for
\operatorname{Re}(w)\geq 0
, it holds that

|R_{n}(w;\mu)|\leq\frac{2C_{n}(\mu)}{1-\sigma}\frac{|a_{n}^{(\mu)}|}{|w|^{2n}}\exp\left\{\frac{4C_{1}(\mu)}{1-\sigma}\frac{\rho}{|w|^{2}}\right\},

where constants
C_{n}(\mu)
satisfy

|C_{n}(\mu)|\leq\left(\sqrt{\pi}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{n+2}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{n+1}{2})}+\frac{2\sigma n}{1+\sqrt{1-4\sigma^{2}}}\right)\frac{2^{n/2}}{(1+\sqrt{1-4\sigma^{2}})^{n/2}}.

Clearly, it holds that
|C_{n}(\mu)|\leq C_{n}^{\prime}
, where
C_{n}^{\prime}
is independent of
\mu
and
w
. Moreover, we have that
4\rho\leq|\mu|(|\mu|+1)+10
. Therefore, if we suppose that

(D.0.2)

\displaystyle|w|\geq\max\{5,|\mu|\}\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{Re}(w)\geq 0,

then
\sigma\leq\frac{1}{2}
and there exist constants
A_{n}
, independent of
\mu
and
w
, such that

(D.0.3)

\displaystyle|R_{n}(w;\mu)|\leq A_{n}\frac{|a_{n}^{(\mu)}|}{|w|^{2n}}.

Now, the model parametrix
Pc(z)e^{\frac{1}{4}z^{2}\sigma_{3}}
, used in Section
6.3
, is explicitly given by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}l@{}}\begin{pmatrix}D_{-\nu}\left(ze^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{2}}\right)e^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}\nu}{2}}&-\alpha D_{\nu-1}(z)\\
\mathrm{i}\beta D_{-\nu-1}\left(ze^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{2}}\right)e^{\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}\nu}{2}}&D_{\nu}(z)\end{pmatrix},&\arg(z)\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{4},0\right),\\
\begin{pmatrix}D_{-\nu}\left(ze^{-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{2}}\right)e^{-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}\nu}{2}}&-\alpha D_{\nu-1}(z)\\
-\mathrm{i}\beta D_{-\nu-1}\left(ze^{-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{2}}\right)e^{-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}\nu}{2}}&D_{\nu}(z)\end{pmatrix},&\arg(z)\in\left(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right),\\
\begin{pmatrix}D_{-\nu}\left(ze^{-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{2}}\right)e^{-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}\nu}{2}}&\alpha D_{\nu-1}\left(ze^{-\pi\mathrm{i}}\right)e^{\pi\mathrm{i}\nu}\\
-\mathrm{i}\beta D_{-\nu-1}\left(ze^{-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{2}}\right)e^{-\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}\nu}{2}}&D_{\nu}\left(ze^{-\pi\mathrm{i}}\right)e^{\pi\mathrm{i}\nu}\end{pmatrix},&\arg(z)\in\left(\frac{\pi}{2},\pi\right),\\
\begin{pmatrix}D_{-\nu}\left(ze^{-\frac{3\pi\mathrm{i}}{2}}\right)e^{-\frac{3\pi\mathrm{i}\nu}{2}}&\alpha D_{\nu-1}\left(ze^{-\pi\mathrm{i}}\right)e^{\pi\mathrm{i}\nu}\\
\mathrm{i}\beta D_{-\nu-1}\left(ze^{-\frac{3\pi\mathrm{i}}{2}}\right)e^{-\frac{3\pi\mathrm{i}\nu}{2}}&D_{\nu}\left(ze^{-\pi\mathrm{i}}\right)e^{\pi\mathrm{i}\nu}\end{pmatrix},&\arg(z)\in\left(\pi,\frac{3\pi}{2}\right),\\
\begin{pmatrix}D_{-\nu}\left(ze^{-\frac{3\pi\mathrm{i}}{2}}\right)e^{-\frac{3\pi\mathrm{i}\nu}{2}}&-\alpha D_{\nu-1}\left(ze^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}}\right)e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\nu}\\
\mathrm{i}\beta D_{-\nu-1}\left(ze^{-\frac{3\pi\mathrm{i}}{2}}\right)e^{-\frac{3\pi\mathrm{i}\nu}{2}}&D_{\nu}\left(ze^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}}\right)e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\nu}\end{pmatrix},&\arg(z)\in\left(\frac{3\pi}{2},\frac{7\pi}{4}\right),\end{array}\right.

where
\nu=\frac{1}{2}+\mathrm{i}\frac{\varkappa x}{\pi}
. In the above formula,
\arg(z)\in(-\frac{\pi}{4},\frac{7\pi}{4})
and multiplication of
z
by
e^{\mathrm{i}\phi}
represents the rotation of the argument of
z
by
\phi
. Hence, all the arguments of the parabolic cylinder functions above always belong to the right half-plane. Thus, assuming that
|z|\geq\max\{5,|\nu+1|\}
, (
6.3.2
) does indeed hold.
